The review's verdict was blunt: "that's the file's convention, not a style nit."
- **Root cause**: "stylistic nit" was applied by feel — the finding named formatting (a link, a date), and formatting reads as cosmetic. The test that was never run is whether the pattern is *load-bearing*: in this file, the link + verified-date pair is how a reader re-checks a version-gated platform claim that will go stale, and it is present on every sibling claim (3/3 `releases/tag/v2.1.*` citations). A pattern that holds across every sibling instance is a convention; deviating from it is a defect, and "the claim itself was verified" does not substitute — the citation exists so the *next* reader can verify without re-deriving.
- **Apply next time**: before dismissing any review/deep-review finding as stylistic, confirm the pattern mechanically instead of judging it: `grep -ncE '<the pattern>' <the file>` and count how many sibling instances carry it. Unanimous or near-unanimous adherence → it is a convention, fix it in the same commit. Mixed → genuinely a nit, dismissible with that count stated as the reason. Especially for citation/provenance patterns, ask what future work the element does — if it lets a later reader re-verify a claim that can go stale, it is functional, not decorative. Complements `[[L-2026-07-25-29]]`: that lesson says grep the repo for what it already *claims*; this one says grep it for how it already *cites*.


## L-2026-07-26-03 — A doc sentence that cites page A for its main fact but makes an adjacent claim owned by page B must be verified against page B; "org-wide enforcement" in Claude Code is always managed-settings.json, never `.claude/settings.json`

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#463 (branch `eva-impl/171-otel-cost-monitoring-debug-on`, cross-repo fix, round 1)
- **Signal**: Eva's REQUEST_CHANGES review flagged `debug-on/SKILL.md:140` — the new Cost-monitoring section's Prerequisites sentence ended "For org-wide enforcement, set the same names under the `env` block in `.claude/settings.json`; managed settings there take precedence over a user's own env vars." That conflates two different files: `.claude/settings.json` is *project* scope and is overridden by `.claude/settings.local.json` and CLI args; only the MDM-distributed managed settings file (`/etc/claude-code/managed-settings.json` on Linux/WSL, `/Library/Application Support/ClaudeCode/managed-settings.json` on macOS, `C:\Program Files\ClaudeCode\managed-settings.json` on Windows since v2.1.75) sits at the top of the precedence chain and "can't be overridden". The review's sharpest point: this is the SAME class of error the PR set out to fix — the PR existed to correct the filing issue's invented `metrics.endpoint`/`metrics.headers` settings.json keys, and shipped a fresh settings.json misstatement in the replacement text. The identical claim was also restated in the PR's own `CHANGELOG.md` entry, which the review did not name but which had to be corrected in the same round.
- **Root cause**: the sentence cited **one** docs page (`monitoring-usage`) and verified only the fact that page owns — the telemetry env-var names. The trailing enforcement/precedence clause is owned by a **different** page (`settings`), and was written from memory of the monitoring page's administrator section, whose "configure … for all users through the managed settings file" example is a bare JSON `env` block shown with **no file path attached**. An unpathed config example silently invites the reader (and the writer) to fill in the settings file they already had in mind — here `.claude/settings.json` — and the borrowed phrase "managed settings" then papered over the substitution, making the sentence read as verified when only its first half was. Verifying the primary claim rigorously created false confidence that the whole sentence had been checked.
- **Apply next time**: when a doc sentence spans two facts owned by two different upstream pages, fetch BOTH before writing — a single "(per <docs link>)" citation covers only the clause that page owns, and the *un*cited clause is the one most likely to be wrong. Two concrete triggers: (1) any config example in upstream docs shown WITHOUT an explicit file path must have its scope resolved (fetch the settings/precedence page) before you name a file in your own prose — never infer the path from the example; (2) in Claude Code, "org-wide enforcement" / "can't be overridden" means `managed-settings.json` distributed via MDM, full stop — `.claude/settings.json` is team-shared *defaults*, not enforcement, and the precedence chain is managed > CLI args > `.claude/settings.local.json` > `.claude/settings.json` > `~/.claude/settings.json`. Corollary, reinforcing [[L-2026-07-25-29]] and [[L-2026-07-25-13]]: when a review flags a factual claim in prose, `grep -rn` the PR's OWN diff for restatements of it (CHANGELOG entry, PR body, README mirror) and fix every copy in the same round — the reviewer names the instance they read, not the instance count. And when a PR's whole purpose is correcting someone else's factual error, hold the replacement text to a stricter standard than the surrounding prose: the error class you are fixing is the one you are primed to reproduce.


## L-2026-07-26-04 — An env-var recipe must be checked against the variable's BINDING TIME; a section that states "session-scoped" in one sentence and "set it then clear it per skill" in the next is self-refuting

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#463 (branch `eva-impl/171-otel-cost-monitoring-debug-on`, cross-repo fix)
- **Signal**: Eva's own REQUEST_CHANGES review flagged that `plugins/ievo/skills/debug-on/SKILL.md`'s new `## Cost monitoring` section contradicted itself inside a single bullet: its **Scope** line correctly stated that `OTEL_RESOURCE_ATTRIBUTES` labels apply to *all* token usage for the session (because OTel resource attributes are resolved once, at process start), then immediately instructed the reader to "set the var immediately before activating the skill and clear it afterward" for per-skill granularity. That recipe is not merely suboptimal — it is unexecutable: a running process's environment cannot be mutated from outside, so a reader following it verbatim gets *no* custom labels at all. The section's opening sentence ("Set it before activating an iEvo skill") carried the same wrong per-skill mental model, so the contradiction had two surfaces, not one. Confirmed against [Claude Code's monitoring docs](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/monitoring-usage): the documented usage is `export OTEL_RESOURCE_ATTRIBUTES=...` before launch, with no mid-session mutation path (the only mid-session refresh mechanism upstream documents is `otelHeadersHelper`, which covers OTLP *headers*, not resource attributes).
- **Root cause**: the implement run's verification effort was spent on the *declarative* facts — it correctly caught and corrected the source proposal's invented `metrics.endpoint`/`metrics.headers` settings.json keys, and correctly derived that resource attributes are session-scoped — but the *imperative* recipe was carried over from the source proposal's per-skill framing and never re-read against the constraint the same section had just established. Verifying "is this fact true?" is a different check from "is this instruction executable given the facts I just wrote two lines above?", and passing the first says nothing about the second. A `deep-review` pass over the diff returned 0 findings because the section is internally plausible sentence-by-sentence; the defect only appears when two sentences are held against each other.
- **Apply next time**: when documenting anything driven by an environment variable, explicitly write down the variable's **binding time** (read once at process start vs. re-read per use) before writing any instruction that uses it, then re-read every imperative verb in the section — "set", "export", "clear", "unset", "switch" — and confirm each is executable under that binding time. For a start-bound variable, the only correct granularity story is *one process per value* (a fresh session, or a one-shot `claude -p` run), never set/clear around an in-session action. And when a review flags a self-contradiction, fix **every** sentence in the section carrying the wrong mental model, not just the line the review cited by number — [[L-2026-07-24-05]]'s "re-audit every remaining instance in the same round" applies to prose framings exactly as it does to code fields.


## L-2026-07-26-05 — `gh pr create --repo X` does not infer `--head` from the local branch; eva-fix-pr's own Phase 3c consolidation snippet omits the flag and aborts after the push has already landed

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#463 (branch `eva-impl/171-otel-cost-monitoring-debug-on`, cross-repo fix)
- **Signal**: Phase 3c's consolidation block ran exactly as written — clone, renumber, commit, `git push -u origin "$BRANCH"` (succeeded, `* [new branch] evolution/consolidate-2026-07-26-1232-ievo-ai-skills-463`) — and then `gh pr create --repo ievo-ai/eva --base main --title ... --label silent --body ...` aborted with `you must first push the current branch to a remote, or use the --head flag`, despite the branch demonstrably existing on the remote with an upstream set. `gh pr list --repo ievo-ai/eva --head "$BRANCH"` returned `[]`, confirming no PR was created. Net effect if unnoticed: the lesson branch lands in `ievo-ai/eva` as an orphan branch with no PR, no review, no merge — a silent loss of the consolidation, indistinguishable in the run log from success because the push itself printed a clean result.
- **Root cause**: passing `--repo` puts `gh` in explicit-repo mode, where it deliberately stops deriving the head ref from the local checkout's current branch / upstream tracking config. The two flags are mutually exclusive in practice: `--repo` disables exactly the local-git inference that supplies `--head`. Phase 3c's snippet supplies `--repo` (needed, since the operation targets `ievo-ai/eva` from a temp clone) but never supplies `--head`, so it can only ever have worked by accident. Nothing in the snippet checks `gh pr create`'s exit status either, so the failure is non-fatal and easy to read past.
- **Apply next time**: whenever `gh pr create` is invoked with `--repo`, always pass `--head "$BRANCH"` explicitly — never rely on branch inference, which `--repo` turns off. After any scripted `gh pr create`, verify with `gh pr list --repo <repo> --head "$BRANCH" --json number` rather than trusting the absence of a visible error, since a successful `git push` immediately beforehand makes a failed PR-create look like a completed hand-off. This is a defect in eva's own `eva-fix-pr` prompt (Phase 3c) and in the identical block in eva-implement's Phase 6 if present — worth an operator-side fix to the prompt text, not just a per-run workaround, in the same spirit as [[L-2026-07-25-18]].

## L-2026-07-26-06 — A "needs no network / `:read-only` suffices" claim about an iEvo skill must be derived per invocation MODE from `commands/<name>.md`, not from the skill body

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#464 (branch `eva-impl/170-codex-permission-profiles-security-check`, cross-repo fix)
- **Signal**: Eva's own REQUEST_CHANGES review found that the new Codex permission-profile section asserted, in two places (`vuln-scan/SKILL.md:63` "This skill needs no network access at all ... the built-in `:read-only` profile ... is sufficient here", and `security-check/SKILL.md:452` "`:read-only` *is* the right profile for `/ievo:vuln-scan`, which reviews local source and needs neither a clone nor network"), a claim contradicted by `plugins/ievo/commands/vuln-scan.md:50`: the `--pr <N>` scope resolves its file list with `gh pr diff <N> --name-only`, and a Codex permission profile leaves `network.enabled = false` by default on *every* profile — `:read-only` included — so that one mode cannot determine what to scan at all. The same PR's CHANGELOG entry repeated the claim verbatim. Ironic failure class: the section being added is itself about getting a permission profile's *actual* footprint right.
- **Root cause**: the network footprint of `/ievo:vuln-scan` was derived from `skills/vuln-scan/SKILL.md`'s own body — Steps 1–4 are Read/Glob/Grep analysis, genuinely offline — while an iEvo slash-command skill's real tool surface is SPLIT across three files: `commands/<name>.md` (argument/scope parsing, where the `gh`/`git` shell-outs live), `agents/<name>.md` (dispatch), and `skills/<name>/SKILL.md` (the analysis prose). Verification was also asymmetric: for `security-check`, the skill I was writing the section *about*, I traced its Step 2 `mktemp -d` + `git clone` flow precisely and correctly concluded `:read-only` breaks it; for `vuln-scan`, the sibling mentioned only in a one-line contrast, I asserted the opposite from the skill body alone without opening its command file. A per-skill claim was made where the truth is per-mode: three of four scopes (`--diff`/`--module`/`--full`) are offline, one (`--pr`) is not.
- **Apply next time**: before writing ANY sandbox/permission claim about an iEvo skill ("needs no network", "needs no writes", "`:read-only` is sufficient", "no custom profile needed"), grep every file that skill actually executes — `plugins/ievo/commands/<name>.md`, `plugins/ievo/agents/<name>.md`, and `plugins/ievo/skills/<name>/**` — for `gh `, `curl`, `git clone`/`git fetch`, and `WebFetch`, then state the conclusion PER INVOCATION MODE (per CLI flag/scope), never once for the whole skill. A skill whose primary path is offline can still have one flag that shells out. Apply the same rigor to a skill mentioned only in a passing contrast clause as to the one the doc is about — a one-line aside is exactly where an unverified assertion hides, and it propagates: the same sentence had already been copied into the sibling SKILL.md and the CHANGELOG entry, so one unchecked claim needed three coordinated corrections.


## L-2026-07-26-07 — An env-var doc section must be derived from the upstream docs' configuration TABLE and quick-start block, not from the narrative section that introduces the variable

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#463 (branch `eva-impl/171-otel-cost-monitoring-debug-on`, cross-repo fix, round 3)
- **Signal**: two of Eva's three findings on the same `## Cost monitoring` section were *omissions*, both of a variable that lives outside the docs page's narrative prose. (1) The section never mentioned `OTEL_METRICS_INCLUDE_RESOURCE_ATTRIBUTES` (default `true`), which is what actually gates whether `OTEL_RESOURCE_ATTRIBUTES` keys land as labels on metric datapoints — an administrator setting it `false` leaves telemetry flowing while every dashboard the section teaches you to build silently returns nothing, i.e. the whole section stops working with no error anywhere. (2) The **Prerequisites** list named `OTEL_METRICS_EXPORTER` / `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT` / `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_HEADERS` but omitted `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_PROTOCOL`, which [Claude Code's monitoring docs](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/monitoring-usage) set in their own quick-start block (`export OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_PROTOCOL=grpc`; `http/json` and `http/protobuf` are the other accepted values). Neither omission is a factual error, so every sentence in the section read as verified — which is precisely why two prior fix rounds on the same section ([[L-2026-07-26-03]], [[L-2026-07-26-04]]) didn't surface them.
- **Root cause**: the section was written from the docs page's *narrative* subsection ("Multi-team organization support"), which introduces `OTEL_RESOURCE_ATTRIBUTES` and explains what it buys you. That narrative is the right source for the feature's *purpose* and the wrong source for its *contract*: the enable/disable switch lives in the page's configuration-details table, and the full set of transport variables lives in its quick-start code block. Verification effort went into checking that each sentence written was true, never into checking that the variable set was complete — a truth check over the sentences you wrote cannot detect a variable you never mentioned. Sharpens [[L-2026-07-26-03]] (which is about a clause owned by a different *page*) by one level: a clause can also be owned by a different *region of the same page*, and reading the page is not reading its tables.
- **Apply next time**: when documenting anything driven by environment variables, enumerate the upstream docs' machine-readable regions explicitly before writing prose — read the configuration/settings **table** row by row and the **quick-start block** line by line, and produce two lists: (a) every variable the quick start sets (these are the prerequisites, verbatim and complete), and (b) every variable whose description mentions gating, including, disabling, or filtering the thing you are documenting (these owe an explicit "off-switch" note with the default stated, because their failure mode is silent — data still flows, the feature just does nothing). Then diff both lists against your draft. Treat a `default: true` gate as load-bearing, not as a footnote: the whole section's value is conditional on it.

## L-2026-07-26-08 — A version floor cited only to a docs page that doesn't state it reads as unverified; the primary source for a version floor is the release note, and it belongs in the file

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#463 (branch `eva-impl/171-otel-cost-monitoring-debug-on`, cross-repo fix, round 3)
- **Signal**: Eva's second finding was "couldn't corroborate the v2.1.161 (2026-06-02) pin in the heading; nearby min-version markers in the docs are 2.1.193+. Confirm or drop it." The pin was in fact correct — `gh api repos/anthropics/claude-code/releases/tags/v2.1.161` (published 2026-06-02) leads with "`OTEL_RESOURCE_ATTRIBUTES` values are now included as labels on metric datapoints, so you can slice usage metrics by custom dimensions like team or repo" — so the correct disposition was *confirm*, not *drop*. But the reviewer could not reach that conclusion from the file: the section's one citation pointed at the monitoring docs page, which states the labeling behaviour as current with **no** `min-version` marker attached, while carrying visible `min-version: 2.1.193/2.1.202/2.1.212/2.1.214/2.1.217` markers on neighbouring variables. A reader checking the cited page therefore finds evidence that looks like it *contradicts* the pin.
- **Root cause**: the implement run verified the floor against the release notes and then cited only the docs page, because that page was the source for everything else in the section. A citation was present, so [[L-2026-07-26-02]]'s "link + date" convention looked satisfied — but the convention's actual job is letting the next reader re-verify the specific claim, and a link only does that job for claims the linked page makes. Version floors are systematically the claim a product docs page *doesn't* make: docs describe current behaviour, release notes record when it started. Nothing in the file recorded which source had been used, so a correct, verified fact was indistinguishable from an invented one — and "drop it" was the cheaper-looking of the reviewer's two offered dispositions, which would have shipped an accuracy regression.
- **Apply next time**: when a doc sentence asserts a "since vX.Y.Z" / "vX.Y.Z+" floor, cite the release notes for that exact tag inline (`gh api repos/<owner>/<repo>/releases/tags/vX.Y.Z` to confirm the tag exists and quote the bullet, plus its `published_at` as the date), and quote enough of the release-note text that the floor is self-evidently sourced. If the same section also cites a docs page for the mechanism, say in one clause which source owns the floor — especially when the docs page carries min-version markers on *other* settings but not on yours, since that asymmetry actively reads as counter-evidence. Corollary for the fixer role: when a review says "confirm or drop", resolve it against the primary source before choosing — "drop" is not the safe default, it is the option that silently discards a true fact, and the fix that survives is the one that makes the confirmation legible in the file rather than only in the PR conversation.


## L-2026-07-26-09 — A new doc section that restates a mechanism the same file already documents must sit next to that section and inherit its caveats, not re-derive the claim from frontmatter

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#464 (branch `eva-impl/170-codex-permission-profiles-security-check`, cross-repo fix)
- **Signal**: Eva's own REQUEST_CHANGES review flagged that the new § "Codex setup" opened with "`disallowed-tools` (frontmatter, above) blocks the agent's own `Write`/`Edit` tools and destructive `Bash` prefixes automatically on Claude Code" — asserting enforcement of the scoped `Bash(rm*)`-style entries, which this repo's `AGENTS.md` § Security model ("Sub-agent tool isolation") records as **unverified**, with an explicit "do not assume either behavior until a dedicated, isolated probe settles it". The identical caveat already existed 377 lines earlier in the SAME file — `security-check/SKILL.md:71`, the closing sentence of § "Sandbox hardening": "skill/agent `disallowed-tools`/`tools:` frontmatter only reliably enforces bare tool names, not scoped specifiers". The same review's nit — the section was appended after § Rules at the very end of the file rather than beside § Sandbox hardening, the section it extends — is not a separate finding: it is the same defect's cause.
- **Root cause**: I authored the new section as a standalone appendix and restated what `disallowed-tools` does from the frontmatter's own declaration list (`Write`, `Edit`, `Bash(rm*)`, …) — i.e. from what the file *declares*, not from what the repo has *verified* — instead of writing it as a continuation of § "Sandbox hardening", which had already done the work of qualifying that exact claim. Appending at EOF is what made the divergence invisible: the sentence contradicting me was ~380 lines away, outside the region I was editing, so "does this file already qualify this mechanism?" never came up. Physical distance within one file, not missing knowledge, is what let two sentences of the same document disagree about the same mechanism — and a security skill is the worst place for the optimistic version to win, since an overstated denial reads as protection the operator does not have.
- **Apply next time**: when adding a doc section whose opening sentence restates a mechanism the file already describes elsewhere, `grep -n '<mechanism-name>' <file>` FIRST (here `disallowed-tools`), read every hit, then place the new section IMMEDIATELY ADJACENT to the existing one and copy its qualifiers verbatim before adding anything new. Treat position as a correctness tool rather than a formatting preference: a section appended at EOF is the one most likely to drift from the file's own established claims, and reviewers correctly read "placed beside the section it extends" as the default. When the move happens, also fix any CHANGELOG/`AGENTS.md` sentence that described the old placement. Sibling to [[L-2026-07-25-29]] (grep the repo for what it already claims before asserting a platform behaviour), sharpened for the intra-file case: there the pre-existing line may be the stale one to reconcile against upstream, here the pre-existing line was CORRECT and mine was the overreach, so the fix is adjacency plus inheritance, not re-verification.


## L-2026-07-26-10 — Adding a new topical SECTION to a skill body is incomplete until the `description:` frontmatter carries that topic's triggers; skills activate by description match, not by body content

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#463 (cross-repo fix, round 4)
- **Signal**: Eva's review found that after four commits adding a `## Cost monitoring (Claude Code v2.1.161+)` section to `plugins/ievo/skills/debug-on/SKILL.md`, the file's `description:` still listed only debug-logging triggers (`"turn on debug"`, `"verbose mode"`, `"log everything"`, `"trace level"`, `"debug logging"`). A user asking about OTel cost attribution or token-spend dashboards would never activate the skill, so the section shipped unreachable by the exact query it was written to answer. The original implement run and three prior fix rounds all edited the body only.
- **Root cause**: treated "document X in skill Y" as a body-edit task, because the issue named the file and the section. But `AGENTS.md` § Skills format states the activation contract explicitly — "Skills are activated by description match (semantic), so descriptions must clearly state WHAT + WHEN to use" — which makes the frontmatter, not the body, the reachability surface. A body section on a topic absent from `description:` is dead weight: it costs context on every unrelated activation and surfaces on none of its own.
- **Apply next time**: when adding a section to a SKILL.md (or an agent file) that introduces a topic the file did not previously cover, treat the `description:` frontmatter as part of the same change — extend both its trigger-word list and its WHAT clause with the new topic, in the file's own existing style. Then measure the edited value against the cap the gate enforces (`description` ≤1024 via `validate_skills.mjs`) before running the gate, per `[[L-2026-07-25-01]]`. The test for "is this section reachable?" is whether a plausible user question about the new topic would match the description alone, with the body invisible.

## L-2026-07-26-11 — Answering a reviewer's "confirm or drop this claim" with the confirmation PROCESS written into the artifact ships the audit trail as product; the artifact takes the confirmed fact plus its primary-source link, the reasoning goes in the CHANGELOG/PR

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#463 (cross-repo fix, round 4)
- **Signal**: round 3's review asked "Couldn't corroborate the v2.1.161 (2026-06-02) pin ... Confirm or drop it." `[pr-fix-3]` confirmed it correctly — but wrote the confirmation *into the skill body*: a "(released 2026-06-02, verified 2026-07-26)" stamp, a verbatim quote of the release note, a verbatim quote of the docs page, and the sentence "so the floor can't be corroborated from the docs page alone". Round 4 then requested changes on exactly that prose ("skill bodies are agent context, not audit trail"), noting it duplicated the CHANGELOG's own **Scope** bullet — costing a whole extra fix round for a finding that had already been answered correctly.
- **Root cause**: a review challenge is addressed to the reviewer, so the fix was written in the reviewer's register — showing the work, pre-empting the next doubt. The artifact has a different audience: a skill body is context an agent pays for on every activation, and provenance about *how a fact was checked* changes nothing an agent does. Sourcing prose that is genuinely load-bearing (the primary-source link, the version floor, the date) is content; "verified <today>", quoted excerpts of the sources, and negative statements about what a source does *not* say are audit trail.
- **Apply next time**: when a review asks to confirm/corroborate a factual claim, split the answer by audience before writing it — the artifact gets the confirmed claim plus a link to the primary source (for a version floor, the release note per `[[L-2026-07-26-08]]`); the CHANGELOG entry and the PR/fix comment get the corroboration reasoning, the excerpts, and what could not be corroborated where. Never date-stamp a doc with "verified <today>": it ages into a liability and re-verifies nothing. If the confirming sentence would read as odd in a file a reader met with no knowledge of the review, it belongs in the review reply, not the file.
- Related: `[[L-2026-07-26-08]]` (the round-3 lesson this over-corrected), `[[L-2026-07-26-02]]` (citation-pattern conventions in the same file).


## L-2026-07-26-12 — Widening a skill's `description:` to a second intent is only half of reachability: the body's unconditional procedure then runs for that new audience, delivering side effects instead of the answer

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#465 (branch `eva-impl/171-otel-cost-monitoring-debug-on`, cross-repo fix, round 1)
- **Signal**: Eva's REQUEST_CHANGES review flagged that `debug-on/SKILL.md` now auto-activates on "cost monitoring" / `OTEL_RESOURCE_ATTRIBUTES` (the frontmatter fix `[[L-2026-07-26-10]]` prescribed), while `## Steps` remained unconditional — so a user who only asked how to label OTel metrics gets Step 2's `.ievo/debug.flag` written and confidential trace logging switched on, unrequested; and if that user has no `.ievo/` at all, Step 1's "init hasn't been run → Exit" fires first, so the `## Cost monitoring` section they activated the skill for is never reached. The section was simultaneously *too* reachable (side effects) and *un*reachable (early exit) for the exact audience the description change had just recruited.
- **Root cause**: `[[L-2026-07-26-10]]`'s remedy was framed as a frontmatter task — "extend `description:` so the new section is reachable" — and was applied as one. But a skill's `description:` is a router, not a table of contents: matching it dispatches the reader into the body's procedure from the top. So widening the description forks the skill's audience, and any procedure written for the original audience alone now executes for the new one too. Reachability was verified as "would a plausible cost-monitoring question match the description?" (yes) and never as "what actually happens to that user after the match?" — the body was treated as inert reference material a reader browses, when in fact `## Steps` is imperative and runs.
- **Apply next time**: when adding trigger words to a `description:` for a topic the skill's `## Steps` does not perform, add a routing branch as the FIRST step in the same change — name the intents, send the new one to the documentation section and stop it there explicitly (no flag file, no directory creation, no early-exit precondition check), and record the docs-only path in `## When to use` so both surfaces agree. The completeness test is a dry-walk, not a match test: trace a user of the new intent from activation through every numbered step in order and check each side effect and each early exit against what that user asked for. A precondition guard written for the original intent (`.ievo/` must exist) is precisely the thing that turns a newly-reachable section unreachable, so it must sit *after* the fork, never before it. Direct sequel to `[[L-2026-07-26-10]]`: those two changes are one change, and shipping the frontmatter half alone converts a dead section into an unrequested side effect.


## L-2026-07-26-13 — A "never return X" prohibition must be placed on the layer that can honour it; on a pass-through relay, a ban on relayed content is unenforceable by construction

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#466 (branch `eva-impl/243-deep-review-mvp-scope-boundary`, cross-repo fix, round 1)
- **Signal**: Eva's REQUEST_CHANGES finding 1 — `deep-review/SKILL.md:220`'s new out-of-scope bullet "Lint or type errors (pre-commit tooling already caught those)" sat on a layer that cannot obey it. The same file's Step 5 (L192, "Present it to the user as-is — do not editorialize, filter, or reorder findings") and its `## Rules` (L227, "**Present findings verbatim.** Do not filter, suppress, or editorialize the deep-reviewer's output") forbid the skill from removing anything the sub-agent returns. Findings are produced only by `agents/deep-reviewer.md`, which got the round's three *other* boundary bullets but not this one — and its Point 8 ("Changed return types that callers haven't adapted to") can legitimately emit type-shaped findings. Net effect: the prohibition was unenforceable where it was written and absent where it was enforceable.
- **Root cause**: the out-of-scope list was authored as one undifferentiated block, so each item inherited the placement of the item next to it. Four of the five bullets (merge timing, architecture refactors, sprint priority, unqualified-LGTM shape) constrain prose the *skill itself* authors, so the skill is the right home; the fifth constrains *findings*, which the skill only relays. The distinction is invisible when the list is read as a list — it only surfaces if each item is tested against the question "which layer emits this, and is that layer allowed to suppress it?" The two-file twin edit ([[L-2026-07-25-03]], [[L-2026-07-25-26]]) was performed — `deep-reviewer.md` was edited in the same commit — so twin-parity alone did not catch it: the twin got *some* of the bullets, and the parity habit checks that the second file was touched, not that each rule landed on the layer that can execute it.
- **Apply next time**: for every item in a new "never return / out of scope" list, name the component that *emits* that class of output before choosing the file — then grep the candidate file for its own filtering/pass-through contract (`verbatim`, `as-is`, `do not filter`, `present`, `relay`) and drop any item that contract makes unenforceable, re-homing it on the producer. When you re-home a rule onto a delegated producer, also walk the **fallback path where the layers collapse**: `deep-review/SKILL.md` Step 4's inline path has the skill execute the reviewer's steps itself, so moving the rule out would have left that path with no boundary at all — the fix had to state that the inline path runs under the agent's `## Rules`, or the rule would bind nowhere. Leave a one-line pointer (not a copy) at the old site so the absence reads as deliberate.
- Related: [[L-2026-07-25-26]] (twin dispatch path reached before the edited steps), [[L-2026-07-25-03]] (skill/agent twin parity), [[L-2026-07-25-12]] (fix the producer, not the consumer's tolerance).

## L-2026-07-26-14 — A new blanket "never do X" boundary must be diffed against the output the same file already mandates, or it bans the artifact's own purpose

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#466 (branch `eva-impl/243-deep-review-mvp-scope-boundary`, cross-repo fix, round 1)
- **Signal**: Eva's REQUEST_CHANGES finding 2 — the new boundary bullets banned "readiness" calls (`deep-reviewer.md:210` "deployment readiness"; `deep-review/SKILL.md:217` "Merge or deployment timing recommendations") while three untouched passages in the very same two files mandate exactly a readiness call: `deep-reviewer.md:174` requires the Summary to answer "is this diff ready to commit?", `SKILL.md:201` emits "Your diff looks ready to commit", and `SKILL.md:208-210` emit "commit at your discretion" / "safe to commit as-is". Read literally, the new rule forbade the output a pre-commit review exists to produce.
- **Root cause**: the boundary was imported wholesale from an external pattern ("draft + verify + explain, not merge + deploy + own production") and written as a fresh section at the end of the file, without re-reading the emission templates earlier in the same file. *Merge*/*deploy* readiness and *commit* readiness collapse into one word — "readiness" — in the source pattern's vocabulary, but they are opposite sides of this skill's boundary: everything before the commit is its remit, everything after is not. Nothing mechanical catches it (all instruction prose, no validator reads cross-section consistency), and the new section sits ~10 lines below the templates it contradicts, so a reader entering at the section header never sees them.
- **Apply next time**: before adding a "never do X" rule to an existing instruction file, grep that file for the literal output strings it already tells the agent to emit (quoted templates, fenced example blocks, `Summary`/report formats) and check each against the new ban — if any collide, the rule needs its in-scope carve-in stated *inside the bullet itself*, next to the ban, not left to inference ([[L-2026-07-25-27]]: a caveat placed below a rule is unreachable from every restatement of it). When the imported vocabulary is coarser than the artifact's actual boundary ("readiness" covering both commit and deploy), pick the precise terms in the rule's own wording ("merge, release, or deployment timing") rather than the source pattern's.
- Related: [[L-2026-07-25-27]] (fold the condition into the rule; grep its copies), [[L-2026-07-26-01]] (a true claim is not the same as a sentence that says what the source says).

## L-2026-07-26-15 — Recommending a sandbox/permission setting for a skill is only verified once you dry-walk that skill's OWN documented steps under it; a name that matches the intent is not evidence

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#464 (branch `eva-impl/170-codex-permission-profiles-security-check`, cross-repo fix, round 1)
- **Signal**: Eva's blocking finding — the new `security-check/SKILL.md` § "Codex setup" told Codex users to switch to the built-in `:read-only` permission profile and claimed "restricting filesystem access to read-only has the same practical effect for this skill". Four screens up, the *same file*'s Step 2 mandates `CHECKOUT_DIR=$(mktemp -d)` + `git clone --depth 1` + `git fetch`/`checkout` as the only sanctioned way to obtain a candidate, and the `security-auditor` twin writes `.ievo/hooks/security-red` on a RED verdict. `:read-only` blocks filesystem writes outright and leaves `network.enabled = false` (verified against the Codex Permissions docs), so following the new section would have made every scan fail to fetch its target — a security skill whose hardening advice disables the skill.
- **Root cause**: `:read-only` was selected on **name match with the intent** ("`disallowed-tools` blocks writes; this profile blocks writes → parity"), and the equivalence was asserted rather than executed. The two controls are shaped differently in a way the name hides: `disallowed-tools` denies the *agent's own* `Write`/`Edit` tools while leaving Bash `git` fully usable, whereas a Codex profile is a *process-level* sandbox that catches the skill's own required subprocesses too. So the Codex control is strictly broader, not equivalent — and "broader" on a security control reads as "safer", which is exactly why it survived self-review. The correct answer was a custom profile deriving from `:workspace` (writes in workspace roots + system temp, where `mktemp -d` lands) with an explicit network allowlist.
- **Apply next time**: before documenting any sandbox/permission/isolation setting as the right one for a skill, enumerate that skill's own required side effects from its own procedure — every file write, temp dir, clone, network host, hook write, and sub-agent write — and check each one against the setting; write the enumeration down in the section itself so the next reader can re-check it. Treat "same practical effect" / "parity" as a claim requiring that dry-walk, never as a summary of two features sharing a verb. Corollary: when two platforms' controls sit at different *layers* (per-tool denial vs. process sandbox), the stricter one is not automatically the safe default — over-restriction on a scanner's own fetch path is a functional break, not conservatism. Sharpens `[[L-2026-07-25-25]]` ("reuses X as-is" is an unverified claim) from callee steps to platform settings.

## L-2026-07-26-16 — An allowlist copied to a different enforcement layer must have its membership re-derived, because the layer decides what the list governs

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#464 (branch `eva-impl/170-codex-permission-profiles-security-check`, cross-repo fix, round 1)
- **Signal**: Eva's second blocking finding — the new § "Codex setup" offered a "stronger parity" custom Codex profile scoping network access to the same four domains the file's existing Claude Code guidance lists (`skills.sh`, `agentskills.io`, `raw.githubusercontent.com`, `api.github.com`), explicitly framed as "matching what the 'Network exfiltration' guidance above recommends". `github.com` is absent from that list — correctly, because on Claude Code the list is a `permissions.allow` block of `WebFetch(domain:...)` rules scoping **only the WebFetch tool**, and WebFetch never clones. Transplanted onto a Codex `[permissions.<name>.network.domains]` table, the identical list governs **every sandboxed process**, `git` and `gh` included — so the profile advertised as the stronger option silently blocked `git clone https://github.com/...`, the skill's Step 2 fetch.
- **Root cause**: the list was treated as a *set of domains this skill talks to* when it is really a *set of domains one specific tool is allowed to reach*. Its membership is a function of the enforcing layer's scope, not of the workload — so copying it across layers preserves the literal entries while silently widening what they have to cover. The omission was invisible on inspection precisely because the source list was correct and complete for its own layer.
- **Apply next time**: when porting an allow/deny list between platforms or enforcement layers, don't diff the two lists — re-derive the target list from scratch by asking what the *new* layer intercepts, then diff your re-derivation against the original and explain every difference in the doc. Concretely: name the layer's scope (this tool only / every subprocess / whole session), enumerate the workload's real destinations at that scope (here: `git clone` → `github.com`, `gh api` → `api.github.com`, WebFetch → the rest), and where the ported list legitimately differs, say so inline so the delta reads as deliberate rather than as a copy error. A framing phrase like "matching the guidance above" is a red flag on a ported list — it asserts equivalence across exactly the boundary that changes the list's meaning. Related: this batch's sibling entry on dry-walking a skill's own documented steps before recommending a sandbox/permission setting (same PR — the parity claim that the enumeration would also have caught).

## L-2026-07-26-17 — A `[[L-...]]` cross-ref to a sibling in the SAME capture batch is a forward reference to an ID the renumber pass is about to change; Phase 3c rewrites headers only, so the anchor silently retargets a stranger's lesson

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/eva#283 (branch `evolution/consolidate-2026-07-26-1233-ievo-ai-skills-464`, eva-repo fix, round 1)
- **Signal**: Eva's REQUEST_CHANGES review flagged that the batch's second entry closed with ``Related: `[[L-2026-07-26-03]]` (same PR — the parity claim…)`` while `L-2026-07-26-03` is the skills#463 managed-settings lesson — a different PR, not a parity claim. The intended target was the sibling entry directly above it, captured in the same skills#464 run. Rebuilding against current `main` made the damage plainer: `main` kept claiming fresh slots while this PR sat open, so the batch's two skills#464 entries — the sandbox-setting dry-walk lesson and the ported-allowlist lesson — were renumbered twice before settling, and every reallocation moved the stale body anchor onto yet another stranger.
- **Root cause**: the anchor was written at CAPTURE time, when the sibling's final `NN` did not exist yet — the writer predicted it (`-03`, the next free slot in the snapshot it had read); the allocator then gave the sibling a different slot — and moved it again on a later rebuild — while `-03` stayed pinned to the skills#463 managed-settings lesson. Phase 3c's renumber loop is the only code that holds the old→new `NN` map, and it deliberately rewrites the `## L-...` header line only ("entry bodies are untouched"), so it discards that map without ever applying it to the bodies. The invariant that protects prose from accidental edits is the same invariant that guarantees intra-batch anchors rot. It survives self-review because the anchor is syntactically valid and resolves to a real entry — it fails only on *meaning*, and only for a reader who opens the target.
- **Apply next time**: never write a sibling's ID at capture time — the `NN` is allocated later, so a predicted ID is guaranteed-stale input. Refer to a same-batch sibling by its title text (or a placeholder resolved after numbering), and if an anchor must be emitted, treat the renumber pass as owning it: apply the same old→new map to `[[L-<date>-<oldNN>]]` occurrences in the batch's bodies, not just to the header lines. When reviewing or fixing a consolidation PR, verify every `[[L-...]]` in the ADDED blocks resolves to the entry the surrounding prose *describes* — existence is not correctness, and an off-by-one anchor misroutes future recall in exactly the file that teaches verification rigor. Same failure shape as this batch's sibling entry on porting an allowlist across enforcement layers (the list stays literally intact while the layer under it changes what it means) applied to identifiers: an ID copied across an allocation boundary keeps its spelling and loses its referent.

## L-2026-07-26-18 — A lesson entry's NARRATIVE is renumber-fragile in exactly the way its cross-ref anchors are: prose citing IDs a rebuild is about to reassign goes stale, and the header-only renumber pass will never touch it

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/eva#283 (branch `evolution/consolidate-2026-07-26-1233-ievo-ai-skills-464`, eva-repo fix, round 1)
- **Signal**: Eva's REQUEST_CHANGES review flagged that the entry added the round before — the one teaching that an intra-batch `[[L-...]]` anchor is a forward reference the renumber pass is about to invalidate — had itself hard-coded the batch's then-current slots into its own Signal and Root cause ("this PR's two entries had to renumber to `-06`/`-07`", "the allocator later assigned `-04`"). Between that round and this one `main` advanced again, the batch relocated, and each of those numbers came to name an unrelated stranger: the slot the prose assigned to the sandbox/permission dry-walk lesson now holds a different lesson about deriving a `:read-only` claim per invocation mode. The entry that names the failure mode reproduced it two lines below the sentence naming it.
- **Root cause**: the previous round's fix was scoped to the syntactic form the review had named — `[[L-...]]` wikilinks — because that is the form a link checker can see and the form the finding pointed at. But what rots is not the bracket syntax; it is *any* reference to an allocator-assigned ID sitting in text the allocator does not rewrite. The renumber pass edits header lines only (deliberately, to protect prose from accidental edits), so a backticked or bare ID in a body is in exactly the same position as an anchor, minus the tooling that would notice. Writing the post-mortem in the same commit as the renumber is what made the numbers feel settled: they were the true observed values *at the instant of writing*, which reads as verified fact rather than as a snapshot of a value still under an open allocator.
- **Apply next time**: inside a lesson entry, never cite an ID the consolidation pass may still reassign — not as an anchor, not as prose. IDs of entries already merged on `main` are stable and safe to cite; IDs belonging to the batch you are writing, or to any still-open consolidation PR, are not — name those by their title text or by a one-clause description of what they teach, which survives any renumber. And when a review names one instance of a stale-ID class, fix the class across the whole added block rather than the instance: grep the added entries for every `L-YYYY-MM-DD-NN` occurrence in any form — `[[…]]`, backticked, bare, or a bare `-NN` suffix — and check each against what its own sentence claims it points at. Writing the post-mortem of a renumber while that renumber can still happen again is the exact situation where "I verified this number" is not enough.
- Related: this batch's sibling entry on intra-batch `[[L-...]]` anchors being forward references the renumber pass invalidates (same PR — the instance this generalizes from anchors to prose).

## L-2026-07-26-19 — A doc step the MODEL must execute has to be written in the shell form whose surface you verified with `--help`, not the interactive slash form a human uses

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#467 (branch `eva-impl/241-plugin-list-verification-init`, cross-repo fix)
- **Signal**: Eva's own REQUEST_CHANGES review found three defects in one 20-line paragraph added to `init/SKILL.md` — a "mechanical post-install verification" the init pipeline itself is supposed to run. (1) It instructed the model to run `/plugin list --enabled`, `/plugin list --disabled` and `/plugin enable ievo`: `/plugin` is Claude Code's *interactive menu* command, which a model can neither drive non-interactively nor read output back from, so the "mechanical" check silently no-ops — the exact opposite of the issue's goal (detect a *silent* activation failure). (2) `/plugin enable ievo` used the bare plugin name, which `version/SKILL.md` § Rules already forbids in this same repo ("never the bare `ievo` name, which fails even when the scope is otherwise correct" — the fully-qualified `ievo@ievo-skills` is required). (3) It gated the whole check on "Claude Code v2.1.163+", a version that is unsourced for this feature — in-repo, v2.1.163 is documented (`hooks-setup/SKILL.md:15`, Step 5.5.5) as Stop/SubagentStop `additionalContext` and nothing else. Running `claude plugin --help` / `claude plugin list --help` in the build runner (the `claude` binary is on `PATH`, v2.1.220) took one tool call and disproved the flags outright: `plugin list` accepts only `--available` and `--json`, there is no `--enabled`/`--disabled`, and `claude plugin list --json` returns `[{id, version, scope, enabled, installPath, ...}]` — a strictly better mechanical signal than the menu ever offered.
- **Root cause**: I wrote the command surface from the shape the *user-facing* text above it already used (`/plugin → Marketplaces → ievo-skills → Enable auto-update`, a menu path a human clicks) and extrapolated plausible `list --enabled` / `enable <name>` subcommands from it, without once running the CLI's own `--help` or grepping the repo for an existing rule about that command. The repo had already litigated all three points — `version/SKILL.md` § Rules mandates the external `claude plugin ...` shell form over the slash form *precisely because* the slash form can't be driven non-interactively, and mandates the fully-qualified id. Human-facing instructions and model-executed instructions look identical in Markdown, which is what let a menu path get pasted into a step the model must actually run; and a version gate is the one part of such a paragraph no local gate can ever falsify, so an invented number survives to review.
- **Apply next time**: when adding a step a SKILL will execute itself, first ask "who runs this — the human reader or the model?" A model-executed step must be the non-interactive shell form (`claude plugin list --json`), never a slash/menu form, and every flag in it must be confirmed by running `<cmd> --help` in the build environment before it is written down — the `claude` CLI is installed on the runner, so this is one Bash call, not research. Then `grep -rn "<command>" plugins/ --include=*.md` for an existing in-repo rule about that same command and follow it rather than re-deriving (here: fully-qualified `ievo@ievo-skills`). Never assert a minimum client version for a feature unless a primary source (release notes) or an in-repo citation supports *that* feature — prefer degrading on observed behaviour (non-zero exit / unparseable output → fall back) over a fabricated version gate, since a wrong gate silently disables the check on versions that support it. Reinforces [[L-2026-07-24-07]] (verify a claim against source before copying it into new prose) for the specific case where the "source" is a CLI's own `--help`.


## L-2026-07-26-20 — A field shape read back from a CLI is a version-local observation: cite where you saw it, and never let "no match" fall through into a mutating remediation step

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#467 (branch `eva-impl/241-plugin-list-verification-init`, cross-repo fix, round 2)
- **Signal**: Eva's REQUEST_CHANGES review on round 1's rewrite found that the new `init/SKILL.md` post-install check asserted `claude plugin list --json`'s entry shape (`id`/`enabled`/`scope`) with no citation — unlike every other platform claim in that same file, which carries one (`openai/codex#18115` for the Codex config limitation, `version/SKILL.md` § Rules for the shell-form/fully-qualified-id rules) — and, worse, routed the "command succeeded but no entry matched `^ievo(@.*)?$`" case straight into "re-run Step 9", the install step. Those two defects compose: a future Claude Code build that wraps the array (`{"plugins": [...]}`) or renames a field parses cleanly, matches nothing, and a perfectly healthy install gets pushed back through a mutating re-install. The paragraph already had a correct non-mutating degrade one bullet below (unparseable output → `/ievo:overlay-status` smoke test) — the fix was to route no-match there too, and only escalate to Step 9 if the smoke test also comes back negative.
- **Root cause**: round 1 fixed the *previous* review's finding (a model-executed step must use the non-interactive shell form whose flags were confirmed with `--help`, not a slash/menu form) by running `claude plugin list --json` in the build runner and transcribing the observed output — correct as far as it went. But an observation made by running a binary is inherently version-local, and I wrote it into the doc in the same voice as a stable, documented contract: no version, no date, no "as observed" hedge, and a match rule strict enough that any drift reads as absence. Having verified the shape empirically made it *feel* more certain than a cited fact, which is exactly backwards — an unwitnessed reader has no way to re-derive it. And because the branch was labelled "no matching entry", its consequence was written from the *intended* cause (install failed) rather than from the full set of states that can produce it (install failed **or** the parse key drifted).
- **Apply next time**: when a doc step keys off a field shape learned by running a command, (1) write the citation the file's own convention expects — here `(confirmed by running \`claude plugin list --json\` / \`--help\` on Claude Code v2.1.220, 2026-07-26)` — since "I ran it" is evidence only if the reader is told which build and when; and (2) enumerate a match branch by the STATES that reach it, not by the cause you had in mind: "no match" always means *failure OR drift*, so it must degrade to the same read-only fallback the unparseable branch already uses, never to a step that writes, installs, or enables. A mutating remediation may only hang off a positive, unambiguous signal (`"enabled"` present and literally `false`) — treat a missing or non-boolean field as drift, not as a disabled plugin. Extends the prior round's lesson on this same PR (a model-executed step must use the shell form whose surface was verified with `--help`): verifying a command's surface is necessary but not sufficient — what you learned from it still needs a provenance stamp and a drift-safe consumer.


## L-2026-07-26-21 — A verification step must be derived from the set of writes the run just made and from every failure mode its own motivation sentence names, not from the artifact the issue is titled after

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#467 (branch `eva-impl/241-plugin-list-verification-init`, cross-repo fix, round 3)
- **Signal**: Eva's REQUEST_CHANGES review found the post-install check verified exactly the one thing that cannot be false when the check runs — iEvo's own `^ievo(@.*)?$` entry — while the `<K>` third-party plugins Step 9b had just enabled by writing `.claude/settings.json` went unverified entirely. The skill is *running from* iEvo, so iEvo is necessarily loaded from some scope; worse, `claude plugin list --json` reports one entry per *install* scope, so a user-scope install alone answers `enabled: true` and says nothing about the project-scope `enabledPlugins` write that team sync actually depends on. The second finding was the same defect from the other end: the paragraph's own opening sentence named two failure modes (an `enabledPlugins` entry left `false`, **and** a path conflict under `.claude/skills/`), but the `enabled: true` branch terminated with "Nothing further needed" — a signal from the plugin registry, which cannot see `.claude/skills/` at all, was accepted as clearing a failure mode that lives only there.
- **Root cause**: I designed the check around the *subject of the issue* ("confirm iEvo activated") and then around the *tool that was easiest to read back* (`plugin list --json`), and never intersected either with the two lists that actually define the step: what the preceding steps wrote, and what the paragraph itself claims to detect. The tool's coverage boundary was never probed — running it against a project whose `settings.json` had just been written shows the entry is absent until the client loads it, so keying the `<K>` plugins' verification off that command alone would have reported every fresh install as unverifiable, which is plausibly why the earlier draft quietly checked only the one plugin already in the registry. And a self-referential check is invisible from the inside: the step reads as a real assertion right up until you ask whether it can execute at all in the world where its assertion is false.
- **Apply next time**: before writing a verification step, enumerate two lists explicitly — (1) every write the preceding steps made (here: iEvo's own self-registration entry *plus* each item in the install step's own queue), and (2) every failure mode the step's own motivation sentence names — then map each element to a check, and let no success branch terminate while an element is unmapped; if a branch's signal doesn't cover one, chain it into the read-only fallback that does rather than ending on it. Apply the falsifiability test to every check: if the step could not run in the world where its assertion is false, it verifies nothing — a plugin whose own skill is executing the check is exactly that case. And probe the verifying tool's coverage boundary empirically first, against the state the pipeline actually produces rather than against an already-healthy machine: a registry-backed CLI lists what is *installed*, so config written moments ago is absent by construction, and that absence has to be documented as expected rather than read as failure. Continues this PR's earlier lessons on writing the model-executed shell form whose surface was confirmed with `--help`, and on stamping a CLI-observed field shape with its build/date and degrading rather than remediating on drift — verifying a command's surface and its output shape is still not verifying that it observes the right *set of things*.


## L-2026-07-26-22 — A cited rule governs the whole sentence, including its "what if the field is missing" fallback; omitting a scope flag is not "no scope", it is the flag's default scope

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#467 (round 4)
- **Signal**: Eva's REQUEST_CHANGES flagged that `init/SKILL.md`'s new post-install remediation said to run `claude plugin enable ievo@ievo-skills -s <scope>` "rather than relying on `-s`'s auto-detect default (omit `-s` altogether if that entry carries no `scope`)" — and cited `version/SKILL.md` § Rules in the very next clause for the fully-qualified-id half. That same Rules bullet states the opposite of both inventions: `-s/--scope` **defaults to `user`** (not auto-detect), so "always detect and pass the actual scope, never omit it". The fallback therefore mutated the *user*-scope copy while the disabled project- or local-scope entry that triggered the branch stayed disabled — a silent write to a target the check never examined.
- **Root cause**: the implementer verified the clause it was citing (the `ievo@ievo-skills` id rule) and authored the neighbouring clause — the degrade path for a missing field — from imagination, without re-reading the same bullet that owned it. Fallback branches are where invented defaults hide: the cited source covers the happy path, so a "what if the field is absent?" sentence written next to it feels covered by the citation while actually being unsourced. Compounding it, "omit the flag" was treated as equivalent to "no scope applied", when a CLI flag with a documented default makes omission a *choice of a different target*, not an absence of one.
- **Apply next time**: when writing a fallback/degrade clause next to a cited rule, re-open the cited section and check it for that clause specifically — a citation earns only the sentence it was read for. For any CLI flag a doc tells the model to sometimes omit, look up the flag's documented default first (`--help`, or the repo's own rule bullet) and state it: if the default targets something other than what the branch detected, omitting the flag is a silent mutation, and the correct degrade is read-only (report + hand the choice to the user), never an unscoped mutating command. When reusing a sibling skill's detection procedure, state any deliberate divergence inline — here `version/SKILL.md` Step 5 filters `enabledPlugins` on `true` because it picks an *update* target, while a disabled-entry hunt must match the key at any value. Related: `[[L-2026-07-26-01]]` (a true claim is not the same as a sentence that says what the source says), `[[L-2026-07-26-03]]` (an adjacent claim owned by a different page must be verified against that page).

## L-2026-07-26-23 — A verification step whose negative reading triggers remediation needs an expected-empty guard: absent-by-design is not failure

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#467 (round 4)
- **Signal**: Eva's REQUEST_CHANGES flagged that the same Step 12 block ended three of its branches in the `/ievo:overlay-status` smoke test and read "no overlays listed" as evidence of an incomplete install (re-run Step 9, hunt for a `.claude/skills/` path conflict). But `init`'s own Step 9a vendors one overlay per item in `final_vendor_list`, and that list is legitimately empty when every candidate was declined by the user, filtered, or security-skipped — so a perfectly healthy run reports zero overlays and gets routed back into a mutating re-install of nothing.
- **Root cause**: the smoke test was lifted from the degrade branch and reused unconditionally, carrying an unexamined assumption from its original context (that something *was* vendored). The implementer checked what the probe would say when the install was broken, and never asked what it says when the run's input set was legitimately empty — the failure branch was designed against the artifact's presence, not against the range of run shapes the same skill permits.
- **Apply next time**: for any verification probe whose *negative* result triggers a remediation (re-run a step, re-install, escalate), first identify the producer of the thing being probed and ask whether that producer can legitimately produce zero — then gate the failure reading on the producer's own count (here: the summary's `<N>` + `<M>` > 0), and say plainly that an empty result is the correct outcome in that case. Same shape as `[[L-2026-07-13-01]]` inverted: there, "unscanned" must not collapse into "absent"; here, "absent by design" must not collapse into "failed". Deriving the check from the set of writes the run actually made is the same discipline `[[L-2026-07-26-21]]` names.


## L-2026-07-26-24 — Fixing one field of a "read it off the matched entry" rule leaves the sibling literals in the same command hardcoded

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#467 (round 5, `eva-impl/241-plugin-list-verification-init`)
- **Signal**: Eva's own REQUEST_CHANGES review flagged that `init/SKILL.md`'s post-install verification branch matches **any** plugin entry whose `id` satisfies the deliberately broad `^ievo(@.*)?$`, then remediates a disabled entry with a hardcoded `claude plugin enable ievo@ievo-skills -s <scope>` — so a disabled `ievo@<other-marketplace>` copy gets an enable for a *different* plugin and stays disabled. The immediately preceding fix round (`[pr-fix-4]`) had already reasoned through exactly this shape for the **`-s <scope>`** argument of that same command ("never unscoped — pass that entry's own `scope`"), and stopped there; the `id` argument, one token to the left in the same command, kept its literal.
- **Root cause**: I treated "the entry the check matched must supply the remediation's parameters" as a fix for the one argument the previous review named (`scope`), not as an invariant over the whole remediation command. Once a lookup is intentionally broader than one literal, EVERY value in the action taken on the match has to come from the match — but a review that names a single argument makes it easy to patch that argument and re-read the rest of the line as already-correct, because the literal is the one that was there when the check only ever matched one thing.
- **Apply next time**: when a fix makes any argument of a remediation command derive from a matched/looked-up record, immediately re-read the *entire* command and ask of each remaining literal "could the match have been something else here?" — the broad match pattern (`^ievo(@.*)?$`) is the checklist: every field it lets vary must be read off the entry, not hardcoded. Also carry the qualification constraint through: an id read off the record can be less qualified than the hardcoded one it replaces (a bare `ievo`), so state the degrade path rather than silently inheriting the assumption the literal used to guarantee.

## L-2026-07-26-25 — A file already over its documented size budget is not license to add to it; the repo's own `references/` split is the pattern

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#467 (round 5, `eva-impl/241-plugin-list-verification-init`)
- **Signal**: Eva's own review flagged that `init/SKILL.md` reached 1110 lines against AGENTS.md's "Body should be ≤500 lines; split detail into `references/` if more is needed", and that the ~100-line block this PR added was self-contained enough to belong in `references/post-install-verification.md` behind a Step 12 pointer — the exact shape `install-protocol.md`, `log-format.md`, `reference-tables.md`, and `security-report-flow.md` already use in that same skill. The original implementation had *noticed* the rule and written the overrun into the CHANGELOG ("already over the 500-line recommendation pre-existing this change ... not addressed here") instead of following the split pattern for the new content.
- **Root cause**: I read the ≤500 rule as a property of the file's current state (already violated → my ~100 lines change nothing) rather than as a rule about where NEW self-contained detail goes. Documenting the overrun in the changelog felt like due diligence and actively substituted for the fix — an acknowledged violation reads as a considered decision, so nothing prompts a second look at the four sibling files that show the intended pattern.
- **Apply next time**: before appending a block of more than ~20 lines to any skill/doc body, check the repo's own size rule AND whether the directory already has a `references/`-style split; if the body is at or over budget, the new block goes into a reference file with a short pointer paragraph in the body, regardless of how far over budget the file already was. Never let a changelog/PR-body acknowledgement of a known convention violation stand in for complying with it on the lines you are adding — a pre-existing overrun is inherited, but every line added on top is newly mine.


## L-2026-07-26-26 — "Companion to the existing X" is a claim about the REPO's contents, not about the issue that proposes X; an open issue titled "document X" is positive evidence X is undocumented
- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#469
- **Signal**: Eva's own review flagged CHANGELOG v0.68.0 L13 for self-contradiction — it called the new note a "Companion to the **existing** v0.141.0 MCP-timeout note tracked separately in #227" while the very next bullet (L14) correctly stated #227 is still open and unmerged. `grep -rn "0\.141" --include="*.md" .` returns only `hooks-setup/SKILL.md` + `references/codex-hooks.md`, both about the `PostToolUse` code-mode blocking fix — an unrelated surface. No MCP-timeout note has ever existed in the repo.
- **Root cause**: #227's merge state was checked (correctly — the issue's own two-branch instruction hinged on it, and L14 got it right), and that check was then silently reused as if it licensed a claim about the issue's *content* being present in the tree. Two different questions — "is #227 merged?" and "does the repo document the v0.141.0 MCP timeout?" — collapsed into one lookup. The near-miss made it worse: the repo *does* mention rust-v0.141.0, so a shallow grep hit reads as confirmation until you check what the hit is actually about (`PostToolUse` code-mode, not MCP timeouts). `cut-release.yml` copies the CHANGELOG section verbatim into the public Release body, so the false claim would have shipped to users.
- **Apply next time**: any word asserting presence — "existing", "already documented", "the current note", "as documented in" — is a claim about the working tree and must be discharged by grepping for the *artifact*, then reading the hit to confirm it is about the same subject, never by reading the tracking issue's title or state. An OPEN issue whose title begins "Propose: document …" is affirmative evidence the thing is NOT documented; cite it as "the proposed note in #N", never "the existing note tracked in #N". Cheapest self-check before committing a CHANGELOG entry: read the bullets as a block and look for two adjacent sentences making opposite claims about the same reference.

## L-2026-07-26-27 — A prose count and the enumeration in the same sentence are two assertions that must be reconciled against each other, not just each against the source
- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#469
- **Signal**: Eva's review flagged CHANGELOG v0.68.0 L15 — "three PRs landed against this same field after the issue was filed (Auto Mode `classifyAllShell` and dual-gate install consent notes)". The count was right (`git log -L '/^compatibility:/,+1:<file>'` since the issue's `createdAt` yields #333, #337, #454); the parenthetical named only the two that were recognised by name, silently dropping #454's Codex `$CODEX_CLI` client-gating clause.
- **Root cause**: the count came from one pass (enumerate the commits touching that line since the issue date) and the parenthetical came from a different, later pass (recall which notes were in the field). Nothing ever compared the two, so the number and the list were each defensible in isolation while contradicting each other on the page — the same failure shape as the "existing note" bullet three lines above it, in the same entry.
- **Apply next time**: when a sentence states a cardinal ("three PRs", "four files", "both callers") and then enumerates, treat the enumeration as the source of truth and derive the number by counting it — or name every item. If an item is genuinely not worth naming, write "three PRs … (including A and B)" so the sentence stops claiming the list is exhaustive. When the items are commits, `git log -L '/<anchor>/,+1:<path>'` scoped to the issue's `createdAt` gives the full set, and `gh api repos/<owner>/<repo>/commits/<sha>/pulls` maps each one to the PR number worth citing.


## L-2026-07-26-28 — A how-to sentence whose own trailing caveat negates one of its steps is not "hedged", it is broken: delete the step, and diff the sibling file that states the same fact correctly

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#470 (branch `eva-impl/235-cursor-compat-sweep`, cross-repo fix, round 1)
- **Signal**: Eva's REQUEST_CHANGES first finding — `README.md:81` read "find and install iEvo via Cursor's unified Customize page — **search \"ievo\"** or add this repo by URL. No `.cursor-plugin/` manifest exists yet, so iEvo **isn't yet listed in Cursor's own marketplace search**." The first clause tells the user to perform the exact action the second clause says will find nothing. The *same commit* wrote the fact correctly in `AGENTS.md:9` ("it's added by repo URL **rather than** surfaced in Cursor's own marketplace search") — no search step, because that sentence was built as one claim instead of step-plus-hedge. The `CHANGELOG.md` bullet describing the README note copied the broken variant verbatim. Every gate was green: no validator, and no `/ievo:deep-review` pass (which reported 0 findings on this diff), has any notion of "this instruction contradicts its own footnote".
- **Root cause**: the how-to clause was drafted from the issue's proposed wording ("search 'ievo' or add by URL") and the no-manifest caveat was **appended after** it as a hedge, rather than folded back into the instruction. Appending is the failure mode: each clause is independently true, so a fact-level check passes on both, and the contradiction lives only in the *relation* between them — which is exactly what a sentence-at-a-time review never looks at. The tell was structural and available without any Cursor knowledge: a user-facing procedure that ends in "…but that won't work yet". The second tell was that the same author had already produced the correct one-claim form in the sibling file 70 lines away, so the repo carried both variants at commit time and the wrong one was the one a *user* reads. Instance of [[L-2026-07-25-27]]'s shape ("adding an exception below a rule you just wrote leaves the rule stale") applied to end-user instructions rather than agent rules, where the cost is a step that visibly fails on first use.
- **Apply next time**: after writing any user-facing procedure, read it back **as the user executing it top to bottom** and check whether a later clause invalidates an earlier step. If it does, the step is deleted, not softened — a caveat may narrow *when* a step applies, never assert that it fails. Then, before committing, `grep -rn "<the fact>" --include=*.md .` and diff every prose home of it against the others (README = user, AGENTS.md = contributor, CHANGELOG = the record): when the same commit states one fact in two places, the variants must be paraphrases of one claim, and if they disagree the *correct* one is usually the one written without an "or"/"but" seam. Fix all copies in the same round — the reviewer names the instance they read, not the instance count (reinforces [[L-2026-07-26-03]]).

## L-2026-07-26-29 — A superlative isolation claim must be derived from what the isolated environment still HOLDS, not from the one path it blocks; "can't reach your local workspace" is not "can't do harm"

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#470 (branch `eva-impl/235-cursor-compat-sweep`, cross-repo fix, round 1)
- **Signal**: Eva's second finding — the new Cursor section in `security-check/SKILL.md` described Cursor v3.7's `/in-cloud` as "process-level isolation **stronger than any tool/permission restriction**, since even a successful prompt injection in that session can't reach your local workspace", and recommended it for HIGH-RISK candidates. Cursor's own [cloud-agent security page](https://cursor.com/docs/cloud-agent/security-network) (verified 2026-07-26) says the cloud VM is granted **read-write** repo privileges ("Grant read-write privileges to our GitHub app for repos you want to edit. We use this to clone the repo and make changes") and that "the agent has internet access by default", with outbound-domain egress controls as an opt-in. So an injection inside that VM can still push commits to the repo and exfiltrate its contents — it is isolated *from the operator's machine*, not contained. Worse, the claim shipped in the skill whose entire job is judging adversarial third-party content, where an overstated safe-harbour directly changes a reader's threat model.
- **Root cause**: the isolation strength was assessed from the **blocked path** the changelog advertises (separate VM ⇒ no local filesystem access) and then generalised into a superlative, instead of from the **remaining capability surface** of the isolated environment. A changelog entry announcing a boundary describes what it stops; it is silent on what still sits inside, so deriving "stronger than any tool/permission restriction" from it is reading a capability claim out of a feature announcement. The superlative also made the sentence unfalsifiable-sounding while being trivially false in the source's own terms — network egress controls *are* a permission restriction, and the docs offer them precisely because the default is open.
- **Apply next time**: for any security claim of the form "X can't reach Y", write the complement clause before deciding the claim is publishable — enumerate the credentials, tokens, repo grants, and network egress the environment *still* holds, from the vendor's security/permissions page (not its changelog or feature announcement), and state it in the same sentence. Ban bare superlatives ("strongest", "stronger than any …") in security prose: name the specific boundary instead ("isolation from your local machine, not containment"), because a superlative is a claim about every alternative, which was never enumerated. When the doc is a security skill, add the operational consequence the caveat implies (here: restrict egress, and treat any branch the session pushed as unreviewed) — a caveat the reader can't act on is decoration.

## L-2026-07-26-30 — A true upstream release-note quote is not a true MECHANISM for your own pipeline: check that the subsystem it names has actors in your code path, and read the whole release for the bullet that actually matches the symptom

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#469 (branch `eva-impl/232-codex-v0142-init-compat`, cross-repo fix, round 2)
- **Signal**: Eva's review called the shipped claim "uncorroborated" — `init/SKILL.md`'s `compatibility` field named Codex rust-v0.142.0+ for "exec-server/MCP disconnect recovery — pre-142 may silently drop Step 6/8 sessions on network blips", but "neither repo-indexer nor security-auditor holds an MCP session (they only scan `.mcp.json`)", and `AGENTS.md:267` — the repo's own in-depth note on that exact dispatch path — attributes Step 6/8 silent failure to unsurfaceable `spawn_agent` approval prompts, not to MCP. The quoted release-note bullet itself was real ("Exec-server processes and stdio MCP sessions now survive transient disconnects…", verified via `gh api repos/openai/codex/releases/tags/rust-v0.142.0`); what was false was that it described anything these two sub-agents do.
- **Root cause**: the issue's **Evidence** section supplied a verbatim upstream quote, and the quote's truth was silently promoted into the truth of its *application* to iEvo's pipeline. The bridging step — does the subsystem the quote names (a stdio MCP session) actually exist in the actors the claim binds it to (`repo-indexer`, `security-auditor`)? — was never taken; a one-line `grep -i mcp plugins/ievo/agents/{repo-indexer,security-auditor}.md` shows only `.mcp.json` as *scan input*, never a held session. The near-miss made it durable: the word "MCP" is genuinely present in both agents, so a shallow grep reads as confirmation until you check which side of the boundary each hit sits on.
