The same release notes did contain a bullet that exactly explains the reported symptom — "Parent agents now receive terminal subagent errors instead of seeing failed work as an empty successful completion" ([openai/codex#28375](https://github.com/openai/codex/pull/28375), merged 2026-06-16) — so the version floor was correct and only its mechanism was wrong; dropping the note (the cheaper-looking disposition) would have discarded a true, useful floor.
- **Apply next time**: when an issue hands you an upstream quote as the mechanism behind a symptom in *our* code path, treat the quote and the binding as two separate claims. Discharge the binding by naming the concrete actors and grepping them for the subsystem (`grep -in "<subsystem>" <each actor file>`), then reading each hit to see whether the actor *holds* that subsystem or merely *reads about* it — "scans `.mcp.json`" and "holds an MCP session" are opposite sides of the same keyword. Then read the **entire** release's notes, not just the bullet the issue quoted: when the issue's bullet fails the binding check, a sibling bullet in the same release often explains the symptom correctly, which turns "drop the claim" into "re-source it" (per `[[L-2026-07-26-08]]`, drop is never the safe default). Finally, a version floor asserted about a pipeline the repo already documents in depth owes a reconciliation in that existing note — carrying the release-notes link the sibling notes already use as convention (`[[L-2026-07-26-02]]`) — not a second, quieter statement of a different mechanism in frontmatter. Extends `[[L-2026-07-25-29]]` (grep the repo for what it already claims) from *contradiction* to *mechanism attribution*: two files can be non-contradictory sentence-by-sentence and still assign one symptom to two unrelated causes.



## L-2026-07-26-31 — The mechanism-binding check must be re-run on the fix round too: a review that says "scope it or drop it" is naming a binding failure, and the reviewer's own suggested scope is itself a claim to verify

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#471 (branch `eva-impl/227-codex-mcp-timeout-compat`, cross-repo fix, round 1)
- **Signal**: Eva's REQUEST_CHANGES review blocked `AGENTS.md`'s new "Codex MCP tool timeout" bullet — it pinned `security-auditor` reliability on Codex's MCP tool-call timeout, but iEvo ships no MCP server and `/ievo:init` Step 8 dispatches the auditor via `spawn_agent` (documented in this same file's "Codex sub-agent delegation" bullet), so those reads are not MCP calls. This is the SAME binding failure `[[L-2026-07-26-30]]` captured one PR earlier (ievo-ai/skills#469), on the SAME repo, about the SAME two agents and the SAME subsystem word "MCP" — the prior lesson's own "grep each actor for the subsystem, then check whether it *holds* it or merely *reads about* it" rule would have caught this bullet before it ever shipped. The review offered two dispositions ("scope it (Codex-as-MCP-server hosts) or drop the causal claim"); verifying the first found it needed correcting too — `openai/codex#28234` moves `DEFAULT_TOOL_TIMEOUT` in `codex-rs/codex-mcp/src/rmcp_client.rs`, the MCP **client**, so in a `codex mcp-server` topology the floor binds the *driving/outer* Codex, not the inner one running iEvo, and a non-Codex MCP client's own timeout governs instead.
- **Root cause**: the implement round asserted a mechanism without discharging the binding, and the fix round nearly repeated it in the other direction — a reviewer's parenthetical suggested scope reads as an approved answer, but it is a claim of the same kind as the one being fixed, sourced from the same partial reading. Two compounding habits: (1) an evolution lesson captured about repo X's *previous* PR is not consulted when the next PR on repo X trips the identical pattern, because the fixer treats the review body as the whole input; (2) the cheap disposition ("drop it") and the reviewer-suggested disposition ("scope it as stated") both look like closure, so neither gets independently re-derived from source.
- **Apply next time**: on a fix round whose finding is "this claim's mechanism doesn't apply here", do two things before writing a word. First, grep `EVA_EVOLUTION_STORE` for the subsystem noun in the finding (here, `grep -i mcp`) — a prior entry describing the same binding failure on the same repo is the strongest available prior, and it names the discharge procedure. Second, treat any scope the reviewer proposes in parentheses as a hypothesis, not a verdict: resolve the upstream change to the exact file and symbol it touches (`gh api repos/<o>/<r>/pulls/<n>/files` → which crate/module, client or server side) and re-derive which side of the boundary each actor sits on. Both dispositions the reviewer offers can be wrong in detail even when the finding is right; the finding is authoritative about the *defect*, not about the *fix*. Extends `[[L-2026-07-26-30]]` from the implement round to the fix round — the binding check is not a one-time gate before shipping, it is owed again by whoever rewrites the claim.


## L-2026-07-26-32 — A fix round that rewrites a *claim* must sweep every derived restatement of it (CHANGELOG summary, PR body, measured numbers), and re-measure any length-capped line it edits

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#471 (branch `eva-impl/227-codex-mcp-timeout-compat`, cross-repo fix, round 2)
- **Signal**: round 1 correctly re-scoped the shipped claim — Codex's MCP `DEFAULT_TOOL_TIMEOUT` is a Codex-as-MCP-*client* control, so it does NOT bound `security-auditor`'s `spawn_agent` file reads — and rewrote `AGENTS.md` + `security-check/SKILL.md` + the CHANGELOG bullets accordingly. Eva's next review still returned CHANGES_REQUESTED on two leftovers: (1) the CHANGELOG `## v0.68.1` **summary line** had grown to 278 chars because round 1 folded the new scoping in as an inline em-dash aside, blowing past AGENTS.md § Changelog's documented ~240-char hard cap (`cut-release.yml` copies that section verbatim into the public Release body, skills#306); (2) the **PR body's** first bullet still asserted the exact claim the shipped `AGENTS.md` text now explicitly negates ("`security-auditor`'s deep multi-file reads … can exceed the … timeout window, silently cutting a scan off"). A third, unflagged instance was present too: the PR body's Test plan still cited the `compatibility` field at 495/500 chars, stale since round 1's own edit pushed it to 499/500.
- **Root cause**: I treated "the claim is wrong" as a *source-file* defect and fixed it only where the claim is authored (`AGENTS.md`, `SKILL.md`, CHANGELOG bullets). But a claim in this repo is restated in at least four derived places — the CHANGELOG summary line, the CHANGELOG detail bullets, the PR body's Summary bullets, and the PR body's Test-plan measurements — and none of those are covered by any gate: `pre-commit`, the coverage gate and the version-bump gate all pass on a PR whose body and summary line contradict the shipped text, because no validator reads a PR body or enforces the ~240-char summary cap. Compounding it, the correction was *appended* as an aside to a summary line that was already near the cap, without re-measuring; and round 1 changed a measured value (the 500-char-capped `compatibility` field) without re-checking every place that number was quoted.
- **Apply next time**: when a fix round changes what a PR *asserts* (not just how code behaves), finish by sweeping every restatement of that assertion before pushing — `gh pr view --json body` and re-read the Summary bullets and Test plan against the shipped text, and re-read the CHANGELOG summary line as well as its bullets. Whenever an edit touches a length-capped line or field, re-measure it right then (`awk '/^## vX.Y.Z$/{f=1;next} f&&NF{printf "%s",$0;exit}' CHANGELOG.md | wc -m` for the ~240-char summary cap; a `[...str].length` count for the 500-char `compatibility` field) rather than assuming the earlier measurement still holds — and prefer moving new scoping into a bullet over appending an em-dash aside to a capped summary line, since the bullets are uncapped and the summary is not. Treat any number quoted in a PR body as a fact with an expiry date: a later round that re-edits the measured artefact invalidates it. Related: [[L-2026-07-23-02]] (a green gate is not evidence a mandated, unvalidated step was done).


## L-2026-07-26-33 — A doc section whose thesis is a CONTRAST owes a citation per axis of the contrast, not just for the identity of the thing being contrasted; a settings row's "the `--foo` flag overrides this for one session" clause IS the persistence evidence

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#472 (branch `eva-impl/218-cc-config-verbose-debug-alt`, cross-repo fix, round 1)
- **Signal**: Eva's own REQUEST_CHANGES review flagged that the new "Native session verbosity" sections in `debug-on/SKILL.md` and `debug-off/SKILL.md` both described Claude Code's `/config verbose=true` as session-only — debug-on: "nothing is written to disk, and nothing persists once the session ends"; debug-off: "for the current session", "nothing persisted" — when `/config key=value` writes a settings key and therefore persists. Verified against the settings reference's raw source: `/config` *is* the Settings interface (v2.1.181 only added a way to set one row without opening it); the `verbose` row reads "**Default**: `false`. Show full tool output instead of truncated summaries. Appears in `/config` as **Verbose output**. The `--verbose` flag overrides this for one session"; the same page states `/config` rows write persistently for other keys ("The **Artifacts** row in `/config` writes this key", "…which writes this key to user settings"). The section's entire thesis was the ephemeral-vs-persisted contrast against `/ievo:debug-on`, so the one claim it existed to make was the one that was wrong — and the heading itself ("Native **session** verbosity") carried the same error.
- **Root cause**: the implement run scoped its doc verification to the *identity* of the thing being documented — is the key called `verbose`? does `/config verbose=true` exist? is v2.1.181 the right floor? — all three correctly confirmed and correctly praised by the review. The *persistence* axis was never looked up at all; it was inherited from the framing chosen at design time ("a lighter, ephemeral native alternative") and from the connotation of the word "verbose" as a display toggle. Worse, the disproving evidence had already been read and transcribed: the shipped CHANGELOG bullet quotes the very same table row's "only a `--verbose` CLI flag override" — read as evidence about *environment variables*, and never turned back on the adjacent bullet's session-scope claim sitting three lines away. A documented per-session override clause exists precisely *because* the underlying setting persists; it is positive evidence of persistence, and it was in hand.
- **Apply next time**: when a doc section's thesis is a CONTRAST ("X is narrower / lighter / more ephemeral than Y"), enumerate the axes the contrast rests on — scope, **persistence/lifetime**, artifact produced, platform reach — and require a distinct primary-source citation for *each axis*, not just for X's identity. Confirming that X exists and is spelled correctly says nothing about the axis the sentence is actually asserting. Two mechanical checks: (1) for any settings key, treat an override clause in its own row ("the `--foo` flag overrides this for one session", "requires a restart", "takes precedence") as the primary evidence about that key's lifetime — a per-session override implies a persisted baseline, never the reverse; (2) before shipping, re-read every fact already quoted in your own CHANGELOG/PR body against every *other* claim in the same change — evidence gathered to answer question A frequently settles question B, and here it did. Extends [[L-2026-07-26-01]] (a truth check on the nouns does not license the sentence) to the axis level, and is the docs-claim sibling of [[L-2026-07-26-04]] (write down the binding time *before* writing any sentence that depends on it). Fix scope note: the wrong mental model lived in the heading too — renaming "Native session verbosity" → "Native verbose output" was part of the fix, per [[L-2026-07-26-04]]'s "fix every sentence in the section carrying the wrong model, not just the line the review cited".


## L-2026-07-26-34 — The CHANGELOG "Version" bullet is a template, and copying it carries the PREVIOUS release's bump-type and its version-race parenthetical into a PR where both are false

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#473 (branch `eva-impl/218-config-verbose-alternative`, cross-repo fix, round 1)
- **Signal**: Eva's REQUEST_CHANGES review praised the docs content, the key (`verbose`), and the completeness of the bump across all four coupled surfaces, then blocked on the CHANGELOG's single **Version** bullet for two independent inherited errors. (1) It ended "(0.68.1 → 0.69.0 — 0.67.0 already claimed by a concurrent open PR)" — a clause copied verbatim from the `## v0.68.0` entry three sections below, where it was true and load-bearing (that build jumped 0.66.0 → 0.68.0 *around* a claimed slot). At 0.68.1 → next there is no slot to skip and 0.67.0 is *behind* main, so the clause is unparseable in the public release body `cut-release.yml` copies it into. (2) It justified the minor bump as "per AGENTS.md rules (`feat:` → minor)" on a PR titled `docs:` — AGENTS.md's bump table lists only `fix:` → patch / `feat:` → minor / `feat!:` → minor, so `docs:` has no mapping and the cited rule does not apply; the immediately preceding docs-only entry v0.68.1 took a patch. Both the *number* (0.69.0) and its *justification* were inherited from a neighbouring entry rather than derived from this PR.
- **Root cause**: AGENTS.md § "Version bumping" mandates the race procedure — "query main's CURRENT version at push time via `gh api`, check open PRs for claimed versions, pick next free slot" — and the implementer evidently did look: the one open sibling PR (#467) does claim 0.67.0. What was never done is the comparison that makes a claim *matter*: #467 is stale, built before v0.68.1 merged, so its claimed 0.67.0 sits **below** main's current 0.68.1 and cannot collide with any slot above it. A version claimed at or below main's current version is evidence the PR is behind, not evidence a slot is taken. Having found a superficially matching fact (the string "0.67.0" really was claimed by a really open PR), the previous entry's ready-made sentence about exactly that string was adopted whole — and the bump-type clause riding in the same sentence came along unexamined, because the copy source was a `feat:` entry and nothing in the copying step re-reads the current PR's own prefix. The general shape: the "Version" bullet reads as boilerplate, so it gets *transcribed* rather than *derived*, and boilerplate is precisely where a stale premise survives every gate — `check-version-bump.mjs` only verifies that plugin.json increased and the coupled files agree, so 0.69.0 with a nonsense rationale is as green as 0.68.2 with a correct one.
- **Apply next time**: treat the CHANGELOG **Version** bullet as three separately-derived facts, never as a copied line. (a) *Bump type* — read THIS PR's own conventional-commit prefix and map it through AGENTS.md's table; if the prefix is absent from that table (`docs:`, `chore:`, `refactor:`), say so explicitly and justify by the nearest same-shaped precedent entry rather than quoting a rule that does not cover you — and prefer keeping an honest `docs:` title with a patch over retitling to `feat:` to make a chosen number legal. (b) *Target number* — `gh api repos/<o>/<r>/contents/plugins/ievo/.claude-plugin/plugin.json --jq .content | base64 -d` on `main`, then increment. (c) *Race parenthetical* — include it ONLY after listing open PRs, reading each one's claimed `plugin.json` version, and confirming at least one claim is **strictly greater than main's current version**; a claim ≤ main is a stale branch, not a collision, and the clause must then be omitted entirely rather than reworded. Mechanically: after writing the bullet, diff it against the entry above it (`git diff` the CHANGELOG hunk against `git show origin/main:CHANGELOG.md`) and require every carried-over clause to be re-justified from this PR's own facts. This is [[L-2026-07-26-32]]'s sweep rule pointed at the *source* rather than the derived copies — there, one true claim failed to propagate into its restatements; here, a neighbouring entry's true clause propagated *into* a PR it was never true of. Same failure of provenance, opposite direction. Corollary from that lesson still applies on the fix round: the PR body restates the version too (`0.68.1 → 0.69.0`) and must be re-edited in the same round, since no gate reads it.


## L-2026-07-26-35 — Documenting a control's limitation is only half the job: check whether the same doc offers the mechanism that closes it

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#476 (branch `eva-impl/208-tool-param-value-positive-enforcement`, cross-repo fix)
- **Signal**: Eva's own REQUEST_CHANGES review accepted every factual claim in the PR (both doc quotes, the `#match-by-input-parameter` anchor, the ask/deny-only scope) but flagged that the new AGENTS.md bullet noted `Agent(model:haiku)` "matches the literal alias `haiku`, not a dated model ID" and then simply **stopped**. The permissions docs the bullet already cites, in the very same "Parameter matching follows these rules" list, state the value "supports `*` as a wildcard that matches any sequence of characters" — so a one-entry companion rule (`Agent(model:claude-haiku-*)`) closes exactly the gap the sentence had just described. Worse, the uncovered case is the pinned-`claude-haiku-...`-ID form this same file's `CLAUDE_CODE_SUBAGENT_MODEL` gotcha bullet explicitly warns about, so the doc named a hazard in one section and shipped a mitigation that silently misses it in another.
- **Root cause**: when documenting a newly-verified platform control, I treated "state the limitation accurately" as the finish line. Accuracy was never in question — completeness was. I read the docs' parameter-matching rule list to extract the *constraints* on the feature (literal match before normalization, omitted params never match) and stopped reading it as a source of *capabilities*; the wildcard bullet sits two lines from the literal-match bullet I did quote. A caveat that describes a bypass without offering the available fix reads as thoroughness but ships a weaker guard than the cited source supports.
- **Apply next time**: whenever a doc change lands on "…but this does NOT cover X", immediately re-scan the SAME source page for a mechanism that covers X before committing — a limitation sentence is a prompt to keep reading, not a conclusion. Then cross-check X against the surrounding sections of the file being edited: if a sibling bullet already flags X as a live hazard (here, the pinned-ID `CLAUDE_CODE_SUBAGENT_MODEL` warning), leaving X uncovered is an internal contradiction the reviewer will catch, not a documented edge case.

## L-2026-07-26-36 — In a cross-repo fix run, `git fetch origin main` under a wrong-repo `origin` corrupts the `[pr-fix-N]` budget count and can fake budget exhaustion

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#476 (cross-repo fix)
- **Signal**: `origin` again resolved to `https://.../ievo-ai/eva.git` while the working tree, `FETCH_HEAD`, and every `refs/remotes/origin/*` branch were genuinely `ievo-ai/skills` — the exact condition [[L-2026-07-15-01]] and [[L-2026-07-22-02]] already recorded (reading the store in Phase 1 this time meant I recognized it instead of re-deriving it). The NEW damage was upstream of the push: Phase 2's prescribed `git fetch origin "$BASE"` pulled **eva's** `main` into `refs/remotes/origin/main` (`+ 2a569c9...9e15aeb main -> origin/main (forced update)`), so the budget probe `git log origin/main..HEAD | grep -c '\[pr-fix-[0-9]+\]'` walked two unrelated histories and returned **USED=69** against `FIX_BUDGET=5`. Taken at face value that is an immediate Phase 2 stop: label `eva-fix-budget-exhausted` + `needs-operator` and hand a perfectly fixable one-commit PR (true USED=0) to the operator. The tell was the commit list — 69 markers on a branch whose own `git log` showed a single commit ahead of the PR base.
- **Root cause**: prior entries framed the wrong-`origin` hazard purely as a *push-destination* problem, so the mitigation they prescribe (verify/redirect before the first `git push`) is positioned too late in the run. Every earlier read-side command that names `origin` — `git fetch origin <base>`, and any `origin/<base>..HEAD` range derived from it — is equally poisoned, and the failure is silent: `git fetch` succeeds, the range resolves, and the number that comes out is plausible enough to act on.
- **Apply next time**: in any run where `$TARGET_REPO != ievo-ai/eva`, verify `git remote get-url origin` resolves to `$TARGET_REPO` as the FIRST git command of the run — before Phase 2's `git fetch`, not just before the push. If it doesn't match, treat `origin` as untrusted for reads too: resolve the base from the API (`gh api repos/$TARGET_REPO/branches/<base> --jq .commit.sha`) and repair the tracking ref locally (`git update-ref refs/remotes/origin/<base> <sha>`) before computing anything from it. Sanity-check the budget count against `git log --oneline origin/<base>..HEAD` — a marker count wildly out of scale with the branch's own commit count means the base ref is wrong, not that the budget is spent. Push by explicit URL (`git push https://x-access-token:${GH_TOKEN}@github.com/$TARGET_REPO.git HEAD:<branch>`) rather than rewriting the remote, which keeps the prompt's "do not change git remotes" rule intact while still landing on the right repo.


## L-2026-07-26-37 — Replacing outcome prose with a "run `/skill`" pointer over-claims unless the skill's own SKILL.md is read first, and must keep the outcome clause the prose stated

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#477 (branch `eva-impl/206-handoff-active-plan-state`, cross-repo fix, round 1)
- **Signal**: Eva's REQUEST_CHANGES review accepted the whole build except one rewritten sentence. `handoff/SKILL.md`'s `## Active overlays` block used to close with "Review these overlays at the start of the next session to inherit project conventions."; the build replaced it with "Run `/ievo:overlay-status` at the start of the next session to load these." The review's objection: `overlay-status` is read-only *enumeration* — it lists each overlay with a one-line summary and mtime — it never reads overlay rules into the session, so "to load these" tells the receiving agent the conventions are active when they are not, and the swap silently dropped the old line's stated outcome ("inherit project conventions"). A second, minor finding: the newly-added `## Active plan state` template section maps to no Step 2 gathering sub-step, unlike `## Context` ← 2b and `## References` ← 2c.
- **Root cause**: converting vague prose into a runnable command reads as a pure legibility upgrade — the issue even asked for "an explicit `/ievo:overlay-status` pointer" — so the edit was made from the skill's *name* and topic rather than from its behaviour. `overlay-status/SKILL.md` was never opened; its frontmatter says "Read-only — never modifies, deletes, or rewrites overlay content" and its `allowed-tools` are `Read`/`Glob`/`Bash(stat|uname|date)`, i.e. it renders a listing and stops. Because the old and new sentences occupy the same slot, the rewrite was scored on *form* (imperative + slash-command = better) and never on whether the promised effect survived; the outcome clause was collateral, deleted rather than kept alongside the new action. The minor finding has the same shape one level up: the PR body and CHANGELOG justified adding no Step 2 sub-step by asserting that sibling template sections "have no dedicated Step 2 gathering sub-step" — an assertion about the file made from memory of its shape, which the file itself contradicts in two places the reviewer found immediately. Nothing in the gate can catch either: `pre-commit`, the validators, the coverage gate, and `check-version-bump.mjs` all pass on prose that is confidently wrong about a sibling skill and about the file it lives in.
- **Apply next time**: (a) before writing `run /x` (or any tool/command pointer) as the action that *achieves* an outcome, open `x`'s own SKILL.md — read the frontmatter description and `allowed-tools`, and match its **effect**, not its topic; a skill that only surfaces information cannot be the whole instruction, so keep the consuming step in the sentence ("run `/x` to see what's active, **then read the listed overlays** to inherit conventions"). (b) A rewrite that adds an action must *preserve* the outcome clause the old prose stated — diff old-vs-new sentence and name what each half asserts; a deleted outcome is a regression even when the replacement is more concrete. (c) Never justify an omission with a structural claim about the file ("the other sections do it this way") without grepping for the mapping first — a structural claim is trivially verifiable and therefore the reviewer's cheapest catch; either verify it or make the change the claim was arguing against. Same provenance failure as [[L-2026-07-26-34]] — a justification adopted from a remembered neighbouring fact instead of derived from the artifact in front of you.


## L-2026-07-26-38 — A CHANGELOG sentence that localizes a fact to a named section of a file needs the same grep-the-source check as an external citation

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#478 (branch `eva-impl/201-hooks-setup-v2.1.176-if-compat`, cross-repo fix)
- **Signal**: Eva's own REQUEST_CHANGES review judged the actual product change correct (the new `v2.1.176+` `compatibility` clause in `hooks-setup/SKILL.md`, its `## References` citation, chronological ordering, 499/500 chars, version lockstep) but blocked on the CHANGELOG prose: the "Gap closed (#201)" bullet asserted the skill "already uses an `if:` path pattern in its own **Step 5 example** (`if: "Write(.ievo/hooks/evolution-captured)"`)". It doesn't — `grep -n 'if:' plugins/ievo/skills/hooks-setup/SKILL.md` returns exactly two hits (the frontmatter line and SKILL.md:53), and :53 documents `evo`'s **per-skill `hooks:` frontmatter** in the "Two complementary hook tiers" section, a different hook tier entirely. Step 5's template (SKILL.md:119) writes `"matcher": "Write(.ievo/hooks/<event>)"` — and SKILL.md:57's own "Known gap" note says in so many words that this is invalid and that the path pattern *belongs* in `if:`, i.e. the file being described explicitly contradicted the sentence written about it. The same false attribution had been restated a second time in the "Scope" bullet ("duplicating the existing Step 5 example"), so one unverified locator propagated to two places in the same entry.
- **Root cause**: the implement run applied real verify-against-source rigor to the **external** fact (it re-fetched the v2.1.176 release notes rather than trusting the issue's citation — and correctly so) but treated the **internal** cross-reference as background knowledge and wrote the locator ("its own Step 5 example") from a recollection of the file formed while reading it for a different purpose. Nothing in the local gate can catch this class of error: `validate_skills.mjs`, the coverage gate, and pre-commit all check the SKILL.md and the version lockstep, and none of them read CHANGELOG prose for factual claims about the repo's own files. The asymmetry is the whole bug — an external citation *feels* like it needs checking, an "I just read that file" internal one does not, and the internal one is the cheaper of the two to verify (one grep).
- **Apply next time**: whenever a CHANGELOG entry, PR body, or release note contains a **locator** — "Step N's example", "the `X` section", "line NN", "its own template" — pointing at a file in the same repo, grep that file for the literal token before writing the sentence, and re-grep after any scope-down reword (the locator often survives a rewrite whose surrounding claim changed). Treat `CHANGELOG.md` prose as shipped product content, not commentary: in `ievo-ai/skills` it is published verbatim as the GitHub Release body, so it carries the SKILL.md accuracy bar while sitting entirely outside the local gate's reach. A useful tell for this specific repo: if the sentence describes something as already-working in a skill's *own* Step, check that skill's "Known gap"/follow-up notes first — a documented known gap is precisely a place where the file's current behavior differs from the behavior a summarizer would assume.


## L-2026-07-26-39 — A prohibition whose scope is bound to an optional detector's output only holds where the detector fired; derive the scope from the harm, not from the mechanism that found it

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#480 (branch `eva-impl/200-vuln-scan-sensitivity-classification`, cross-repo fix, round 1)
- **Signal**: Eva's own REQUEST_CHANGES review's Main finding. The build added a classification step (Glob a module's paths against 11 canonical sensitive-path patterns → `sensitive_files`) and, in `agents/vuln-scanner.md` + `skills/vuln-scan/SKILL.md`, a rule that opened "**Never quote sensitive file content.** If dispatch's `sensitive_files` is non-empty, you may still read those files in full … but never quote their raw values". The conditional made the guarantee exactly as wide as the detector: a hardcoded credential in `config.js` or `terraform.tfvars` matches none of the 11 patterns, and the step's own text declares itself "optional-but-recommended" (an empty list on Glob failure, and the orchestrator may skip it entirely) — so in both cases the scanner reads "`sensitive_files` is empty ⇒ the rule does not bind" and the secret stays quotable verbatim in `title`/`exploit_chain.*`/`recommendation`/`notes`. The feature exists to stop exactly that leak, so the shipped rule failed open in the precise cases it was written for. Nothing in the gate could see it: `pre-commit` (9 hooks), the 146 validator tests, the coverage gate and `check-version-bump.mjs` all passed on both the original and the fixed wording — the defect is entirely in the semantics of a natural-language rule an LLM will later obey literally.
- **Root cause**: the issue proposed a mechanism (classify sensitive files → pass the list to the scanner → tell it not to quote *those* files), and the rule's scope was written as a restatement of that mechanism's data flow instead of being derived from the harm the feature prevents (any raw secret reaching the report, from any file). Once a detector exists, its output reads like the natural subject of the prohibition — but a pattern list is a *heuristic* about where secrets usually sit, and an optional step's output is not a fact about the world; binding a "never" to either converts a guarantee into a best-effort hint without any sentence ever saying so. The `notes`-field gap the pre-PR `/ievo:deep-review` had already caught was the same defect one level down (enumerate the channels vs. cover them all), and fixing the enumeration made the rule look complete while the conditional framing above it stayed unexamined.
- **Apply next time**: when a change ships BOTH a detector and a prohibition, write the prohibition unconditionally over the whole class of harm first, then introduce the detector's output strictly as a prioritization hint ("where to look hardest"), never as the rule's antecedent. Mechanical check before pushing any rule of the form "if `<detector-output>` is non-empty, never X": ask what the consumer does when that output is empty, absent, or the producing step was skipped — if the answer is "X becomes allowed", the scope is wrong. Say so explicitly in the artifact too (an empty list is *not* evidence the module is clean) and mirror the unconditional wording into every place the rule is restated, including the verbatim dispatch-prompt string the orchestrator sends, since that string — not the agent's own file — is what a dispatched subagent actually reads.

## L-2026-07-26-40 — "Matches the name the issue proposed" is not a defense of a name that asserts an ordering the document contradicts

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#480 (branch `eva-impl/200-vuln-scan-sensitivity-classification`, cross-repo fix, round 1)
- **Signal**: the pre-PR `/ievo:deep-review` raised the "Phase 0.5" numbering as its third finding; the implement run dismissed it in the PR body as "a naming nitpick (intentional, matches the issue's proposed name — no change made)". Eva's PR review then raised the same point as an actionable Minor, and it was correct on the merits: the section's own first working sentence is "For each module **from Phase 1d**…", i.e. it consumes Phase 1's output and is placed between `## Phase 1` and `## Phase 2` in the file — so `0.5` names a position before Phase 1 that the step cannot occupy. The fix (heading, the emitted log line, and the `sensitive_files` input-field description in both consuming files) was mechanical. A second minor from the same review had the same shape: both new rules ended "…the Excerpt containment backtick-wrapping rule **below**", while that note is *above* them in both files (`vuln-scanner.md`:125 vs :172, `SKILL.md`:192 vs :223) — and the sibling bullet three lines away already used the repo's stable form, `see § 2 "Output structured JSON"'s "Excerpt containment" note`.
- **Root cause**: two different provenance failures for the same class of defect. The number was inherited from the issue's proposal and treated as a naming *preference* (defensible by citing its source) rather than as a factual claim about execution order that the document itself falsifies in its next sentence. The "below" came from writing a cross-reference from the drafting position — the new rule was composed while looking at the Excerpt containment note, which had been read *earlier*, so "below" encoded the authoring sequence rather than the file's layout; positional words ("above", "below", "the next section") are the only cross-reference form that silently rots when a block is inserted anywhere else, which is why the sibling bullet's `§`-name form exists.
- **Apply next time**: treat an ordinal or positional token in a heading (`Phase 0.5`, `Step 3`, `pre-flight`) as an assertion to verify against the section's own data dependencies and its literal placement in the file, not as a label — and never let "the issue called it that" settle it, since an issue proposes a name before the insertion point is known. For cross-references, never write "above"/"below"/"the following section": grep the file for how sibling references are already phrased and copy that form (`§ <heading>`'s `<sub-heading>` note) — it survives relocation, and matching the neighbours is free. Corollary on reviews: when a self-review finding is going to be dismissed, record the dismissal as a checkable claim ("intentional because X"), then check X — here X was "matches the issue's proposed name", which is provenance, not correctness, and a one-line reading of the section would have overturned it before the PR was opened.


## L-2026-07-26-41 — Prose that "supersedes" a documented constraint obligates a grep of the SAME file for every claim derived from that constraint; and a PR-body sentence describing the diff's contents is a claim to verify against the diff, not a plan

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#482 (branch `eva-impl/194-sub-agent-nesting-pipeline-docs`, cross-repo fix, round 1)
- **Signal**: Eva's first finding — the new AGENTS.md § Pipeline paragraph opens "supersedes the older 'no agent nesting' documented constraint", but `AGENTS.md:251` (§ Security model, 55 lines *above* in the same file) still asserted "`evolution.md` is a Task-dispatched sub-agent, so it can't itself dispatch a nested `security-auditor` … (both withheld from Task-dispatched sub-agents by Claude Code)". The new text says nesting is on by default at depth 3; the old text says the platform withholds it. Worse, the implement run's own PR body asserted the staleness was "flagged inline in the new AGENTS.md text so it's visible to the next reader/agent" — the review's verdict was flat: "it isn't". No such flag was ever written. The correct repair was also cheaper than the PR body implied: the *behavioural* claim survives (the agent still can't dispatch), but the *reason* changed from platform-imposed to self-imposed — `evolution.md`'s own `tools:` list grants Read/Write/Edit/Glob/Grep/Bash and no `Agent`/`Task`.
- **Root cause**: two compounding errors, both about the gap between an intention and an artifact. (1) The word "supersedes" was treated as describing the *upstream platform's* history rather than as an obligation against the *document being edited*: superseding a constraint invalidates every sentence in the file whose reasoning rests on it, and those sentences are found by grep, not by recall — here `grep -n 'nest' AGENTS.md` would have surfaced L251 immediately. The implement run instead applied a "keep this PR scoped to the single file the issue names" rule, which was satisfied — the contradiction was *inside* that same file. (2) The PR body's "flagged inline" sentence was written from the plan, not from the diff; it describes work that was considered and then dropped, and it reads to a reviewer as a statement of fact about shipped content. Sharpens `[[L-2026-07-26-03]]`'s corollary (grep the PR's own diff for restatements of a flagged claim): the reverse direction matters too — grep the diff for whatever the PR *body* claims the diff contains.
- **Apply next time**: whenever a doc change contains "supersedes" / "replaces" / "no longer true" / "corrects the older X", immediately `grep -n` the edited file for the superseded fact's keywords and fix or annotate every hit in the SAME commit — an in-file contradiction is never out of scope, however narrowly the issue is scoped, because "one file" scoping makes the whole file in scope by construction. When the stale sentence's *conclusion* still holds, do not delete it: re-derive its reason from the artifact (here, the agent's own `tools:` frontmatter) and say which half changed, so the correction is legible instead of looking like a reversal. And before submitting, re-read every sentence of the PR body that describes what the diff contains against `git diff` — any "flagged inline", "noted in the text", "documented below" claim must resolve to real added lines or be deleted from the body.

## L-2026-07-26-42 — A version HISTORY is N independent version claims, and the file's link + verified-date citation convention is owed per row, not once per section

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#482 (branch `eva-impl/194-sub-agent-nesting-pipeline-docs`, cross-repo fix, round 1)
- **Signal**: Eva's second finding — "The 5→1→3 depth history cites nothing; every neighbouring version claim links the release tag + '(verified <date>)'. Add both." The new AGENTS.md bullets asserted three separate upstream behaviours (v2.1.172–v2.1.216 fixed at 5 layers; v2.1.217–v2.1.218 default 1; v2.1.219+ default 3 with `CLAUDE_CODE_MAX_SUBAGENT_SPAWN_DEPTH`) with zero links and zero dates, in a file where every sibling version claim carries both. All three facts were true — `gh api repos/anthropics/claude-code/releases/tags/{v2.1.172,v2.1.217,v2.1.219}` confirms each verbatim — so this was again a provenance defect, not an accuracy one. One sub-claim did *not* survive contact with the source: "not configurable" for the 5-layer era is stated nowhere in the v2.1.172 note (the env var is first named in v2.1.217), so citing the tag forced the wording down to what the release actually supports.
- **Root cause**: the section had one gesture at provenance — "verify current behavior at `code.claude.com/docs/en/sub-agents`" — and that single pointer created the feeling that the citation duty was discharged for the whole block. But a docs page states *current* behaviour only; it cannot source a superseded default, which is precisely what rows 1 and 2 of a version history assert. This is `[[L-2026-07-26-02]]` (deviating from a file's citation convention is a defect, not a nit) and `[[L-2026-07-26-08]]` (a version floor's primary source is the release note, not the docs page) meeting in one construct: a history is the shape where the docs page is *guaranteed* insufficient for all but the last row, and it is also the shape most likely to be written as a single tidy bullet list that looks like one claim.
- **Apply next time**: treat every row of a version-history list as its own citable assertion — `gh api repos/<owner>/<repo>/releases/tags/vX.Y.Z --jq '.published_at, .body'` per row, link the tag inline, quote the release-note bullet, and stamp `(verified <today>)` on each. For a row asserting a *superseded* default, the release note is the only valid source; the docs page can be cited alongside, but only for the current row. Then check the trailing row's "current" word by listing the releases published since it (`gh api 'repos/<owner>/<repo>/releases?per_page=10'`) and grepping their bodies for the same keyword — "current" is a claim with an expiry, so name the newest release you checked against. Finally, re-read each row's adjectives against the quoted text: any qualifier the release note does not state ("not configurable", "unconditionally", "always") must be dropped or re-sourced.


## L-2026-07-26-43 — A doc section's framing sentences drifted from the carefully-cited detail block they bracket

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#482 (branch `eva-impl/194-sub-agent-nesting-pipeline-docs`, cross-repo fix)
- **Signal**: Eva's own REQUEST_CHANGES review verified every citation in the new AGENTS.md § Pipeline "Sub-agent nesting" section verbatim against the Claude Code release pages (v2.1.172/217/219/220 — quotes and dates all correct) and still requested changes, on two internal contradictions, BOTH of them in the prose framing the cited bullet list rather than in the list itself. (1) The section header said "min-version 2.1.172" — the release that first *shipped* nesting — while its own bullets documented that the default flipped 5 → 1 → 3 and that nesting is **off** by default on v2.1.217–218, so 2.1.172 is not a usable floor for relying on the behavior; 2.1.219+ is. (2) The closing worked example called the main-session → `vuln-scanner` dispatch "one layer of nesting" while the paragraph four lines above called that identical shape "one layer deep, no further nesting" — a depth-1 dispatch is not nesting, so the section used the same word for two different things in adjacent paragraphs.
- **Root cause**: the section was assembled detail-block-first — release notes fetched, quoted, and date-verified with care — and the surrounding header/summary/example sentences were written from the mental model I held *before* doing that research (nesting = "sub-agents can dispatch", shipped in 2.1.172). Once the bullets were correct I re-checked them against the sources but never re-read the framing prose against the finished bullets. Citation-checking and internal-consistency-checking are orthogonal passes: verifying every quote against its upstream source cannot surface a contradiction between two sentences that both live in my own diff. That is also why the product gates were green — nothing mechanical reads doc prose for self-consistency.
- **Apply next time**: after finishing any doc section that pairs a researched, cited detail block (version history, compatibility matrix, release-note quotes) with framing prose (a bolded header, a "min-version"/"requires" claim, a summary sentence, a worked example), do a dedicated second read of ONLY the framing sentences with the finished block in view, asking two specific questions: (a) does every version floor name the release where the behavior I am telling the reader to rely on actually holds, rather than the release that introduced the feature — these differ whenever a default flipped mid-history; and (b) is every load-bearing term used with exactly one meaning across the whole section (grep the section for the term and read every hit together). Treat "all citations verified" as evidence about the sources only — never as evidence the section is internally consistent.

## L-2026-07-26-44 — Flagging an out-of-scope stale-doc defect inline is not filing it; the follow-up needs a real tracker

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#482 (branch `eva-impl/194-sub-agent-nesting-pipeline-docs`, cross-repo fix)
- **Signal**: while documenting the Claude Code nesting-depth history, the implement run correctly noticed that `plugins/ievo/agents/evolution.md:34,275` still describe nested spawning as "off by default" (verified 2026-07-23, i.e. pre-v2.1.219) and correctly judged that fixing it was outside the PR's scope — but discharged that judgment by writing "correcting those comments is a follow-up outside this section" into AGENTS.md and nothing else. Eva's review explicitly called this out: "file a follow-up issue for the stale `evolution.md:34,275` 'off by default' text rather than only flagging it inline." An in-prose "this is a follow-up" note has no owner, no queue, and no state — it is indistinguishable from a permanent caveat, and in a repo where an autonomous router picks work off the issue tracker it is invisible to the only mechanism that would ever action it.
- **Root cause**: I conflated *documenting* a known defect with *tracking* it. The scope rule ("don't fix what the issue didn't ask for") correctly stopped me from editing `evolution.md`, but I let the scope rule also stop me from the zero-scope-cost action that actually routes the work — filing an issue, which touches no file in the diff and cannot expand the PR's review surface. Worse, the inline note made the omission feel handled, so nothing prompted a second look.
- **Apply next time**: whenever a build or fix run discovers a real defect it deliberately declines to fix as out-of-scope, file a GitHub issue in the target repo for it in the same run (`gh issue create`, cite exact `path:line`, state the impact and whether it is wording-only vs behavioral), and make the in-diff note point at that issue number rather than at an untracked "follow-up". Filing an issue is never scope creep — it changes no file under review — so the scope rule that blocks the fix must not be read as also blocking the tracker. Rule of thumb: if a sentence in my diff says "this is a follow-up" / "out of scope" / "should be corrected separately" about a concrete defect, that sentence must carry an issue reference before the commit lands.


## L-2026-07-26-45 — Retargeting a version floor is a two-part edit: the number and the property phrase glued to it must move together, or the "corrected" header contradicts the same bullets in a new way

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#482 (branch `eva-impl/194-sub-agent-nesting-pipeline-docs`, cross-repo fix, round 3)
- **Signal**: round 2 applied [[L-2026-07-26-43]]'s own prescription — "does every version floor name the release where the behavior I am telling the reader to rely on actually holds, rather than the release that introduced the feature" — and moved the AGENTS.md § Pipeline header's floor from `min-version 2.1.172` to `min-version 2.1.219`. The number was right and the round-2 finding was genuinely closed, but the noun phrase attached to the old number was carried over and *extended* rather than replaced: the header shipped as "min-version 2.1.219 for nesting **on by default**". Eva's round-3 review caught that this contradicts the section's own first bullet, which documents nesting as on by default (5 deep) across v2.1.172–v2.1.216 — so 2.1.219 is emphatically not the floor for "nesting on by default"; only v2.1.217–v2.1.218 defaulted off. 2.1.219 is the floor for a *different* property: the depth-3 default. One header, two consecutive rounds, two different self-contradictions against the same unchanged bullet list.
- **Root cause**: I treated the floor as the variable and the property as fixed context, so the edit was a number swap with a clarifying suffix appended. But a version floor is a single indivisible claim — `<release>` is only meaningful as "the release from which `<property>` holds" — and when a default has flipped mid-history the file documents *several* properties with *different* floors (capability-exists: 2.1.172; on-by-default: 2.1.172, minus the 217–218 gap; depth-3: 2.1.219). Picking the new number off the "current behavior" bullet while keeping the old sentence's property phrase silently pairs a floor with a property it does not govern. The suffix "for nesting on by default" even felt like it *added* rigor, which is why it survived my own re-read: I checked that 2.1.219 was correctly sourced (it was) instead of checking what the sentence now asserted as a whole.
- **Apply next time**: when a fix round changes a version number inside a `min-version`/`requires`/`as of` marker, rewrite the property phrase in the same edit, then read the marker as one sentence against the detail block — "X holds from `<release>` onward" — and confirm the block does not show X holding earlier or lapsing later. If the block documents a default that flipped, the header must name the property whose floor is actually the cited release (here: "depth-3 default as of v2.1.219+"), not the most general-sounding one. Corollary, reinforcing [[L-2026-07-26-31]]: the framing-vs-detail consistency pass of [[L-2026-07-26-43]] is owed on every fix round that touches the framing, including the round that is applying that very lesson — a finding is closed only when the replacement sentence has been re-diffed against the bullets, not when the flagged token has been changed.


## L-2026-07-26-46 — Supplementing one git command's output with a second one requires checking that both share a scope AND a path-form convention

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#485 (branch `eva-impl/483-deep-review-untracked-files`, cross-repo fix)
- **Signal**: Eva's own REQUEST_CHANGES review flagged that `deep-review/SKILL.md`'s new untracked-file coverage used bare `git ls-files --others --exclude-standard` to supplement `git diff`. Verified empirically in a throwaway repo: run from a subdirectory, `git diff --name-only` returned the repo-wide `other/tracked.txt` (root-relative) while `git ls-files --others --exclude-standard` returned only `untracked-sub.txt` (cwd-scoped, cwd-relative). So the "fix" for a silent-coverage-gap issue reproduced that exact gap one level down — untracked files outside the cwd were still silently skipped, and the report still came back clean.
- **Root cause**: I reached for the canonical one-liner everyone uses to list untracked files and assumed it composed with `git diff` because both are porcelain-ish git commands over "the working tree". They do not: `git ls-files` applies an implicit cwd pathspec and prints cwd-relative paths; `git diff` applies no implicit pathspec and prints root-relative ones. Two commands being "about the working tree" says nothing about whether they agree on *which* working-tree subset or *how* they spell a path. The bug is invisible from the repo root — the only place a skill author naturally tests — which is why it survived my own testing and the deep-reviewer pass.
- **Apply next time**: whenever a fix supplements command A's output with command B's (union of two diffs, two file lists, two search results), explicitly test BOTH from a subdirectory before trusting the composition, and check two independent axes — scope (does B apply an implicit cwd pathspec A doesn't?) and path form (relative to cwd or to the repo root?). For git specifically: `git ls-files` needs `--full-name -- :/` to match `git diff`'s repo-wide, root-relative behaviour. And note the coupled second-order effect: once the paths become root-relative, any per-path follow-up command must also run from the repo root (`git -C "$(git rev-parse --show-toplevel)" ...`) or it fails with `Could not access '<path>'` — fixing the scope without fixing the resolution just moves the break.

## L-2026-07-26-47 — Widening a gate's "is there anything here?" test can silently orphan the fallback branch behind it

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#485 (branch `eva-impl/483-deep-review-untracked-files`, cross-repo fix)
- **Signal**: Eva's own REQUEST_CHANGES review's second finding: to close the untracked-file gap in `deep-review`'s default entry path, I widened Step 1's "are there unstaged changes?" test from `git diff` alone to `git diff` OR `git ls-files --others`. That was correct in isolation, but the very next branch read "if staged AND unstaged are both empty, fall back to the committed diff on this branch" — so a clean PR branch carrying one stray untracked file (a scratch note, an editor backup, an unignored build artifact) now took the widened branch and was offered only "review working tree" or "Cancel". The committed-diff branch review — the whole point of that fallback, and the common case on a PR branch — became unreachable, traded away for a one-file working-tree review.
- **Root cause**: I evaluated the widened predicate only against the case it was meant to fix (a repo whose *only* change is a new untracked file, which now correctly reaches working-tree mode) and never against the cases it newly *captures* — inputs that used to fall through to the else-branch and now don't. A predicate widening is a control-flow change to every branch downstream of it, not just to the one being fixed; the harm shows up in the branch that stops executing, which by construction produces no error, no warning, and no diff to notice.
- **Apply next time**: after widening any gate condition (`if empty` → `if empty-by-a-broader-definition`, adding a new source to an existence check), enumerate the branches *below* it and ask for each: "which inputs used to reach this and no longer do, and is losing that acceptable?" Where the answer is no, the fix is usually to offer both paths rather than to narrow the predicate back — here, resolving the merge base *before* prompting and adding the committed diff as a third option alongside the working-tree one, so neither scope is lost. Narrowing the predicate again would have re-opened the original bug; the two requirements are only in conflict if the gate is assumed to pick exactly one outcome.


## L-2026-07-26-48 — A "supplement the diff with an enumerated file list" feature needs a cap at the moment it is written, not after review flags it

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#485 (branch `eva-impl/483-deep-review-untracked-files`, cross-repo fix)
- **Signal**: Eva's own REQUEST_CHANGES review judged the core fix correct (`git ls-files --others --exclude-standard --full-name -- :/` + per-path `git diff --no-index`, verified) but flagged that the new untracked supplement was **unbounded**: `deep-review/SKILL.md` told the skill to synthesize and inline a full diff for *every* untracked path, so a repo with an unignored `dist/`, `node_modules/`, or coverage-output directory would push thousands of generated files into the deep-reviewer's prompt before it reached a single real change. Three prior rounds (the original implement run, a self-review round, and `[pr-fix-1]`) all reviewed this exact block for *correctness of scope* — cwd-relative vs root-relative paths, quoting, index mutation, fallback reachability — and none of them asked "how many, and how big".
- **Root cause**: the feature was framed throughout as a **coverage** problem ("#483: untracked files get zero review coverage"), and every review pass inherited that frame, checking only whether the supplement covered *enough*. Nobody asked the dual question — whether it could cover *too much* — even though the repo already carries settled constants for exactly this class of bound: `MAX_SCAN_FILE_BYTES` / `MAX_VALIDATE_FILE_BYTES` (256 KB) in `scan_repo.mjs` and the validators, and `DEFAULT_TOTAL_LIMIT` (50) in `discover.mjs`. The resource bound was available to copy the whole time; the framing is what hid it.
- **Apply next time**: whenever a change makes something **enumerate a set and inline each element** into a prompt, a report, or an issue body — untracked paths, glob hits, search results, log lines, API pages — decide the count cap AND the byte cap in the same edit that adds the enumeration, before writing the prose. Grep the repo for existing cap constants (`MAX_*_BYTES`, `*_LIMIT`) and mirror them rather than inventing numbers, so the new bound is defensible on review. And apply [[L-2026-07-13-01]]'s rule to the cap itself: a skipped element must propagate an explicit "not reviewed" state to every downstream consumer (here: the sub-agent's dispatch prompt, the user-facing report, and the empty-result exit path) — never let a capped run render identically to a complete one.

## L-2026-07-26-49 — Each pr-fix round appends a CHANGELOG bullet, and the earlier bullets quietly become wrong

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#485 (branch `eva-impl/483-deep-review-untracked-files`, cross-repo fix)
- **Signal**: Eva's own REQUEST_CHANGES review flagged that the `## v0.70.2` CHANGELOG section's bullets 2 (**Fix**) and 5 (**Self-review catch**) still quoted `git ls-files --others --exclude-standard` — the command form from the *first* implementation — while bullet 6 (**Review catch — cwd scoping**), added a round later, documented that every invocation now uses `git ls-files --others --exclude-standard --full-name -- :/`. The section contradicted itself, and in `ievo-ai/skills` that section ships **verbatim as the public GitHub Release body** (`cut-release.yml`), so the contradiction would have shipped to users, not just to reviewers.
- **Root cause**: the repo's changelog convention is a narrative accretion — each review round appends a new `**Review catch — ...**` bullet describing what changed. That is genuinely useful history, but it means every round's bullet silently *supersedes* command text, flag lists, or behaviour claims quoted in earlier bullets of the same unreleased section, and nothing forces a reconciliation pass. Appending is the whole habit; re-reading the bullets above the one being added is not.
- **Apply next time**: on every `[pr-fix-N]` round that touches a repo where the changelog section doubles as release copy, after writing the new bullet re-read the **entire** unreleased `## vX.Y.Z` section top-to-bottom and reconcile it against the final shipped state — concretely, grep the section for each command/flag/identifier the round changed (`grep -n 'git ls-files' CHANGELOG.md`) and confirm every occurrence shows the *final* form, not an intermediate one. Earlier bullets may keep describing the old behaviour as the *problem* they diagnose; they must never present it as the shipped command. Treat "the section is internally consistent as a whole" as part of the fix, not as documentation polish deferred to review.


## L-2026-07-26-50 — Hardening a path-handling command for the shell is only half the job: git's own porcelain output C-quotes non-ASCII paths by default

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#485 (branch `eva-impl/483-deep-review-untracked-files`, cross-repo fix, round 3)
- **Signal**: Eva's REQUEST_CHANGES review found that `deep-review/SKILL.md`'s new untracked-file supplement, `git ls-files --others --exclude-standard --full-name -- :/`, silently drops any path containing a non-ASCII byte. Reproduced locally on git 2.54.0: `core.quotePath` defaults to **on**, so `café.md` is listed as the literal `"caf\303\251.md"` — surrounding double quotes and octal escapes included. Fed to the per-path `git diff --no-index -- /dev/null "<path>"`, that name matches nothing, git writes `error: Could not access ...` to stderr and **exits 1 — the exact status `--no-index` returns on a *successful* difference**, which the SKILL.md prose had already documented as "expected, not an error". So the failure was undetectable by exit code and the file got zero review coverage: a second instance of the very bug class (#483) this PR was opened to fix.
- **Root cause**: round 2 of this same PR had already been told to harden the command for hostile filenames — and did, but only at the **shell** layer (it wrapped `<path>` in double quotes so spaces and metacharacters survive). I treated "filenames with spaces are handled" as "filename encoding is handled" and never asked the separate question of how git **emits** paths in the first place. The two layers are independent: shell quoting protects the path on its way *into* a command; `core.quotePath` corrupts it on its way *out* of one. Nothing failed loudly, because the corrupted path produced a nonzero exit that the design had pre-declared benign.
- **Apply next time**: any time a command consumes another command's list of paths programmatically, pass `-z` on the producing side (`git ls-files -z`, `git diff -z --name-only`, `find -print0`, `grep -z`) and split the consumer on NUL, not newline — never rely on `core.quotePath` being off, since it defaults on and its corruption is silent rather than fatal. Shell-quote at the use site *as well*: `-z` removes git's quoting, it does not escape anything for the shell — the two fixes are complementary, not alternatives, and having done one is no evidence about the other. Separately: whenever a design documents a nonzero exit as "expected, not an error", check whether the *error* path returns that same status. If it does (as `git diff --no-index` does with 1), an exit-code check cannot distinguish them — require a positive success signal (a non-empty diff body, empty stderr) before counting the item as handled, or the error silently books itself as success.

## L-2026-07-26-51 — Changing a dispatch prompt's contract is a two-file edit: the receiving agent definition is not in the diff, so nothing points the reviewer at it

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#485 (branch `eva-impl/483-deep-review-untracked-files`, cross-repo fix, round 3)
- **Signal**: Eva's REQUEST_CHANGES review flagged that `agents/deep-reviewer.md` was untouched by the PR even though the PR changed what the orchestrator sends it. Round 2 had added a `## Coverage caveats` section to `deep-review/SKILL.md`'s Step 4 dispatch prompt (naming untracked files the 50-path / 256 KB caps left out), but the receiving agent's `## Input` still documented exactly three fields — `diff`, `changed_files`, `repo_context` — so the new section arrived undocumented on the callee side. Worse, that agent's standing rule "**Complete checklist.** All 11 points must be evaluated … Skipping a point is not allowed" pushed directly against the caveat's whole purpose: the caller was saying "your input is partial, say so", the callee's contract was saying "report full coverage regardless".
- **Root cause**: the caller and the callee live in different files, and only the caller's file was in the diff. A prompt template is an API — adding a section to it changes the contract — but it doesn't *look* like an API change the way editing a function signature does, so the "who consumes this?" reflex never fired. Every local gate was green and stayed green, because prose-to-prose contracts between a skill and its sub-agent are enforced by nothing mechanical. The PR's own `/ievo:deep-review` self-review pass could not have caught it either: `agents/deep-reviewer.md` was not in the diff, so it was not in `changed_files`, so the reviewer never read it.
- **Apply next time**: when a change adds, renames, or removes a section of a **dispatch prompt / sub-agent input template**, treat the receiving agent definition as part of the same change set — grep for the `subagent_type` / agent name and open that file in the same edit, updating both its input contract *and* any standing rule that the new field is meant to modulate. Then verify the two read as one coherent instruction, not merely as two files that each mention the field. More generally: for cross-file contracts carried in prose rather than in code, the diff is not a sufficient review surface — deliberately pull the counterpart file into scope, because a reviewer restricted to `changed_files` is structurally blind to the half you didn't touch.


## L-2026-07-26-52 — A byte-level delimiter is only correct if it survives the transport the *consumer* reads through: `-z`'s NUL bytes never reach a model reading Bash output

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#485 (branch `eva-impl/483-deep-review-untracked-files`, cross-repo fix, round 4)
- **Signal**: Eva's REQUEST_CHANGES review found round 3's own fix unusable as written. `deep-review/SKILL.md` told the skill to run `git ls-files -z --others --exclude-standard --full-name -- :/` and "**split the listing on NUL, not on newline**" — but raw NUL bytes do not survive Bash-tool output (verified: `printf 'a\0b\0'` reads back as `a b`), so every untracked path arrives merged into one space-joined string, indistinguishable from a single filename containing spaces. The documented split was literally unperformable, and the joined string matches no file, so the per-path `git diff --no-index` failed and **every** untracked file got zero coverage. Round 3's fix was strictly *worse* than the bug it closed: `core.quotePath` corruption only hit non-ASCII names, while this fires on any tree holding two or more untracked paths — the third consecutive round to re-open #483's own bug class while closing it.
- **Root cause**: I applied the previous round's captured remedy verbatim — [[L-2026-07-26-50]]'s "pass `-z` on the producing side and split the consumer on NUL" — without re-deriving it for *this* consumer. That rule is correct when the consumer is a real shell pipeline (`xargs -0`, `read -r -d ''`, `find -print0`), which is the context it was written in. Here the consumer is a model reading rendered tool output, and the boundary between producer and consumer is lossy for control bytes: the NUL is stripped before anything can split on it. Nothing failed loudly, because the corrupted listing again produced the pre-declared-benign exit 1. A stored lesson carries an implicit consumer, and pasting its remedy into a design with a different consumer silently drops the precondition that made it work.
- **Apply next time**: before prescribing any byte-level protocol in a SKILL.md, agent prompt, or dispatch template — `-z`, `--null`, `-print0`, `-0`, raw binary, ANSI escapes, tab-delimited output — first name who actually parses the bytes: a real shell process, or the model reading rendered tool output. For the latter, prove the byte survives the boundary with a one-liner (`printf 'a\0b\0'`) *before* writing the instruction, and add a normalizing filter at the producer (`| tr '\0' '\n'`) so the documented split is performable, keeping the original flag for the property it still buys (`-z` still disables `core.quotePath` — the two are complementary, not alternatives). Treat "can the reader of this instruction actually execute it?" as a review question distinct from "is this command correct" — a correct command paired with an unperformable parsing step is a broken instruction. And when reusing a lesson from the store, re-derive its remedy against the current consumer instead of pasting it: applying [[L-2026-07-13-01]]'s propagate-an-explicit-unscanned-state rule is what kept this round's failure *visible* (the newline-in-filename residual books itself as a skipped `could not be read` path) rather than silent.


## L-2026-07-26-53 — Quoting an interpolated placeholder is not escaping it: a command *template* whose `<path>` a model fills from tool output is code, not data

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#485 (branch `eva-impl/483-deep-review-untracked-files`, cross-repo fix, round 5)
- **Signal**: Eva's REQUEST_CHANGES review found command injection in `deep-review/SKILL.md`'s untracked-file supplement. The skill instructed the agent to run `git -C "$(git rev-parse --show-toplevel)" diff --no-index -- /dev/null "<path>"` once per untracked filename — with `<path>` substituted into the command *text*. Double quotes do not neutralize `$`, backticks or `\`, so a file named `` `id` `` or `$(curl …|sh)` executes when the line is parsed. Reproduced in a throwaway repo: the documented form ran `touch /tmp/PWNED` straight out of a filename. Filenames are attacker-chosen (a dependency's postinstall, an unpacked archive, a repo cloned *in order to review it*), so "review my diff" was arbitrary command execution — and the repo's own AGENTS.md already states "No untrusted free-text crosses into a shell". Rounds 1–4 of this same PR each hardened this exact block (cwd scope, quoting, `-z`, `| tr`) without once questioning the interpolation shape itself.
- **Root cause**: round 1 had been told "filenames with spaces break this" and fixed it by wrapping the placeholder in double quotes. That closed the *breakage* and read like it closed the *injection*, because both failures present as "weird filename, weird result" — so every later round inherited "paths are quoted, therefore paths are handled" and audited only the data flowing into the placeholder (is it root-relative? is it C-quoted? does the NUL survive?), never the placeholder itself. The distinction that got lost: a shell variable's stored bytes are substituted without re-parsing, while text pasted into a command line is parsed. Quoting only helps with the former. A SKILL.md actively hides this, because a "template with a `<path>` hole" looks like an API call with a parameter, when the hole is really string concatenation into source that a shell will then compile — the same class as SQL string-building, one abstraction layer up.
- **Apply next time**: when a skill, agent prompt, or runbook tells a model to run a command **once per item** over a list the model got from tool output, do not write the command with a `<placeholder>` at all — write the loop that reads the items itself (`while IFS= read -r -d '' p; do … "$p"; done` over a `-z`/`-print0` producer), so the value is a variable the model never retypes, and wrap it in a subshell if it needs to `cd`. Ask "who parses this byte — a shell, or a callee receiving it as an argv entry?" and treat every `"<name>"`, `"<path>"`, `"<title>"` hole in a documented command as unescaped code until that loop exists. Grep the repo first: a project that has already been bitten states the rule somewhere (`feedback/SKILL.md`'s `--title "$TITLE"` read back from a file, `commands/update.md`'s "never interpolate `source.path` into a Bash command"), so reusing that pattern is both faster and reviewable. Corollary for fix rounds, sharpening [[L-2026-07-26-50]]: when an earlier round fixed a *symptom* of hostile input (spaces breaking a command), never read that as coverage of the *class* — re-derive the threat from scratch, because the cheap fix and the correct fix look identical in the diff.


## L-2026-07-27-01 — A hand-written glob pattern list must be checked for anchoring consistency: a bare `*.ext` entry is root-only, not recursive

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#486 (branch `eva-impl/200-vuln-scan-file-sensitivity-classification`, cross-repo fix)
- **Signal**: Eva's own REQUEST_CHANGES review flagged that Phase 1.5's new sensitive-path pattern list in `plugins/ievo/commands/vuln-scan.md:136` was half-anchored — `.env*`, `*.pem`, `*.key`, `*.p12`, `.netrc` carried no `**/` prefix while `**/secrets.*`, `**/.aws/credentials`, `**/service-account*.json`, `**/*.token`, `**/id_rsa`, `**/id_ed25519` did. Glob anchors a relative pattern to the search root, so the five bare entries matched only at the module root: a nested `config/.env.test` or `certs/server.key` — precisely the case the classification step exists to surface — was silently missed, and the failure is invisible (an empty `sensitive_files` list is a documented, legitimate outcome, so the miss looks like a clean module).
- **Root cause**: the list was written in the mental idiom of `.gitignore` / shell `find -name`, where a bare `*.pem` means "any `.pem` anywhere". The `**/` prefixes that did appear were added ad hoc for the entries with an obvious directory component (`.aws/credentials`, `secrets.*`), not from a deliberate per-entry anchoring decision — so the two conventions coexisted in one line and read as intentional variety rather than a bug. Nothing mechanical could catch it: the file is prose instructions for an agent, so no linter, test, or CI gate in the repo evaluates the patterns.
- **Apply next time**: when authoring or editing ANY glob/pattern list that will be fed to a recursive matcher (Glob, `fast-glob`, `picomatch`), do an explicit per-entry anchoring pass before finalizing — read each entry alone and ask "does this match at every depth or only at the root?", and require every entry in one list to share the same convention. Treat a list that mixes `**/`-prefixed and bare entries as a defect on sight, not a style choice. This is the pattern-list analogue of [[L-2026-07-18-02]]'s regex-anchoring lesson (`grep -E '^...$'` anchors per line, not per string): in both cases the anchoring semantics of the matcher, not the pattern text's apparent intent, decide what actually matches, and both failures are silent on the happy path.


## L-2026-07-27-02 — Writing a workaround's COST presumes its EFFECT; and a step whose only possible actor is the user must not be phrased as guidance the skill can act on

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#488 (branch `eva-impl/193-init-cd-directory-switch`, cross-repo fix, round 1)
- **Signal**: Eva's REQUEST_CHANGES review on a one-paragraph `init/SKILL.md` addition found the paragraph's fallback clause inverted the very defect the issue (#193) exists to fix. The paragraph said `/cd <project-path>` (CC v2.1.169+) moves the session, and that on older Claude Code "`Bash(cd ...)` works but invalidates the cache for every subsequent tool call". `Bash(cd ...)` does not work: it changes only that call's shell directory, while `Read`/`Write`, `.claude/` settings resolution and the pipeline's project-scoped installs keep resolving against the session's working directory — so `.ievo/` and every install still land outside the project. The doc sold a *correctness* failure as a *performance* trade-off, which is worse than omitting the fallback: a reader who accepts the cache cost walks straight into the silent misdirection. The second finding was structural — `/cd` is a built-in command Claude Code only recognizes when the **user** types it at the start of a message ([commands reference](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/commands)), so a paragraph inside a SKILL.md telling the reader to "get there first" names no actor that can execute it. The fix had to become a step the skill runs (`pwd`, project-signal check, conditional `AskUserQuestion`, halt), with `Bash(cd ...)` explicitly forbidden rather than offered.
- **Root cause**: two failures that compose. (1) I verified the *primary* mechanism against a primary source (re-fetched the v2.1.169 release notes and confirmed the `/cd` bullet verbatim — the review said that citation verifies clean) and then wrote the *fallback* from prior belief, never asking the same question of it. A trade-off sentence ("X works, it just costs Y") is two claims, and the cost clause is rhetorically load-bearing: it signals the effect claim was already settled, so neither I nor a reviewer re-examines it. Hedging the cost made the unverified part read as the careful part. (2) The paragraph was written in the register of the human-facing prose around it, so "if this session started outside the project, get there first" was never tested against "who runs this — the user or the model?" — the same actor question [[L-2026-07-26-19]] recorded for `/plugin`, here in its mirror form: that entry caught a *user-typed slash command written as a model step*; this is a *user-only action written as ambient advice with no actor at all*, which no `--help` run can falsify because there is no command to check.
- **Apply next time**: when a doc sentence has the shape "A is best; if A is unavailable, B works but costs C", treat B as a separate, unverified claim and confirm B's EFFECT before writing its price — a workaround documented with a trade-off is more dangerous than one documented as unavailable, because the trade-off tells the reader the decision is already made. State "no equivalent exists" when that is the truth. And run the actor question on every added instruction, not just on ones that name a command: for each sentence ask "who executes this — the user, or the skill?", then write it in that actor's form (a user-only action becomes a halt plus a message addressed to the user; a skill-executed action becomes the tool call the skill makes). A sentence whose actor is neither is not guidance, it is decoration. Extends [[L-2026-07-26-19]] beyond "use the shell form, not the slash form" to "every step needs an actor, and steps only the user can take must terminate the skill's own control flow".


## L-2026-07-27-03 — A table cell asserting the ABSENCE of something ("leaf technique", "n/a", "no sub-types") is an unverified claim about a page you never opened, and it silently switches off the guidance underneath it

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#490 (cross-repo fix)
- **Signal**: Eva's own REQUEST_CHANGES review flagged that the new ATT&CK cross-reference table in `vuln-scan/SKILL.md` marked **T1552** (Unsecured Credentials) and **T1546** (Event Triggered Execution) as `— (leaf technique)` in its `Sub-technique example` column. Both are wrong: verified against `attack.mitre.org`, T1552 has **8** sub-techniques and T1546 has **18**; T1195 has 3 and T1059 has 13; only T1190 is a genuine leaf. The implement run had done real external verification on this very table — it fetched MITRE to establish that `T1059.007` is specifically *JavaScript* (correcting the issue's proposal) and to correct the issue's "v15" citation to the verified v19.1 / 2026-04-28 — and wrote both corrections up in the CHANGELOG. The two rows it never opened a page for are exactly the two it got wrong.
- **Root cause**: verifying that an identifier *exists and is named X* is a positive claim, and it feels like it needs a fetch — so it got one. Writing `— (leaf technique)` is a claim that a whole set is *empty*, and it doesn't feel like a citation at all; it reads as a formatting choice for "nothing to put in this cell". So the absence rows were filled from recollection while the presence rows were filled from the source, inside one table, in one sitting. The damage was larger than one wrong cell because the column was load-bearing: the paragraph directly below instructed "prefer a sub-technique ID over the bare parent when determinable", and `— (leaf technique)` is precisely the annotation that *cancels* that instruction for a row. Two false leaf labels therefore disabled the sub-technique guidance for the credential-exposure and hook-abuse rows — the two an iEvo plugin/skill scan hits most — turning a factual slip into a wrong instruction on the hottest path. No local gate can catch this class: `validate_skills.mjs`, pre-commit, and the coverage gate all read structure, never the truth of a prose or table claim.
