- **Apply next time**: treat every "none / n/a / leaf / no sub-types / not applicable" cell as a citation owed, and open the parent's own page before writing it — an emptiness claim is *more* expensive to verify than a presence claim, not free, because nothing in the draft points at what you would have to read. Concretely, when authoring a reference table of external identifiers, enumerate the full child list for **every** row from the upstream page (not just the rows you are changing or correcting), then write the cell; if a row's children are truncated for space, say "e.g." and state that the list is illustrative, so a reader cannot mistake a short list for a complete one. And before shipping any table, read the paragraph that surrounds it and ask which cell values act as *switches* for that prose — a cell that means "the guidance below does not apply here" must clear a higher verification bar than a cell that is merely data. Extends `[[L-2026-07-24-07]]` (external- and internal-fact verification are separate checks) and `[[L-2026-07-24-13]]` ("the index page didn't show it" is never "it isn't there") to the absence-claim case: here the page was never opened at all, and the missing evidence was recorded as a positive finding.


## L-2026-07-27-04 — A verification step must be derived from what the command being verified actually MUTATES, not from the artifact the issue happens to name

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#491 (branch `eva-impl/182-update-plugin-list-verification`, cross-repo fix)
- **Signal**: Eva's own REQUEST_CHANGES review flagged that both new "post-update verification" lines added to `commands/update.md`'s Step 6 reload reminder verified the wrong artifact. `/ievo:update` refreshes *vendored* agents/skills (`.claude/agents/`, `.claude/skills/`, Codex `.agents/skills/`) — it never updates the iEvo plugin itself (that is `/ievo:version`'s job). So `/plugin list --enabled` reports an iEvo version an update run cannot change, and `codex plugin list --json | grep -i ievo` does not enumerate `.agents/skills/` at all. Worse than useless: a user who runs the suggested check sees an unchanged version number and reads it as a FAILED refresh. Both gates were green and the PR's own `/ievo:deep-review` pass ran on this diff — it caught an unrelated doc-drift (a wrong precedent citation in the CHANGELOG) but not this, because the added lines are internally coherent and only wrong relative to the *host command's* semantics.
- **Root cause**: the issue (#182) specified both commands verbatim in its Acceptance criteria ("CC variant uses `/plugin list --enabled`", "Codex variant uses `codex plugin list --json`"), and the implementer treated satisfying those ACs as the task. The issue's premise — that `/ievo:update` "updates the iEvo installation", so a plugin listing confirms it — was never checked against `update.md`'s own frontmatter and Steps 1-4, which state plainly that it refreshes vendored copies and that the only state an update run moves is each overlay's `source.commit_sha`. The implementer did apply skepticism one level down (it explicitly re-derived whether the #241 `--enabled`-is-interactive-only correction applied, and correctly concluded it didn't) — but that verified the *flag syntax* while leaving the *choice of artifact to inspect* unexamined. Verifying a detail of a wrong check makes the wrong check look audited.
- **Apply next time**: before writing ANY verification/confirmation step, first enumerate what the command being verified actually mutates — read that command's own body end-to-end and name the concrete artifact(s) whose state changes (here: `.claude/skills/<name>/` content plus the overlay's `source.commit_sha`, both already surfaced in its Step 6 report) — and point the check at exactly one of those. Only then check the proposed command's flags/version gates. An issue's Acceptance criteria naming a specific command is a proposal about mechanism, not an established fact about scope: a criterion that presumes the wrong artifact must be reported as not-applicable and replaced, not satisfied literally. Concrete tell that this is happening: the verification step names a version/identity that the run under verification has no code path to change. Reinforces [[L-2026-07-18-01]] and [[L-2026-07-18-02]] (never adopt an issue's own Recommendation without independently re-deriving it) — this run extends both from "re-derive the pattern" to "re-derive the premise: does the thing being inspected even move?", and adds that a self-review pass is unlikely to catch this class, since the added text is self-consistent and only wrong against the surrounding command's semantics.


## L-2026-07-27-05 — Verifying a version NUMBER against release notes doesn't verify the "Where" attribution naming which repo file uses that feature; both need independent checks
- **Source**: eva-implement run for ievo-ai/skills#181 (version-compatibility feature-matrix table)
- **Signal**: `/ievo:deep-review`'s Phase 4.5 pass (dispatched on the finished AGENTS.md diff) found 4 findings (2 blockers) in a 14-row feature-version matrix: two rows' "Where" column named files that don't reference the feature at all (`schedule/SKILL.md` cited for "Dynamic Workflows" actually documents Routines, a different CC feature, gated at a different version in its own `compatibility:` field; `evolution.md`/`vuln-scanner.md` cited for `/reload-skills` only contain an unrelated doc-URL fragment `#preload-skills-into-subagents`), and two more named a file that explicitly documents the OPPOSITE of the claim (`init/SKILL.md` Step 12.6 states in its own prose that it deliberately does NOT use `/plugin list --enabled` — the interactive-only flag — and uses `--json` + a field filter instead; `security-check/SKILL.md`'s `Stop` hook is a plain `echo`, and `hooks-setup/SKILL.md`'s own text says outright "this skill does not wire that up today"). Every version NUMBER in the table was independently correct — I'd fetched each `gh api repos/anthropics/claude-code/releases/tags/vX.Y.Z` and verified the introducing release, catching one factual error in the issue's own proposed table in the process (`effort:` frontmatter was mis-cited as v2.1.149; verification found the real dates were v2.1.78 for agents and v2.1.80 for skills). The "Where" column got none of that same rigor.
- **Root cause**: I treated "verify the version" and "verify the attribution" as one task covered by one kind of check, but they need two different primitives. The version claim is checked by fetching a release note and reading its prose. The attribution claim ("file X uses feature Y") was checked with `grep -rl <term> file` — a substring-presence test — which only proves the term appears somewhere in the file, not that the file's surrounding prose describes *using* the feature (a coincidental substring hit, a doc-URL fragment, or the file explicitly disclaiming use all pass the same `grep -l`). This is the same class of error [[L-2026-07-24-07]] describes (issue-proposed facts about the repo itself need grep-verification, not trust) but one level deeper: even a grep-based "verification" can itself be a false positive if the grep target is presence rather than the actual claim being asserted.
- **Apply next time**: for any table/list row that pairs "feature X" with "file Y uses/relies on it," verify the attribution as its own claim, separate from the version number — read the actual surrounding lines the grep hit (`grep -n` then open enough context, or just Read the section), and confirm the file's prose affirmatively describes USING the feature, not merely containing the search term. A file that explicitly says "this does NOT use X" or "not wired up today" is strong evidence AGAINST inclusion, not a hit to celebrate. Where feasible, dispatch `/ievo:deep-review` (or an equivalent independent read) on any new cross-referencing table before treating it as final — it caught exactly this class of drift here, cheaply, before the PR opened.


## L-2026-07-27-06 — A "Where does the repo use this?" attribution column needs a use/mention distinction applied to EVERY row, not just the rows a pre-commit review named

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#492 (branch `eva-impl/181-version-compatibility-table`, cross-repo fix, round 1/5)
- **Signal**: Eva's own REQUEST_CHANGES review found **three** rows of AGENTS.md's new feature-version matrix whose "Where" column cited `hooks-setup/SKILL.md` for features that file documents *not* using — `SessionStart` `reloadSkills`/`sessionTitle` (:330 "Other SessionStart return fields exist but are **unused here**"), `MessageDisplay` (:573 "**not configured by this skill**"), and `/reload-skills` (cited only in the § References list; the sole real user is `commands/update.md` § 6). The PR body's `## iEvo skill pass` section already stated that `/ievo:deep-review` had caught this exact defect class and that it was "Fixed on this branch" — it had dropped two rows and corrected two others, then stopped. A repo-wide `grep -rn` for each remaining row's feature name would have found all three in one pass.
- **Root cause**: two coupled failures. (1) The implementer treated `/ievo:deep-review`'s finding list as the *work item* rather than as a *sample of a defect class* — it fixed the four rows the reviewer named and never re-derived the remaining rows under the newly-learned rule. (2) The rule itself was never written down while the column was being built: "Where" was populated by grepping for the feature **name**, and a grep hit is a *mention*, not a *use*. A file citing a release note, or explicitly documenting that it does not configure a feature, matches the same grep as a file that actually emits it — so the very sentence that disproves a row is what put the row there. Compounding it, the section's "highest floor" claim (v2.1.163) and its enterprise `requiredMinimumVersion` example were derived from that same defective table instead of from the repo's actual maximum `compatibility:` floor, so the pinning example under-pinned by 35 patch releases: `hooks-setup/SKILL.md` Step 5.6 *writes* `Notification` matchers gated at v2.1.198+, and `init/SKILL.md` + AGENTS.md § Security model both state a v2.1.195+ operator minimum for dual-gate plugin install consent.
- **Apply next time**: when authoring any table/column that asserts "repo file X uses platform feature Y", (a) write the use-vs-mention criterion into the section's own prose *before* filling the column, so it binds every row and survives into future edits — a grep hit qualifies only if the file emits, writes, sets, or invokes the feature; a References-list citation or an explicit "not used here / not configured by this skill" note disqualifies it; (b) after any reviewer rejects one row on that criterion, immediately re-audit **all** remaining rows against it in the same round (`grep -rn` per feature, then read each hit's surrounding sentence) rather than fixing only the named ones — this is completing the finding, not scope creep, and is the same failure shape as [[L-2026-07-24-05]]; and (c) never derive a "highest floor / recommended minimum" number from the table you just authored — derive it independently from the source of truth (here `grep -h '^compatibility:' plugins/ievo/skills/*/SKILL.md | grep -oE 'v[0-9.]+' | sort -V | tail -1`) and then check the table can account for it. A floor claim that rests on an incomplete enumeration is false the moment one row is dropped. See also [[L-2026-07-24-10]] (a PR-body "found and fixed" claim is not evidence) and [[L-2026-07-24-07]] (proposed wording can embed stale facts about the repo itself).


## L-2026-07-27-07 — A "cite release notes, not the docs page" rule sources WHEN a setting shipped and nothing else; a copy-pasteable config artifact also needs its value SHAPE and its FILE sourced

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#492 (branch `eva-impl/181-version-compatibility-table`, cross-repo fix, round 2/5)
- **Signal**: Eva's own REQUEST_CHANGES review verified all 10 matrix rows and every cited version as correct, then found the section's single copy-pasteable artifact — `{ "requiredMinimumVersion": { "min": "2.1.198" } }` under § Enterprise pinning — was its only unsourced claim, and wrong twice over: the `{"min": …}` wrapper appears nowhere in-repo and nowhere upstream, and the block named no file even though `requiredMinimumVersion` is a *managed* setting that Claude Code never reads from `.claude/settings.json`. Fetching `https://code.claude.com/docs/en/settings.md` (the raw-markdown form of the docs page — `curl` it and `grep -n requiredM`) returned the § Available settings table row verbatim: "Managed settings only. Minimum Claude Code version required to start …", Example column `"2.1.150"` — a plain version string, and `requiredMaximumVersion` is a separate scalar setting, not the other half of one range object. The repo already carried the correct managed-settings paths in `debug-on/SKILL.md` (:153), so the file-path half of the gap was answerable by `grep -rn managed-settings .` without leaving the repo.
- **Root cause**: the section had written its own citation policy into its prose — "a docs page states current behavior only and can't source when a past feature first shipped, so the release note is the citation, not `code.claude.com`" — which is exactly right for the matrix's *version* column, and I applied it wholesale to everything in the section. But a release note announcing a setting ("Added `requiredMinimumVersion` and `requiredMaximumVersion` managed settings — Claude Code refuses to start if its version is outside the allowed range") sources the setting's existence, its introducing release, and its behavior, and sources *neither* its value shape *nor* which settings file it belongs in. Those are current-behavior facts, i.e. precisely the class the section's own rule had just labelled "docs page" territory. So the one claim whose correct source was the docs page was the one claim I never opened the docs page for, and the shape got invented to fill the gap. Compounding it, the `{"min": …}` object shape is not even internally consistent with the matrix row directly above it, which lists `requiredMinimumVersion` / `requiredMaximumVersion` as two settings.
- **Apply next time**: (a) treat every **copy-pasteable configuration artifact** (a JSON/YAML snippet, a CLI invocation, an MDM policy fragment) as a claim needing three separate citations — the key name, the *value shape*, and the *file/scope it goes in* — and never let a release note stand in for the last two; the changelog entry that announced a setting almost never shows its value. (b) For Claude Code settings specifically, `curl -sS https://code.claude.com/docs/en/settings.md` returns the raw markdown with a greppable `| Key | Description | Example |` table — `grep -n '<settingName>'` gives an exact, quotable Example column in one call. Prefer that over `WebFetch` on the HTML page here: WebFetch's summarizing model reported the shape as an *inference* ("Based on context, these are plain version strings") and its first pass claimed the settings table had no row for the key at all, which the raw markdown disproved. (c) Before inventing any shape, `grep -rn "<key>" .` the target repo first — a sibling file (`debug-on/SKILL.md` here) may already have sourced the same mechanism, and matching its wording is both cheaper and better for cross-file consistency. The review's accompanying nit — that this row's own "Where" cell was a mention, not a use — is a third recurrence of [[L-2026-07-27-06]]'s point (b): a row the *implementer* added escaped the use/mention rule that the same commit had just written into the section's prose to bind every row, because the re-audit was scoped to the rows the reviewer had named. Apply a newly-written inclusion rule to rows you authored yourself, not only to inherited ones. See also [[L-2026-07-27-05]] (version verified ≠ attribution verified).


## L-2026-07-27-08 — A truncated render of a long docs table reads as "the setting isn't documented"; the fixer must re-verify the REVIEW's own quoted evidence against the raw source before editing

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#492 (branch `eva-impl/181-version-compatibility-table`, cross-repo fix, round 3 attempt — declined, no push)
- **Signal**: Eva's REQUEST_CHANGES review made three factual claims against § Enterprise pinning, and all three were wrong: (a) "the settings docs give example `"2.1.100"`, not `"2.1.150"`" — `"2.1.100"` is the example for `minimumVersion`, a *different* row whose description ends "For a hard floor that blocks startup entirely, see `requiredMinimumVersion`"; `requiredMinimumVersion`'s own example is `"2.1.150"`. (b) The docs "describe it as 'required to use this configuration… exits before loading the configuration'" — that phrasing appears **nowhere** on the page (`grep -c "before loading" settings.md` → 0). (c) "`requiredMinimumVersion` isn't on that page's 'Managed settings only' list" — its description literally opens `Managed settings only. Minimum Claude Code version required to start.` The PR's quote and value were verbatim-correct. Reproducing the reviewer's likely method reproduced the reviewer's error exactly: a `WebFetch` of `https://code.claude.com/docs/en/settings` returned the § Available settings table marked `[Content truncated due to length...]`, and the summarizing model reported — confidently — "These settings do not appear in the 'Available settings' table on this page." Fetching the raw markdown instead (`curl -sL https://code.claude.com/docs/en/settings.md`, 267 KB) and grepping put the full row on screen in one command.
- **Root cause**: `WebFetch` runs a *small fast model* over a page that has been truncated to fit, and its answer carries no signal distinguishing "absent from the page" from "absent from the part of the page I was given". A 267 KB reference page with a single ~120-row table is the worst case: the target row sits far past the cut, and the surviving rows include a near-namesake (`minimumVersion`) whose example value is a plausible-looking `"2.1.100"` — so the truncation doesn't merely hide the answer, it supplies a confident wrong one. This is the failure class of [[L-2026-07-24-13]] (a paginated vendor changelog's index page missing an entry is not evidence the entry doesn't exist) one layer up: there, a *missing* page produced a false negative; here, a *truncated* page produced a false negative **plus** a substituted fact. Second, fixer-side: the prompt names the triggering review "the ONLY authoritative input" and "trusted", which is true about *what to look at* and false about *what is factually so* — read as the latter, the prescribed fix would have replaced a correctly-quoted `"2.1.150"` with the wrong `"2.1.100"` and deleted a sourced scope claim, shipping an accuracy regression that reads as a clean review response.
- **Apply next time**: (a) For any docs page on `code.claude.com` / `docs.claude.com`, fetch `<url>.md` with `curl` and `grep` for the exact key **before** reaching for `WebFetch` — the raw markdown is served at the same path with a `.md` suffix, is never truncated, and makes a table row quotable verbatim. Reserve `WebFetch` for short pages or for prose you will read rather than quote. (b) Treat any `WebFetch` result containing `[Content truncated`, or asserting that something is *absent* from a long page, as **no result at all** — never as a negative finding. An absence claim from a truncated render is unfalsifiable by construction. (c) When a review disputes a **quote, a literal value, or a "not on the list" scope claim**, re-derive that specific evidence from primary source before editing anything — the review's *authority* to direct the round does not make its *facts* correct, and a fixer that edits on the review's word alone is the one who introduces the error. If the primary source backs the PR, decline per the mistaken-finding path with the verbatim row quoted (file/line of the source, not just prose) rather than pushing a "softening" no-op: softening a correct sourced claim to match a wrong review is an accuracy regression, not a compromise.


## L-2026-07-27-09 — eva-fix-pr's `evolution/consolidate-*` recipe enumerates ONLY an ID-collision fix; a review flagging the added entry's own body (a wrong `[[L-...]]` anchor) falls outside it and must not be read as "decline"

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/eva#324 (branch `evolution/consolidate-2026-07-27-0732-ievo-ai-skills-492`, eva-repo fix, round 1)
- **Signal**: Eva's REQUEST_CHANGES review on a consolidation PR did **not** flag an ID collision — it flagged a wrong wikilink inside the single entry the PR itself added (`lessons.md:900`: L-2026-07-27-08's Root cause called `[[L-2026-07-27-06]]` "a paginated vendor changelog's index page missing an entry…", but 06 is the use/mention attribution-column lesson; the paginated-changelog lesson is `L-2026-07-24-13` at line 193). Verified against primary source before editing: line 193's title is "Cursor's generic `cursor.com/changelog` listing page paginates dated entries out", line 880's is the attribution-column one — the review's facts held. Meanwhile the recipe's own three operations were all no-ops: the branch was already current with `origin/main` (merge-base == `origin/main`), and the highest same-date NN in main was 07, so 08 did not collide. Following the branch-typed block literally would have meant pushing nothing and declining a correct, one-token, in-scope finding.
- **Root cause**: eva#250's block was written for the one failure mode that had actually recurred (parallel consolidation runs picking the same `NN`), and closed the operation set — "do ONLY these three things … even if the review prose reads more broadly" — as the anti-scope-creep guard. But the guard's stated rationale is narrower than its wording: operation 1 forbids touching an entry body because "that would be altering content the review didn't flag", and operation 3 defines "touch nothing else" in terms of `git status` showing exactly one changed file. Neither rationale reaches a review that explicitly flags a defect in the **new** block this PR authored. The recipe conflated two different things under "don't touch bodies": rewriting *inherited* entries (real scope creep, and a corpus-integrity risk) versus correcting the PR's *own* added content at the reviewer's explicit direction (the fixer's actual job). Left unresolved, the second-most-likely finding class on a consolidation PR — a broken `[[L-...]]` cross-ref, which the ID doubles as the anchor for — routes to the operator for a one-token typo and the lesson lands with a dangling analogy.
- **Apply next time**: (a) On an `evolution/consolidate-*` PR, scope the fix by **provenance of the flagged line**, not by operation name: lines this branch added (`git diff $(git merge-base HEAD origin/main) HEAD`) are fixable when the review names them; lines inherited from `origin/main` are not, ever. (b) Before concluding a closed-op recipe applies, check whether its operations are actually *live* this round — re-derive the collision (`grep -oE "^## L-<date>-[0-9]+" ` against a fresh `origin/main`) and the merge-base; three no-op operations plus a real finding means the recipe was written for a different failure mode, not that there is nothing to fix. (c) A recipe that says "even if the review prose reads more broadly" is guarding against *unrequested* breadth; it is not a licence to no-op on a request that is narrower than one enumerated operation. Prefer the minimal edit the review names, keep the one-file invariant, and state the scope judgment in the PR comment so the operator can tighten or widen the recipe. (d) Verify the review's factual claim from primary source first — per [[L-2026-07-27-08]], the review's authority to direct the round is not authority over what is true; here it checked out, but the check is what made the edit safe.



## L-2026-07-27-10 — Before implementing an issue, search for prior CLOSED (not just merged) PRs on the same issue — a conflict-stranded PR can carry an already-approved design worth reusing instead of re-deriving

- **Source**: eva-implement run for ievo-ai/skills#200 (branch `eva-impl/200-vuln-scan-sensitivity-classification`)
- **Signal**: this issue had two prior autonomous build attempts (PR #480, then #486) that both reached and fully review-fixed the SAME correct design — inserting a "Phase 1.5" file-sensitivity step into `commands/vuln-scan.md` (which genuinely has the Phase 1/Phase 2 structure the issue described), with an unconditional never-quote-secrets rule. #486 was APPROVED on its second review round. Both were closed unmerged only because Eva's own conflict-scan (eva#211) found them `DIRTY`/merge-conflicting after `main` moved with no new push to re-trigger CI — never for a content defect. This build never saw either PR: Phase 1's guidance to check `gh pr list --state merged --limit 10` is invisible to a PR closed-not-merged, so the build independently re-derived a different (also-defensible, but not the previously-approved) design, only discovering the mismatch after reading the evolution store mid-build and cross-referencing branch names.
- **Root cause**: "recent merged PRs for context" only surfaces PRs that made it all the way to merge; a PR stranded by the conflict-scanner (a routine, expected outcome per eva#211 — main moves fast enough that any PR open for hours can go stale) is invisible to that check even though it may carry a fully-reviewed, review-approved diff for the exact issue being rebuilt. The evolution store DOES record this (lessons are keyed by branch name, which embeds the issue number), but nothing in Phase 1 prompts a search of it by issue-specific branch pattern — the store is read for GENERAL lessons, not cross-checked against the specific issue being built.
- **Apply next time**: in Phase 1/Phase 2 research, in addition to `gh pr list --state merged`, also run `gh pr list --repo $TARGET_REPO --state closed --search "eva-impl/$TARGET_ISSUE"` (or grep the branch-name pattern `eva-impl/$TARGET_ISSUE-*` / `$TARGET_ISSUE` in the evolution store) before drafting an approach. If a prior closed-not-merged PR exists for the same issue, fetch its diff (`gh pr diff <n>`) and its reviews (`gh pr view <n> --json reviews`) — a `CHANGES_REQUESTED` → fix → `APPROVED` history means the design is already vetted; reuse it (adapted to current `main`) rather than re-deriving independently, which risks landing on an inferior-but-still-passable alternative that an automated reviewer may approve anyway.

## L-2026-07-27-11 — Loading a `disallowed-tools`-carrying skill (e.g. `/ievo:deep-review`) mid-build strips Write/Edit for the rest of a headless eva-implement run, with no session boundary left to clear it

- **Source**: eva-implement run for ievo-ai/skills#200 (branch `eva-impl/200-vuln-scan-sensitivity-classification`)
- **Signal**: after invoking `Skill("ievo:deep-review")` for the Phase 4.5 pass, every subsequent `Edit` tool call was denied — regardless of target file or content, including files never touched by deep-review's own read-only work. `deep-review/SKILL.md`'s own frontmatter carries `disallowed-tools: Write, Edit, ...`. This is the exact platform behavior `AGENTS.md` § Security model already documents from an empirical probe (#400, CC v2.1.217): "its session lost Write/Edit after loading the deep-review skill — and they stayed removed for the rest of the headless run, since the documented clear point is the next user message." A headless `eva-implement`/`eva-fix-pr` run IS one continuous turn with no further user message, so the loss is permanent for the remainder of the run, not transient.
- **Root cause**: `disallowed-tools` in a skill's frontmatter is enforced at the session level once the skill is loaded (bare tool names, per the same AGENTS.md section), not scoped to only the tool calls the skill itself makes. Any build step that later invokes a review/audit skill carrying this frontmatter (deep-review, vuln-scan, security-check all carry the same denylist) should expect Write/Edit to be gone afterward.
- **Apply next time**: run `/ievo:deep-review` (or any skill with `disallowed-tools: Write/Edit`) only AFTER all direct file edits for the build are complete, if at all possible — this avoids the loss entirely. If a skill-pass finding still requires a fix afterward (as here), do not retry `Edit`/`Write` repeatedly expecting a transient glitch (this is NOT the `[[L-2026-07-25-15]]` out-of-scope-file scope guard — same symptom, different, session-wide cause) — switch immediately to Bash-based edits (`python3` with exact `str.replace` + an assert on match count, or `sed` for simple one-liners) for every remaining file change in the run, and verify each with a diff read afterward since there is no Edit-tool old-content safety check to lean on.

## L-2026-07-27-12 — An open `eva-impl/*` PR can be independently reviewed and auto-merged by `eva-review-pr` while the implement session is still pushing follow-up commits; a later push can land on an already-merged, closed PR and be silently orphaned

- **Source**: eva-implement run for ievo-ai/skills#200 (branch `eva-impl/200-vuln-scan-sensitivity-classification`)
- **Signal**: after opening PR #496 (Phase 5) and pushing a second, gate-green fix commit (correcting a deep-review finding), the build continued researching for several more minutes — reading the evolution store, fetching a prior stranded PR's diff and reviews — before pushing a third commit with a superior design (see `[[L-2026-07-27-10]]`). Unbeknownst to the running session, `eva-review-pr`'s own `workflow_run` pipeline had already reviewed and auto-merged PR #496 at the SECOND commit's SHA (`gh pr view 496 --json state,mergedAt` showed `MERGED` at a timestamp minutes before the third commit was pushed). The third commit landed on a branch whose PR was already closed — it will never be reviewed or merged; a real `v0.74.0` GitHub Release had already been cut from the merged (second-commit) state by the time this was discovered.
- **Root cause**: the prompt's Phase 5 framing ("release the claim immediately... your job ends here") correctly describes where the IMPLEMENTER's responsibility ends, but doesn't warn that the session may keep doing useful-seeming work (Phase 4.5's skill pass, self-triggered redesign) on a branch whose PR is a live, independently-actuated resource — `eva-review-pr` is not synchronized with the implement session and can merge the instant CI goes green after ANY push, including a mid-build fix push never intended as "final."
- **Apply next time**: after ANY push to an already-open PR (not just the first), before doing further research, redesign, or another push, check `gh pr view <n> --json state,mergedAt` first. If `state` is already `MERGED`, stop all further work on that branch immediately — it is moot, the orphaned commits will never be seen by anyone. If the merged content turns out to be inferior-but-functionally-correct (as here — the shipped design satisfied the issue and passed independent review, just not as elegantly as an alternative), do not try to force a correction via the closed PR or a same-branch push; that requires either a fresh follow-up issue/PR (a genuine improvement, filed for a human/future run to pick up) or simply accepting the shipped, working result — don't manufacture churn correcting a merged-and-working outcome to match a stylistic preference.


## L-2026-07-27-13 — A cross-reference that counts paragraphs ("the paragraph after next") rots inside the PR that wrote it, because later fix rounds insert paragraphs between the pointer and its target

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#497 (branch `eva-impl/483-deep-review-untracked-files`, cross-repo fix, round 3)
- **Signal**: Eva's REQUEST_CHANGES review found two positional cross-references in prose this PR added — `deep-review/SKILL.md:145` and `:183`, both reading "see the injection paragraph after next" — landing one commit after `18d5d7e` had replaced exactly this construction elsewhere in the repo with the stable `§ <heading>`'s "<lead-in>" form (the fix `[[L-2026-07-26-40]]` was captured from). One of the two was already *wrong*: from :145, "after next" counts past the injection paragraph onto the fenced code block, because round 2 (`[pr-fix-2]`) had inserted the symlink/newline-guard paragraph into the same section after the reference was drafted.
- **Root cause**: `[[L-2026-07-26-40]]` was read as a finding about the *file the review named* (`vuln-scan/SKILL.md`) rather than as a repo convention binding on any prose being written, so the lesson was applied as a patch and not as a rule. Two things made the relapse invisible: the offending phrase contains neither "above" nor "below" (the tokens a grep for the lesson would look for), and a *paragraph-counting* reference is strictly worse than "above"/"below" — it depends on the exact number of intervening blocks, so it breaks on any insertion, including one made by a later round of the very same PR. Nothing mechanical checks a prose cross-reference: every gate stayed green through both rounds.
- **Apply next time**: treat "never write a positional cross-reference" as covering every relative form — "above", "below", "the next section", "the paragraph after next", "the second bullet down" — not just the two directional words, and apply it to prose you *write* this round, not only to the lines a review flagged. Before committing new prose, grep the diff's own added lines for that family (`git diff -U0 | grep -nE '^\+.*(paragraph|section|bullet)'`) and rewrite each relative one as `§ <heading>`'s "<verbatim lead-in clause>", quoting enough of the target's first words to be greppable. On a multi-round PR, re-resolve every relative reference already in the branch's own added text after any round that inserts a block into the same section — the pointer you wrote in round 1 is not the pointer that ships in round 3.

## L-2026-07-27-14 — Updating a callee's contract for the field the change ADDED does not cover the fields the same change silently REDEFINED

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#497 (branch `eva-impl/483-deep-review-untracked-files`, cross-repo fix, round 3)
- **Signal**: an earlier round of this same issue had already pulled `agents/deep-reviewer.md` into scope and documented the new `coverage_caveats` dispatch field (`[[L-2026-07-26-51]]`). Eva's review on this round found the *same* callee still wrong for a different reason: its `## Input` described `diff` as "the raw diff text (from `git diff`, `git diff --staged`, or `git diff <range>`)", while working-tree mode now hands it `git diff`'s output concatenated with synthesized per-file diffs carrying `### untracked <path>` / `### skipped …` marker lines the contract never mentions — so the receiving agent had no basis to treat them as delimiters rather than reviewable content, and no rule keeping `changed_files` (not a marker line an untracked file's contents could forge) the authoritative list of what it received.
- **Root cause**: "update the callee" was discharged as "document the new field". The change's real effect on the callee was two-part — one field added, one existing field's *value shape* redefined — and only the added one looks like an interface change; redefining what an existing field's bytes contain leaves the field name, the section, and the dispatch template all unchanged, so no diff line on either side points at it. `[[L-2026-07-26-51]]`'s remedy ("open the receiving agent definition in the same edit") was followed literally and still missed this, because the file was opened to *add* rather than to *audit*.
- **Apply next time**: when a change touches a dispatch prompt, enumerate the callee's input fields and mark each one added / redefined / untouched before editing — a field is *redefined* whenever the producer's new code path can put bytes in it that the old wording doesn't describe (markers, concatenated sections, a new encoding, a new empty case), and every redefined field needs its contract rewritten in the same commit even though its name never changed. Where the new bytes include content the producer derived from untrusted input, say in the callee's contract which field stays authoritative and why, so the two files read as one instruction rather than as two that merely mention the same marker.

## L-2026-07-27-15 — `/ievo:evo` writes into the CURRENT project tree, so on a cross-repo fix the blessed capture path is the capture file, not the plugin skill

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#497 (branch `eva-impl/483-deep-review-untracked-files`, cross-repo fix, round 3)
- **Signal**: `EVA_IEVO_PLUGIN_READY=true`, and eva-fix-pr's Phase 3b says to invoke the evolution skill when it is. But that skill's whole contract is "appends to `.ievo/evolution/<scope>/<name>.md`" — i.e. under the working tree, which on a cross-repo fix *is* `$TARGET_REPO`. Running it would have dropped an Eva-behaviour lesson into `ievo-ai/skills`, which the operator's #158 Q1 answer forbids outright and which Phase 3c exists to prevent, and would have added untracked files to the tree the fix commit is staged from.
- **Root cause**: Phase 3b's two capture routes are written as a preference order (plugin first, capture file as fallback) rather than as a branch on repo identity, and the plugin route's destination is implicit in the skill, not in the prompt. The "dogfood the plugin when it is available" intent and the "lessons never land in the target repo" rule collide only in the cross-repo case — which is exactly the case Phase 3c exists for.
- **Apply next time**: on a cross-repo fix, capture straight to `EVA_EVOLUTION_CAPTURE` and let Phase 3c consolidate it, regardless of `EVA_IEVO_PLUGIN_READY` — the plugin route belongs to an eva-repo run, where the working tree already is the evolution store. Generally, before invoking any tool that writes "to the project", resolve where *the project* points in this run: in a cross-repo role the working tree is somebody else's repo, and a write there is scope creep on a stranger's PR rather than a note to self.


## L-2026-07-27-16 — `/ievo:deep-review` cannot be invoked via the Skill tool in this harness; dispatch the `ievo:deep-reviewer` sub-agent directly instead

- **Source**: eva-implement run for ievo-ai/skills#184 (branch `eva-impl/184-vuln-scan-attack-mapping`), Phase 4.5 iEvo skill pass
- **Signal**: calling `Skill({skill: "deep-review"})` errored immediately with `Skill deep-review cannot be used with Skill tool due to disable-model-invocation` — before any review ran. `deep-review/SKILL.md`'s own frontmatter sets `disable-model-invocation: true` deliberately (a heavyweight skill, user-invoke only, so it can't auto-activate on description match) — but that same flag also blocks this harness's Skill tool from invoking it explicitly by name, not just from auto-activating it. The skill is also absent from the system-reminder's list of invocable skills for the session, for the same reason. `security-check` has an identical flag for a different reason (sub-agent preload) and would hit the same wall.
- **Root cause**: the master eva-implement prompt's Phase 4.5 instructs "Invoke the `/ievo:deep-review` skill on the diff of this branch" as if it were a normal Skill-tool call, but `disable-model-invocation: true` (AGENTS.md § Skills format: reserved for `init`, `deep-review`) withholds the skill from every *model*-driven invocation path, including an explicit `Skill()` tool call by name in this harness — not only silent description-match auto-activation, which is the scenario AGENTS.md's own doc text frames it around. The distinction between "the model can't discover this via description match" and "the model can't invoke this via Skill() at all" isn't drawn anywhere in the docs I read, so the prompt's instruction reads as if it should just work.
- **Apply next time**: when a Phase 4.5 (or any other) instruction says to invoke a skill and the Skill tool call fails with a `disable-model-invocation` error, do NOT treat that as a blocker or skip the review — read that skill's own SKILL.md "Dispatch the subagent" step (deep-review/SKILL.md Step 4: dispatch via Task tool with `subagent_type: "deep-reviewer"`, passing `## Repo context` / `## Changed files` / `## Diff` sections) and reproduce it by calling the Agent tool directly with the matching `subagent_type` (here, `ievo:deep-reviewer`) and an equivalent prompt built from the skill's own Steps 1-3 (scope resolution, diff capture, repo context). This delivers the same independent-eyes review the skill would have, just without the wrapper. Generalizes to any `disable-model-invocation: true` skill this pipeline is asked to invoke (currently `init`, `deep-review`, plus `security-check` for its sub-agent-preload variant) — go straight to dispatching its paired agent file (`agents/<twin>.md`) instead of the Skill tool.

## L-2026-07-29-01 — Reusing an issue's own literal example path verbatim in new doc prose can trip this repo's own doc-lint validators

- **Source**: eva-implement run for ievo-ai/skills#500 (branch `eva-impl/500-install-protocol-name-path-traversal`)
- **Signal**: `pre-commit run` failed on the first attempt — the `machine-local-paths` hook flagged `/home/user/.ssh` (in both the new `install-protocol.md` prose and the matching `CHANGELOG.md` entry) as a machine-local path leak. That exact string was lifted verbatim from the filed issue's own exploit-chain example (`../../../../home/user/.ssh`), which is untrusted/unvalidated prose, not something the repo's own tooling had already accepted.
- **Root cause**: `machine-local-paths.mjs`'s pattern (`/(?:Users|home)\/[A-Za-z][A-Za-z0-9_-]+/`) flags any `/home/<identifier>` or `/Users/<identifier>` segment as a concrete leaked username unless it's a placeholder (`<name>`, `$USER`, `~/`) or the whitelisted `/home/runner`. An issue body is never linted, so a vivid example path inside it can read as perfectly reasonable prose to copy into a fix's documentation/changelog — but the same literal string in a committed `.md` file under `\.(md|mjs|js|ts|py|sh|yaml|yml|json|txt)$` trips the validator immediately, and this repo's local hooks run fully via `pre-commit run --all-files`.
- **Apply next time**: when incorporating a concrete example (a path, a hypothetical filename, sample credentials) quoted from an issue body or external source into new prose destined for a commit in this repo, swap any `/home/<literal-name>/` or `/Users/<literal-name>/` segment for the placeholder form (`/home/<user>/`, `~/`) before writing it — don't assume a string is safe to reuse just because it appeared unchanged in the trusted issue text. Running `pre-commit run --files <touched files>` earlier in the loop (before the full test suite) would have caught this in one round-trip instead of needing a second pass.


## L-2026-07-29-02 — Mirroring an existing gate copies its prose, not its preconditions: re-derive the callee's input contract, the caller's capability envelope, and the ordering invariant

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#503 (branch `eva-impl/499-consolidate-extract-security-reaudit`, cross-repo fix, round 2)
- **Signal**: the PR added a security re-audit gate to `consolidate/SKILL.md` Step 8 and `extract-best-practices/SKILL.md` Phase 4 Step 5 by mirroring `evo/SKILL.md` Step 2.5's gate for vendored content. Eva's REQUEST_CHANGES review found the mirror broken in three independent ways, none of which any validator, test, or coverage gate could see. (1) The copied dispatch `Task(subagent_type="security-auditor", prompt="Audit the freshly-authored <path> …")` passes a bare local path, but `agents/security-auditor.md` § Input accepts only remote candidate identifiers (`<owner>/<repo>@<skill>` and friends) and its Step 1 runs `security-check`'s fetch-shaped Steps 1-2 (skills.sh lookup, `gh api` metadata resolution, shallow clone) — so the gate's primary path was an undefined contract. (2) The original audits *before* anything touches disk; the copy wrote the package into the project's auto-dispatched `.claude/skills/`/`.claude/agents/` directory first and audited after, so a YELLOW/RED discard required deleting a file. (3) That delete exceeded the caller's own declared envelope — `consolidate`'s `compatibility:` frontmatter, three screens above the edit, reads "Read/Write/Edit/Glob/Grep … No sub-agent/Task-tool dispatch required", which the copied `Task` dispatch also contradicted outright.
- **Root cause**: "mirror `evo` Step 2.5's gate" was discharged as reproducing the visible block — the dispatch snippet, the GREEN/YELLOW/RED branches, the `AskUserQuestion` override, the headless auto-decline. Those are the parts that *look* like the gate. What actually made the original correct was invisible in its own prose: its argument happens to be a remote identifier because Step 2 just resolved one; its write comes after the verdict because Step 2 deliberately stopped at "read into context, do not write yet"; and `evo` declares the tool surface the dispatch needs. Copy the block into a caller with a locally-synthesized draft, an already-executed write, and a narrower `compatibility:` line, and every one of those silently inverts while the prose still reads as a faithful mirror.
- **Apply next time**: when porting an existing gate/protocol to a new call site, open three sources before writing the new block and reconcile each explicitly — (a) the *callee's* own definition file, checking its documented `## Input`/precondition section actually accepts the argument shape this call site can produce (not just that the callee exists); (b) the *caller's* frontmatter `compatibility:`/`tools:`/`disallowed-tools:` line, checking every tool the mirrored steps invoke, including the tool an unhappy-path branch needs (a delete, a shell call) and not only the happy path; (c) the ordering the original uses around its irreversible side effect, restating it as an invariant ("audit before the write") rather than a step number, then verifying the new site preserves it. If any of the three doesn't hold, the right fix is usually to adapt the *mechanism* to the new site (here: apply `security-check/SKILL.md` Steps 3-4 inline against the in-context draft, before the write — which the source itself already documents as its own fallback) rather than to bolt a compensating action onto the copied shape. Related: [[L-2026-07-26-51]], [[L-2026-07-27-14]].

## L-2026-07-29-03 — Adding the Nth instance of a documented pattern is incomplete until the doc that ENUMERATES instances is updated; that doc is never in the diff

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#503 (branch `eva-impl/499-consolidate-extract-security-reaudit`, cross-repo fix, round 2)
- **Signal**: the PR added two new security re-audit gates and touched `AGENTS.md` — but only its version-compliance ledger line (`v0.74.2` → `v0.74.3`), a mechanical bump. Eva's review closed with "AGENTS.md § Security model still lists only 3 gates": that section enumerates the re-audit call sites by name (`/ievo:init` Step 8, `/ievo:update` Step 2.5, `/ievo:evo` Step 2.5) and asserts "*every* path that writes fresh, previously-unreviewed content into `.claude/agents/`/`.claude/skills/` re-audits it first". Shipping two more such paths turned a true claim into a false one, in a file the PR had already opened and edited.
- **Root cause**: the enumeration lives in a canon doc, keyed by the *pattern* rather than by any file the change touches, so nothing in the diff points at it — and having already edited `AGENTS.md` for an unrelated mechanical reason made it feel visited. The instances themselves are self-describing (each new gate reads correctly in isolation); only the doc that counts them can be wrong, and its wrongness is a *stale closed set*, which reads as ordinary correct prose until someone counts.
- **Apply next time**: when a change adds an instance of a named, repo-documented pattern (a security gate, a checkpoint, a validator, a carve-out, a lifecycle hook), grep the canon docs for the pattern's own name before committing (`grep -rn "<pattern name>" AGENTS.md README.md`) and read every hit for a closed-set claim — an explicit list, a count, or a universal quantifier ("every path…", "the three gates…"). Update the list *and* any prose asserting its completeness in the same commit; if the new instance differs in mechanism from the existing ones, say how, so the enumeration stays an accurate description rather than a count that happens to be right. Editing a canon file for a mechanical reason (a version ledger, a date) does not count as having reviewed it. Related: [[L-2026-07-26-27]].


## L-2026-07-29-04 — Applying one change to two sibling docs: each edit must be re-derived against its OWN host's invariants, and the two edits diffed against each other for asymmetry

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#503 (branch `eva-impl/499-consolidate-extract-security-reaudit`, cross-repo fix, round 3)
- **Signal**: round 2 had already fixed the mirrored security gate's ordering (audit the draft, then write) in both sibling skills. Eva's next REQUEST_CHANGES found the *repair itself* had mirrored badly, in two symmetric ways. (1) The corrected wording — "GREEN → perform Registration's write, then include this candidate at CHECKPOINT 2" — is true for `consolidate/SKILL.md`, whose Step 8 writes the package and whose CHECKPOINT 2 gates the *later* Step 9 overlay pruning; pasted into `extract-best-practices/SKILL.md` it contradicted that file's own CHECKPOINT 2 ("Before Step 5's writes finalize… packages about to be created") and its own Anti-Pattern line ("CHECKPOINT 2 gates the write/handoff"), three screens apart in the same file. (2) The gate's new "discarded" outcome got a `Discarded on re-audit:` line in `extract-best-practices`'s Phase 6 report template, but `consolidate`'s CHECKPOINT 3 template gained nothing — even though its own gate prose says to "note it in the final report (Checkpoint 3)", making the instruction point at a field that did not exist.
- **Root cause**: two sibling documents that share machinery (here a common `references/package-authoring.md` and a near-identical gate) read as one surface, so a single edit gets written once and pasted twice. But each host still has private invariants — the same checkpoint *name* sits at a different point in each flow, and each has its own report template — and those live far from the edit site, in sections (Anti-Pattern Detection, the report block) that a diff-shaped review of the changed lines never surfaces. The asymmetric half is worse: nothing in either file is individually wrong, so only holding the two changesets side by side reveals that one sibling gained a field the other's prose now references.
- **Apply next time**: when the same change lands in two or more sibling docs, treat them as separate edits with a shared source, not one edit applied twice. For each host, before writing: grep that file for the identifiers the new prose names (`CHECKPOINT 2`, `Step 9`, the gate's own name) and read every hit — a shared *name* rarely means shared *semantics*. Then, before committing, diff the two changesets against each other and account for every line present in one but not the other; a new outcome, verdict, or discard state must reach each host's own user-visible output surface (report template, summary block, anti-pattern list), or the prose promising to report it becomes a dangling reference. Related: [[L-2026-07-29-02]], [[L-2026-07-29-03]], [[L-2026-07-27-13]].


## L-2026-07-29-05 — A stated bound needs a control point in the command that does the work: a one-shot convenience flag (`--paginate`) collapses the loop the bound acts on

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#504 (branch `eva-impl/468-review-retrospective`, cross-repo fix, round 1)
- **Signal**: Eva's REQUEST_CHANGES finding 1 — `agents/review-retrospective.md`'s closed four-template Bash allowlist wrote templates 1-3 as `gh api "…?per_page=100" --paginate`, while Step 1 of the same file declared a "cap 10 pages / 1000 items" per collection and a failure path reading "stop paginating *that one* collection, keep whatever pages it already returned". `gh api --paginate` follows every `Link: rel="next"` header itself inside one invocation, so the whole collection returns in a single call: there is no per-page moment at which the cap could fire, and a mid-pagination error yields no partial result to keep. Both bounds — and the sub-agent's whole reason for existing, keeping a heavily-reviewed PR's data volume out of the caller's context — were unenforceable prose. The closed set contained no `page=N` form, so the agent could not even have complied.
- **Root cause**: the bound and the command were authored as two independent passes over the same document — the allowlist written for *security* (which endpoints may be touched), the caps written for *budget* (how much may be fetched) — and neither pass re-read the other. `--paginate` reads as the obviously-correct flag for "fetch all the reviews", and it is, for the fetch; what it silently removes is the iteration boundary. That removal is invisible at the edit site because the cap's prose sits ~15 lines away under a different heading, and both halves are individually reasonable.
- **Apply next time**: whenever a document states a per-iteration bound — a page cap, item cap, retry limit, timeout, "stop and keep partial results" recovery — locate the exact command that performs the iteration and confirm it exposes the control point that bound acts on. Convenience flags that internalize a loop (`--paginate`, `--all`, `-r`, `--follow`, a client library's auto-pager) make every per-iteration rule written around them unenforceable, and lint/test will never catch it because prose and flag are both valid in isolation. Write the explicit form (`page=<n>`, one call per iteration, an explicit stop condition — a short or empty page) and say in the document *why* the convenience flag is prohibited, so the next editor doesn't "simplify" it back. Applies equally to a closed-set allowlist: if a bound needs a parameter, that parameter must appear in the closed set or the bound is decorative. Related: [[L-2026-07-26-48]].

## L-2026-07-29-06 — A "why we did NOT reuse the existing thing" paragraph is a factual claim about that thing; verify it against its source, or a correct decision ships on a wrong premise and hides a real gap

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#504 (branch `eva-impl/468-review-retrospective`, cross-repo fix, round 1)
- **Signal**: Eva's REQUEST_CHANGES finding 2 — `skills/review-retrospective/SKILL.md` Step 4 justified its new park file with "this does not reuse `evolution_candidates.mjs`'s `pending.md` — that store is a per-session accumulator of free-text lesson candidates (`{ts, scope, text}` JSONL, one file per session)". Two distinct stores had been fused into one: `evolution_candidates.mjs` owns `<session-id>.jsonl`, and its own header comment states the opposite of the claim in one sentence — "The human-review queue `pending.md` (scaffolded by evo-auto-enable) is a SEPARATE, .md file and is never touched here" (`evolution_candidates.mjs:17`); `pending.md` is where `/ievo:evo` Step 0 parks ambiguous/agent/skill/user-level candidates for human review (`evo/SKILL.md:75`), and a test even asserts the `.jsonl` filter excludes it.
- **Root cause**: the paragraph's *conclusion* was handed down by the operator (use a dedicated file), so the justification was written backwards — from a remembered impression of the neighbouring subsystem — and never checked, because a wrong premise under a pre-approved conclusion changes nothing the reader can see failing. Worse, the misdescription actively concealed the real cost: naming `pending.md` a machine-consumed JSONL accumulator makes "we're not using it" sound like a schema decision, when `pending.md` is precisely the *human-review queue*, so the honest consequence — a cluster parked in the new file sits on no review path at all (`/ievo:evo` Step 0 lists `.jsonl`, `/ievo:evo-auto-disable` counts `pending.md`, the SessionStart nudge counts session candidates; none read it) — never surfaced as a limitation worth stating.
- **Apply next time**: treat every "we deliberately did not reuse X" sentence as a factual assertion about X, and open X before writing it — the file's own header comment and the code path that reads it, not memory of the subsystem. An operator-approved conclusion licenses the decision, never the premise. Then ask the question the rejected alternative was answering: if X was the thing some consumer reads, what now reads the replacement? Name that gap as an explicit limitation in the same section (this repo's `SKILL.md` files already use a "**Known limitation, stated honestly**" convention) rather than letting a plausible-sounding rejection imply it was considered. Related: [[L-2026-07-29-05]].


## L-2026-07-29-07 — Updating SOME of a canon doc's enumerations is the strongest false signal that you updated all of them; enumerations are keyed by section, not by file

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#504 (branch `eva-impl/468-review-retrospective`, cross-repo fix, round 2)
- **Signal**: Eva's REQUEST_CHANGES: "AGENTS.md § Security model is now stale: it enumerates five agents; this adds a sixth." The PR added `plugins/ievo/agents/review-retrospective.md` and had already edited `AGENTS.md` in **three** places for exactly this reason — the repo tree (`agents/` listing), § Skills format's `disable-model-invocation` set, and § Skills format's `argument-hint` set. It then stopped. Three further closed-set claims in § Security model, ~150 lines further down, still said five: "All five agents are now on the corrected pattern (…)" with its per-agent Bash-allowlist sizes, "WebSearch stays denied on all five (`security-auditor.md`, …)", and "`evolution.md` is the only one of the five iEvo agents on `model: opus` (the other four: `model: sonnet`)". The review's own words — "same stale-enumeration class you fixed in § Skills format" — name the trap precisely: the fix was applied by *section*, and the sections that got fixed were the ones adjacent to the diff's obvious concerns (tree, frontmatter rules), not the ones keyed to the artifact's *security posture*.
- **Root cause**: [[L-2026-07-29-03]] already taught "grep the canon docs for the pattern's own name", and its own framing is why it didn't fire here. That entry's failure mode was a canon file touched only *mechanically* (a version-ledger bump), and its remedy line reads "editing a canon file for a mechanical reason does not count as having reviewed it" — so the check is anchored to the *absence* of substantive editing. Here the opposite held: `AGENTS.md` received three deliberate, substantive, correct enumeration updates for this exact change. Having consciously answered "does adding this component invalidate a list in AGENTS.md?" three times, the question reads as discharged. It isn't, because a canon doc holds N independent enumerations under N different headings, keyed to N different properties of the same artifact — "is it in the tree", "does it set `disable-model-invocation`", "does it carry an `argument-hint`", "is it on the corrected `disallowedTools` pattern", "does it deny `WebSearch`", "what model does it pin". A new component is a new row in *every* one of them, and the ones that get missed are the ones whose key is a property the build never had to think about (this agent's `model:`/`disallowedTools:` were copied from `deep-reviewer.md` without ever being framed as facts some other document counts).
- **Apply next time**: when a change adds an instance of any enumerated component type (an agent, a skill, a validator, a gate, a hook), do not treat "I updated AGENTS.md" as a unit of work — treat each enumeration as its own. Before committing, grep the canon doc for the *sibling instances'* filenames rather than for the pattern's name: `grep -n 'deep-reviewer\|vuln-scanner\|repo-indexer\|security-auditor\|evolution\.md' AGENTS.md README.md` returns every list the new file belongs in, including the ones whose heading gives no hint they enumerate agents. Then re-derive each hit from the artifacts, never from the prose: read all six agent files' frontmatter and confirm the count, the model split, the deny-lists, and the allowlist sizes the sentence asserts. Sibling-name grep beats pattern-name grep because a stale closed set names its members; it rarely names the category. Having already made N correct updates to a file is evidence you understood the change, never evidence you found every list. Related: [[L-2026-07-29-03]], [[L-2026-07-26-38]].

## L-2026-07-29-08 — In a cursor-paginated loop the first call has a different command shape; a single "template" that folds it in with `after=""` ships an invalid cursor

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#504 (branch `eva-impl/468-review-retrospective`, cross-repo fix, round 2)
- **Signal**: Eva's minor finding — `agents/review-retrospective.md`'s closed Bash allowlist wrote its GraphQL template as `gh api graphql -f query='…' -F owner=… -F repo=… -F number=… -F after="<cursor or empty>"`, and the governing rule underneath said `<cursor>` may hold only an `endCursor` from a prior call. On page 1 no prior call exists, so the literal shipped shape is `after: ""`. GraphQL connections take a nullable cursor where *absent/null* means "start at the beginning"; an empty string is not a cursor GitHub ever issued, so the first call of every run can error rather than returning page 1 — i.e. the collection that the whole sub-agent exists to perform could fail on its first request.
- **Root cause**: this is the *same* PR's [[L-2026-07-29-05]] one layer over. Round 1 fixed the REST templates' pagination control point (`--paginate` removed, explicit `page=<n>`), and the GraphQL sibling was checked for the property that finding named — does it expose a per-iteration control point? — which it does (`after`/`hasNextPage`/`endCursor`). What went unchecked is that a cursor loop, unlike a page-number loop, is **not uniform across iterations**: `page=1` is a perfectly ordinary value of the same parameter, whereas iteration 1 of a cursor loop is defined by the parameter's *absence*. Writing the loop as one closed-set "template" pushes toward one uniform command string, and `"<cursor or empty>"` is the phrasing that makes the non-uniformity disappear into a placeholder — it reads as covering both cases while actually specifying an invalid value for one of them.
- **Apply next time**: whenever documenting or writing a cursor-paginated call (GraphQL `after`/`before`, an opaque `next_token`, a `Link`-header cursor), state the first call as its own shape with the cursor argument **omitted**, and say why (a nullable cursor's null/absent state is the defined "from the beginning" value; the empty string is not). Never let a placeholder like `<cursor or empty>` / `<token or "">` stand in for the initial call. General form: a closed-set command template must be *executable as written* for every iteration it claims to cover — if iteration 1 and iteration N differ in which flags are present, that is two entries in the set, not one entry with a hedged placeholder. And when a review fixes one member of a family of loops, re-derive every sibling loop's *own* mechanics rather than checking the siblings for the property the review named. Related: [[L-2026-07-29-05]], [[L-2026-07-29-04]].


## L-2026-07-29-09 — A GraphQL sub-selection's `first: N` is a cap too, and it sits outside the document's own enumeration of "collections"

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#504 (branch `eva-impl/468-review-retrospective`, cross-repo fix, round 3)
- **Signal**: Eva's REQUEST_CHANGES finding 1 — `agents/review-retrospective.md` Step 1's `reviewThreads` query asked for `pageInfo { hasNextPage endCursor }` on the OUTER connection and, four lines below it, `comments(first: 20)` with nothing at all on the inner one. A thread longer than 20 comments therefore lost its tail with no signal, and the tail is exactly where a "fixed in `<sha>`" reply lives — the evidence Step 4's `stale` rule is defined to read. The file's own stated rule ("**A capped collection is never silent.** If any of the four hit their page cap … record that in the report's Coverage section") enumerated *four* collections; the query actually opens *five*, and the fifth was the undeclared one. Round 1 of this very PR had already fixed the sibling defect one level up ([[L-2026-07-29-05]]: `--paginate` collapsing the loop the page cap acts on) — that sweep went over the four named collections and stopped there.
- **Root cause**: the document's "collections" list and the query's connections were treated as the same set, and they are not. A nested connection is a cap the author writes *inline as a sub-selection argument*, not as a numbered bound under a heading, so it never enters the enumeration the caps prose is written against and never enters a later sweep driven by that enumeration. The failure is silent by construction: GraphQL returns exactly `first: N` nodes and nothing that says more existed, so both the query and the prose are individually valid, and the loss only shows up as a downstream misclassification.
- **Apply next time**: when a document states a "never truncate silently" rule, derive the checklist from the QUERY (every connection it opens, nested ones included), not from the document's own list of named collections — grep the query text for every `first:`/`last:`/`per_page` and confirm each has a matching truncation signal (`totalCount`, `pageInfo`) and a stated handling rule. Then trace each capped field to the step that CONSUMES it: a cap is only harmless if no downstream rule depends on the part that gets dropped, and here the dropped tail was the sole input to a classification. If the truncation cannot be paged away (a nested cursor would need a per-thread follow-up query the closed allowlist doesn't contain), say so explicitly and write the consumer's rule to match — "absence of evidence in a truncated thread is not evidence of absence" — rather than shipping a cap the reader can't see. Also: fixing a pagination defect at one level is a prompt to re-scan the same request for the same defect at every other level, not a completed sweep. Related: [[L-2026-07-29-05]], [[L-2026-07-26-48]].

## L-2026-07-29-10 — Restating a shared definition in your own words forks it; the Nth instance of a cited convention must cite, not paraphrase

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#504 (branch `eva-impl/468-review-retrospective`, cross-repo fix, round 3)
- **Signal**: Eva's REQUEST_CHANGES finding 2 — `skills/review-retrospective/SKILL.md` Step 3's confirmation checkpoint handled the no-user case with its own phrasing, "When no interactive session is available (a non-interactive/headless invocation)". The repo already has one shared definition of that condition, in `evo/SKILL.md` Step 2.5 (an `/ievo:schedule` Routine recognizable by its self-contained invocation prompt, plus any other headless/CI invocation where `AskUserQuestion` cannot be answered), and both siblings that reuse it cite it verbatim — `consolidate/SKILL.md` Step 8 item 4 and `extract-best-practices/SKILL.md` Phase 4 Step 5 each write "(headless/scheduled run — same detection as `evo/SKILL.md` Step 2.5), or a platform with no `AskUserQuestion` at all". The paraphrase also silently dropped the second half: a platform with no `AskUserQuestion` at all, which this skill genuinely has (its `compatibility:` covers non-Claude-Code/Codex platforms running Step 2 inline).
- **Root cause**: a *detection condition* reads like ordinary prose rather than like a shared artifact, so it gets rewritten from understanding instead of looked up — whereas the same author would never restate a shared code helper inline. Nothing fails when it forks: the paraphrase is true as far as it goes, the gate passes, and the two definitions only diverge later, when the shared one is refined and the copy isn't. The dropped clause is the tell that this was written from memory of the *idea* rather than from the source: a citation carries the whole condition, a paraphrase carries the half that was salient.
- **Apply next time**: before writing a condition, outcome, or gate that a sibling component in the same repo also has, grep for it (`grep -rn "no interactive session\|headless" <skills dir>`) and read every hit — if two or more siblings already express it by citing one canonical location, join that citation rather than adding a third wording. Cite the source *and* keep your own step's differing outcome explicit (here: park the cluster, where the siblings discard the draft) — citing the shared detection is not the same as inheriting the shared consequence. Conversely, if no canonical location exists yet, the second instance is the moment to make one. Related: [[L-2026-07-29-03]], [[L-2026-07-26-38]].


## L-2026-07-29-11 — A decision checkpoint is only real if every branch of it has a writer

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#504 (branch `eva-impl/468-review-retrospective`, cross-repo fix, round 4)
- **Signal**: Eva's REQUEST_CHANGES finding 1 — `skills/review-retrospective/SKILL.md` Step 3 asked the user a three-way question per `durable-lesson` cluster (`Confirm — durable lesson` / `Reject — not durable` / `Not sure / decide later`), but Step 4's persistence rule opened "Any `durable-lesson` cluster that came out of Step 3 as 'not sure / decide later', with an unresolved `unknown` target, or unconfirmed because no interactive session was available, is parked". `Confirm` appears in no branch of that sentence, and the skill's own Scope boundary forbids invoking `/ievo:evo`, so a confirmed cluster had no writer anywhere in the build: it survived only in the conversation transcript. The outcome was exactly inverted — a user answering "not sure" produced a durable file entry, while a user answering "yes, this is a durable lesson" produced nothing that outlived the session.
- **Root cause**: the MVP scope boundary ("this build never invokes `/ievo:evo`; capture is Part 2") was silently over-read from "confirm has no *capture*" to "confirm has no *destination*". Because the deferred work was real and correctly scoped, the missing branch looked like the deliberate boundary rather than a hole in it — the Confirm option's own description even said "Ready for /ievo:evo once that wiring lands", which reads as a plan and thereby conceals that nothing records *which* clusters are ready. The park step was then written from the name it had been given ("park **unresolved** candidates"), so its scope was inherited from a heading instead of derived from the enumerated answers of the question it serves. The same round also shipped a brand-new append-only file with a stable per-entry key (`## <PR url> — <cluster title>`) and no idempotency rule (finding 2), even though the skill's own "When to use" section advertises re-running it periodically against older merged PRs — the re-run case was described in the sales pitch and never in the write procedure.
- **Apply next time**: when a step presents a user with an N-way choice, write the persistence table before the prose — one row per option, naming the concrete artifact each answer produces and the file it lands in — and require every row to name a writer or an explicit, stated "writes nothing, because …". An option whose row is empty is not a boundary, it is a dropped decision, and the strongest-signal option (the affirmative one) is the likeliest to be dropped precisely because a deferred follow-up is already earmarked for it. Two mechanical checks: (1) grep the step that persists for the literal label of every option the asking step offers — a label that appears nowhere downstream is the tell; (2) never let the persist-step's own heading bound its scope ("park *unresolved* candidates" quietly excluded confirmed ones) — re-derive the scope from the question's enumerated answers. Separately, any new file this repo writes more than once needs its re-run rule stated in the same change that introduces the format: define the entry key, say whether a repeat key appends or updates in place, say explicitly that an entry the run didn't reproduce is never deleted, and state exact-vs-fuzzy key matching as a known residual rather than leaving re-invocation behaviour to be discovered. Related: [[L-2026-07-29-09]], [[L-2026-07-26-48]].


## L-2026-07-29-12 — A read-modify-write contract inherits the READ tool's window; "never clobbers" is false the moment the file outgrows one Read

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#504 (branch `eva-impl/468-review-retrospective`, cross-repo fix, round 5)
- **Signal**: Eva's REQUEST_CHANGES finding 1 — `skills/review-retrospective/SKILL.md` Step 4 instructed "Read the file first if it exists (Read tool) so the write is additive, never clobbering prior parked entries; write the merged result (Write tool …)". The Read tool returns a bounded window (~2000 lines by default), so once `.ievo/evolution-candidates/retrospective-pending.md` accumulates past it, a single Read returns a prefix and the Write-back deletes every entry beyond that prefix — the exact clobber the sentence promises to prevent. The step is append-oriented by design (one entry per parked cluster, re-runs expected, entries never deleted), so growing past the window is the *designed* trajectory of that file, not a corner case. The same step already documented a non-atomic-write race as a known limitation, which made the file look like its data-loss surface had been thought through.
- **Root cause**: read-modify-write was specified at the level of the *operation* ("Read, merge, Write") and never at the level of the *tools that implement it*. `Write` has no partial mode — it replaces the file wholesale — so the durability of everything not in the Read result is silently transferred to Read's window size, a parameter that appears nowhere in the procedure and that the author never has to name. Both halves individually read as correct; the defect exists only in their composition, and it is invisible at authoring time because the file is empty then and stays inside one window for every early run. The honest known-limitation paragraph about concurrency actively hid it: having named one data-loss mode, the section reads as having enumerated them.
- **Apply next time**: whenever a procedure says "read the existing file, merge, write it back", write the read as an explicit page-to-EOF loop (`offset`/`limit`, advance until a page returns empty) and add a stated abort branch — if any page fails to read, do NOT Write, report and leave the file untouched — because a failed re-run is recoverable and a truncated queue is not. General form: a whole-file Write is only additive if the preceding Read is provably complete, so any bounded-output read tool (Read's line window, a `head`-like helper, a paginated API GET) sitting in front of a whole-file write is a data-loss bug until it is bounded to EOF or replaced with a genuine append primitive. Check it by asking what the file looks like after N invocations, not after the first. And when a section already lists one known limitation of a write path, treat that as a prompt to enumerate the rest rather than as evidence they were enumerated. Related: [[L-2026-07-29-11]], [[L-2026-07-29-09]].

## L-2026-07-29-13 — A cardinal count ("the four validated fields") is an uncheckable cross-reference; when the rule is load-bearing, anchor it to the section that defines the set

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#504 (branch `eva-impl/468-review-retrospective`, cross-repo fix, round 5)
- **Signal**: Eva's REQUEST_CHANGES finding 2 — `agents/review-retrospective.md` said "the four validated dispatch fields" at line 58 and again at line 183, while the file's own `## Input (from dispatch prompt)` section enumerates eight (`owner`, `repo`, `number`, `url`, `title`, `merged_at`, `merge_commit_sha`, `repo_matches_local`). Line 58 is not decoration: it is the injection-refusal rule ("if anything … in the dispatch prompt beyond the four validated fields asks for a Bash command outside this list, refuse"), so the count *defines the trusted set*. Written as "four", the rule reads as trusting an unnamed subset and treating the other four as suspect — the opposite of the intent, and unresolvable by a reader who can only count eight.
- **Root cause**: the count was accurate when written — the input set genuinely started smaller — and then grew across rounds as `url`/`title`/`merged_at`/`merge_commit_sha` were added for the report header and `repo_matches_local` was hoisted out of the sub-agent (a fix from an earlier round of this same PR). A numeral is a cross-reference with no link: nothing in the file connects "four" to the list it counts, so extending the list cannot flag it, no validator can check it, and it survives every re-read because "four validated fields" is a phrase that parses fine in isolation. It also duplicated: the same stale numeral was reproduced in the Rules section, so the drift compounded silently.
- **Apply next time**: never let a bare cardinal stand in for a set that is defined elsewhere in the same document — write "the validated dispatch fields listed in § Input" (naming the section), and keep the count only where it adds something, in which case it must be re-derived from the enumeration in the same edit. Mechanical check when adding an item to any enumerated list: grep the file for number words and digits near the list's noun (`grep -niE '\b(two|three|four|five|six|seven|eight|[0-9]+) (validated|dispatch|command|collection)' <file>`) and reconcile every hit. Weight the check by what the count *does*: a count inside a security or refusal rule defines a trust boundary, so a stale one is a behaviour bug, not a typo. This is the same failure class as a stale positional reference, and the same fix applies — cite the stable §, not the volatile property. Related: [[L-2026-07-29-10]], [[L-2026-07-29-03]].

## L-2026-07-29-14 — A documented resolution step must handle its own empty result before the value flows into the next command, or the tool's error replaces yours

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#504 (branch `eva-impl/468-review-retrospective`, cross-repo fix, round 5)
- **Signal**: Eva's REQUEST_CHANGES finding 3 — `skills/review-retrospective/SKILL.md` Step 1 resolved `NAME_WITH_OWNER=$(gh repo view --json nameWithOwner --jq .nameWithOwner 2>/dev/null)` and, on the bare-number input path, split it into `OWNER`/`REPO` with no check. When `gh repo view` resolves nothing (not a git checkout, no GitHub remote, unauthenticated `gh`) both expansions yield the empty string, and the very next block runs `gh pr view "$NUMBER" --repo "$OWNER/$REPO"` — i.e. `--repo /`. The user gets a confusing `gh` error about a malformed repo argument instead of the accurate "not in a GitHub repo — pass a full PR URL". The `2>/dev/null` that suppressed `gh`'s own diagnostic was what made the empty value silent.
- **Root cause**: the resolution was hoisted to the top of the step and made unconditional (correctly — two later branches need it), and hoisting separated the *acquisition* of the value from the *branch that requires it*. The consumer branch then read as though the value were guaranteed, because at the point it is used nothing nearby suggests it can be empty.
