- **Apply next time**: when porting a containment/escaping/redaction rule into a NEW rendering template, derive the value list from the destination template itself, never from the source rule or from the destination's most prominent structures: walk the template literally placeholder by placeholder (`<...>` and every backticked slot), and for EACH one write down provenance (which fetch step produced it) and a verdict — contained, or exempt-with-a-stated-reason. Emit both lists in the rule and mark the enumeration "exhaustive and closed", so a later reader can audit it against the template rather than re-deriving it. Two traps this catches: (a) a placeholder the template already shows inside single backticks reads as "already handled" but is illustrative, not a fence sized to the value; (b) a validation that exists upstream may not cover the render — `inspect`'s `^[A-Za-z0-9._/-]+$` path allowlist gates *fetching* only, and scripts are fetched last, so a path dropped by the 30-fetch cap still renders never-validated. Also state per-surface which normalizations apply (line-break collapsing on every surface — a break ends a list item or blockquote too, not just a table row — while pipe-escaping is table-only), since a mechanism ported from a non-table surface carries no such distinction. Related: [[L-2026-07-30-02]] (a prior port dropped the source rule's companion counterweight) — same family, opposite direction: that one lost content from the source, this one failed to add coverage the destination needed.



## L-2026-08-01-11 — Appending a short qualifier to a compatibility field near its cap still needs a post-edit re-measure, not just "it looks shorter than before"
- **Source**: eva-implement run for ievo-ai/skills#461 (branch `eva-impl/461-codex-desktop-detection`, cross-repo build)
- **Signal**: first `validate_skills.mjs` run failed on two files — `evo-auto-enable/SKILL.md`'s `compatibility` field at 542 chars and `init/SKILL.md`'s at 523 chars, both over the agentskills.io 500-char cap — after I appended a short Codex-Desktop qualifier (e.g. "(CLI or Desktop — `/ievo:init` Step 1.5's detection rule)") to an existing `Codex (`$CODEX_CLI`)` phrase in each. A first trim attempt (shortening the parenthetical) still left both fields over cap (508/503) because I judged the edit "clearly shorter" by eye instead of running `awk -F'"' '{print length($2)}'` against the edited line before re-running the gate.
- **Root cause**: this exact failure mode is already captured verbatim as `[[L-2026-07-25-01]]` in this store ("A length-capped field's pre-edit headroom is not permission to append; re-measure the edited value before the gate") — I read the store at Phase 1 but did not apply it proactively while writing the edits; I only re-measured reactively, after the gate failed, and even then trimmed once by eyeball before actually running the length check.
- **Apply next time**: when editing ANY `compatibility`/`description`-style capped SKILL.md field, compute `awk -F'"' '{print length($2)}'` (or equivalent) on the edited line immediately after writing it, BEFORE running the validator — not after a failure, and not by eyeballing whether a trim "looks shorter". A lesson already in the store is only useful if applied at write-time, not re-discovered at gate-failure-time.


## L-2026-08-01-12 — Porting a mechanism to a second platform: verify the CARRIER is loaded there before tuning the matcher inside it; a leaf detail confirmed against docs proves nothing about the enclosing layer

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#528 (branch `eva-impl/461-codex-desktop-detection`, cross-repo fix, round 1)
- **Signal**: Eva's REQUEST_CHANGES — "Hook half looks inert on Codex: your own `codex-hooks.md` § 'Config file location and scopes' lists only `.codex/hooks.json`/`config.toml`/plugin-bundled — nothing shows Codex reads SKILL.md/agent frontmatter `hooks:`, and `evolution.md:56` says plugin-shipped agents ignore `hooks:`." I had diagnosed #461's hook half as a *matcher* problem (Claude Code's `Write` tool name is unreachable on Codex's `apply_patch` surface) and shipped a second `matcher: "apply_patch"` entry inside `evo/SKILL.md`'s and `agents/evolution.md`'s YAML frontmatter `hooks:` block. Re-checked against the live docs this round: Codex loads hook config **only** from `<repo>/.codex/hooks.json`, `<repo>/.codex/config.toml`'s `[hooks]`, the `~/.codex/` equivalents, and a Codex plugin's bundled `hooks/hooks.json`; its own SKILL.md frontmatter is documented as `name`/`description` only. Frontmatter `hooks:` is a Claude Code feature. So the added entry could never fire on either platform — dead config, shipped with a CHANGELOG bullet announcing it as the fix.
- **Root cause**: I spent the verification budget on the innermost detail and none on the enclosing one. I *did* go to the Codex hooks reference — to confirm the `apply_patch`/`Edit`/`Write` matcher aliases and that the matcher filters on tool name only, and I wrote both findings up in `references/codex-hooks.md` in the same PR. But every one of those questions presupposes that Codex reads the block the matcher sits in, and I never asked that question, because the block was already there and already "worked" (on Claude Code). An existing, functioning construct reads as infrastructure rather than as a claim needing proof, so porting it felt like editing a value inside a known-good container instead of asserting the container exists on the new platform. The refuting evidence was not merely available, it was in a file I edited in the same diff, two sections above my own addition — I extended `codex-hooks.md` without reading its § "Config file location and scopes" against what I was building. Structurally identical to [[L-2026-07-31-13]] (guard the parsed ROOT before its members): the missed link is the enclosing scope, so no amount of care about the contents can surface it.
- **Apply next time**: when porting any mechanism to a second platform (hook, config block, frontmatter field, env contract, plugin manifest key), verify the chain **outermost-first** and write the chain down before editing: does platform B load this *file*? does it read this *section* of the file? does it accept this *field*? — and only then, does it accept this *value/matcher*? A confirmed answer at the leaf is worthless if any link above it is unconfirmed, and the leaf is the seductive one to research because it is the part that looks technical. Two concrete checks: (1) when the answer to "does platform B read this carrier?" is not in the platform's own config-discovery docs, treat the discovery list as **exhaustive by default** — an omission there is a negative answer, not silence, so say so in the doc explicitly (the fix here adds "This list is exhaustive" to that very table so the next reader can't repeat the inference); (2) if the PR touches a reference doc that describes the target platform, re-read that doc's *neighbouring* sections against your own change before appending to it — an in-repo document contradicting your own diff is the cheapest possible review, and here it was one scroll away. When the honest answer is "no config in this carrier can work on platform B", the deliverable is documentation of the real path (a ready-to-paste config in the layer that IS loaded) plus a CHANGELOG that says what actually shipped — never inert config plus a claim it closes the gap. Related: [[L-2026-07-31-13]], [[L-2026-07-18-02]].

## L-2026-08-01-13 — A security-coverage variant consciously scoped OUT during design was the exact gap the independent review pass then flagged as a high-confidence leak
- **Source**: eva-implement run for ievo-ai/skills#530 (branch `eva-impl/530-scrub-pem-url-credentials`)
- **Signal**: while designing the PEM private-key redaction pass I explicitly considered the four-dash SSH.com/Tectia armor dialect (`---- BEGIN SSH2 ENCRYPTED PRIVATE KEY ----`, PuTTYgen's ssh.com export) and rejected it as "beyond the issue's scope" — the issue's examples were all five-dash RFC 7468 armor. The Phase 4.5 `/ievo:vuln-scan` pass on my own diff then reported exactly that dialect as its one high-confidence CWE-200 finding: a complete real-tool-generated private key that passed every stage of the new code unredacted. The fix was ~10 lines (a sibling regex reusing the existing structure) plus three tests.
- **Root cause**: treating "the issue's examples don't include this variant" as a scoping boundary for a FAIL-CLOSED security control. For a redaction/validation gate, coverage variants of the same secret class are not scope creep — the issue's class ("PEM private-key blocks") is the scope, and its example list is just a sample, the same way [[L-2026-07-18-01]] established that an issue's sample is not the grammar. The design-time rejection felt like scope discipline but was actually re-opening the bug class the issue existed to close.
- **Apply next time**: when implementing a fail-closed matcher (redaction pass, validator, allow/deny gate) and a KNOWN same-class format variant surfaces during design, default to covering it in the same PR when the marginal cost is small (sibling regex + tests), and record the variant in the PR body either way. Reserve "out of scope" for genuinely different secret/threat classes, not dialects of the one being closed. Design-time variant lists that get scoped out are exactly what the Phase 4.5 independent pass should be told about explicitly (list them in the review prompt) so the reviewer confirms or refutes the scoping call instead of rediscovering it.


## L-2026-08-01-14 — Editing bullets inside the newest CHANGELOG entry with a block-spanning replacement silently deletes every older entry the block reached over

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#528 (branch `eva-impl/461-codex-desktop-detection`, cross-repo fix, round 3)
- **Signal**: Eva's REQUEST_CHANGES counted 160 `## v` headings on the head vs 164 on `origin/main` — `CHANGELOG.md` had lost five *shipped* releases (v0.76.4, v0.76.3, v0.76.1, v0.76.0, v0.75.11), leaving the file jumping v0.76.5 → v0.75.10 and the new entry's own "0.76.4 → 0.76.5" version bullet citing history that no longer existed. `git show <sha> -- CHANGELOG.md` localized the loss to the *previous fix round's* own commit (`[pr-fix-2]`, 3 insertions / 62 deletions), not to the original implement commit (which was a clean 11-line insertion) — the fix round whose entire assignment was three bullets inside the newest entry.
- **Root cause**: round 2 needed to reword three bullets (`**Detection fix**`, `**Hook coverage…**`, `**Scope**`) inside the v0.76.5 section. Instead of three targeted per-bullet edits, it replaced one large span that began at the first changed bullet and ended below the section, so every line the span reached over — five complete older entries and their `---` separators — was replaced by the rewritten bullets alone. Nothing catches this: the deleted lines are pure prose, so all nine pre-commit hooks, the coverage gate, and the version-bump gate stayed green, and the commit's *own* diff stat (`3 insertions, 62 deletions`) is the only place the loss was visible. The existing guard for the neighbouring failure mode ([[L-2026-07-25-23]] — renaming the top heading in place) prescribes exactly the check that would have caught this too (`grep -n '^## v' CHANGELOG.md | head -5`), but it is written as a rule about *adding* an entry, so a round that was only *editing* an existing entry never saw itself as in scope.
- **Apply next time**: treat `CHANGELOG.md` (any append-only, reverse-chronological ledger) as edit-by-anchor only — one replacement per changed bullet/line, never a span that crosses a `## ` heading or a `---` separator. Extend [[L-2026-07-25-23]]'s check to *every* touch of the file, not just entry-adding ones: after editing, run `git diff --numstat -- CHANGELOG.md` and require deletions ≈ the number of lines you deliberately rewrote, then compare the whole tail against base (`diff <(git show origin/<base>:CHANGELOG.md | sed -n '/^## <previous-top-version>/,$p') <(sed -n '/^## <previous-top-version>/,$p' CHANGELOG.md)`) and require it empty — everything from the previous top entry down must be byte-identical to base. To repair, rebuild rather than hand-patch: base file + the new entry spliced in above the old top entry, then re-verify both halves against their sources.


## L-2026-08-01-15 — When a rule cites a spec clause that is conditional, the instruction must be derived FROM that condition: an action narrower than its own quoted precondition is self-refuting, and sweeping it to N siblings ships the contradiction N times

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#535 (branch `eva-impl/531-inspect-excerpt-containment`, round 4) — Eva REQUEST_CHANGES on Eva's own PR.
- **Signal**: round 3 closed the leading/trailing-backtick fence-merge case by writing, in one sentence: "add a single literal space between the fence and the excerpt **on whichever side(s) touch a backtick**; CommonMark strips exactly one leading/trailing space from a span's content **when BOTH ends have one**, so the displayed excerpt is unpadded." The cited clause is correct (CommonMark § Code spans: "If the resulting string both begins and ends with a space character... a single space character is removed from the front and back"), and it refutes the instruction sitting beside it — under a one-sided pad the strip never fires, so the value renders with a stray leading or trailing space. The rule was structurally sound (the span does form, so the CWE-79 injection is still contained) but cosmetically wrong on every value that begins-xor-ends with a backtick, and agents execute this prose literally. Round 3 had swept the wording verbatim into all seven excerpt-containment sites, so one flawed sentence shipped seven times; `inspect/SKILL.md` additionally disagreed with *itself* — its bullet heading said "on each side", its body said "whichever side(s) touch", its "Apply in this order" line said "each side that touches".
- **Root cause**: the spec clause was looked up and quoted accurately, then the *action* was written from the intuition that had motivated the fix ("the problem is the side that touches the fence, so pad that side") rather than re-derived from the clause's precondition. Quoting the clause in the same sentence made it feel verified — the citation was doing rhetorical work, not logical work, and the conjunction "so" papered over a step that does not follow. The sweep to six siblings then amplified it: a verbatim copy propagates a defect at exactly the rate it propagates the fix, and reviewing the sweep for *consistency* ("do all seven now say the same thing?") is not the same as reviewing it for *correctness*, so the sweep's own success criterion actively hid the flaw. The three-way internal disagreement inside `inspect/SKILL.md` was the visible tell that the sentence had been re-typed from intent at each site instead of derived once.
- **Apply next time**: when a normative rule quotes a spec clause containing a condition ("only when", "if and only if", "when BOTH"), read the condition as the rule's *specification* and write the instruction as whatever satisfies it — then re-read instruction and citation as a standalone pair and ask "does the quoted clause actually fire under the action I just told the agent to take?" A citation adjacent to an action is not evidence the action follows from it. For the CommonMark code-span pad specifically: pad **both** sides whenever the value begins **or** ends with a backtick — one-sided padding is never stripped. And before sweeping a normative sentence to N sibling files, get it right at one site and then propagate the *identical* text mechanically; if the same rule is restated in more than one place within a single file (heading, body, procedure summary), make all restatements agree, because an internal disagreement is the cheapest available signal that the rule was re-derived rather than copied. Related: [[L-2026-08-01-09]], [[L-2026-08-01-10]] (same PR, same containment rule, earlier rounds).

## L-2026-08-01-16 — Appending a bullet to an enumerated list leaves the lead-in's count AND its spec attribution stale; the new item is often the one that doesn't belong to the family the lead-in names

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#535 (branch `eva-impl/531-inspect-excerpt-containment`, round 4)
- **Signal**: `inspect/SKILL.md`'s normalization section opened "Wrapping alone is not enough on this template's surfaces: **two GFM mechanics** cut a code span open from the outside" and then listed **three** bullets — round 3 had appended the backtick-padding rule without touching the lead-in. Both halves of the lead-in were wrong: the count, and the attribution — the added mechanic is CommonMark's code-span fence rule, not a GFM extension (only the pipe-escape genuinely is; GFM § Tables). An agent reading normatively gets a miscount of the mandatory steps and a wrong spec to consult.
- **Root cause**: the round-3 edit was scoped as "add a bullet", and a list's lead-in sentence is not part of the list — it sits above the diff hunk's semantic boundary even when it is inside the hunk's context lines, so nothing in the edit or in reading the resulting diff drew attention to it. The attribution error had a second cause: the earlier bullets were both GFM-flavoured, so "GFM mechanics" had been an accurate collective label right up until the bullet that broke the family was appended — the lead-in was not stale-by-neglect so much as invalidated by the very edit that ignored it.
- **Apply next time**: when adding or removing an item in an enumerated list, re-read the sentence introducing the list and check every claim it makes about the set as a whole — the cardinal ("two", "both", "either"), the collective attribution ("GFM mechanics", "these APIs", "all three validators"), and any ordering or exhaustiveness claim ("in this order", "the complete list"). A count is the obvious one and the attribution is the one that bites, because a collective label stays true for every same-family addition and silently goes false on the first cross-family one. Cheap check: `grep` the file for the numeral and for the family name after the edit. Same-round corollary from this PR: any *procedure summary* restating the list ("Apply in this order: ...") is a third site that must be updated with it.


## L-2026-08-01-17 — Mirroring a fix into N ported sibling copies syncs the defect being fixed, but leaves the siblings' OTHER inherited claims unverified

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#535 (branch `eva-impl/531-inspect-excerpt-containment`, round 4)
- **Signal**: Eva's own REQUEST_CHANGES review found that five sibling files (`deep-reviewer.md`, `review-retrospective.md`, `security-auditor.md`, `vuln-scanner.md`, `vuln-scan/SKILL.md`) still asserted "A multi-line excerpt is still safe to wrap this way — CommonMark collapses embedded newlines in a code span to spaces". That is true only for a *single* newline: a code span is an inline construct, so a **blank** line ends the enclosing paragraph before inline parsing runs, no span forms at all, and everything after the break renders as live Markdown — the exact CWE-79 the canonical rule being written in the same PR (`inspect/SKILL.md`'s "a code span cannot contain a blank line" bullet) closes. Three earlier fix rounds had already touched those same five paragraphs (round 3 propagated the leading/trailing-backtick padding gap into every one of them) without ever noticing the pre-existing false reassurance sitting two sentences away.
- **Root cause**: when a security rule exists as one canonical version plus N hand-ported copies, each round's diff was scoped to *the specific defect being propagated* — locate the shared sentence, patch it identically in each sibling, move on. The copies were treated as a single unit to sync on that one axis, so no round ever re-read the siblings' full paragraph against the canonical version or re-derived their remaining spec claims. A sentence that exists ONLY in the ported copies (never in the canonical source) is invisible to that workflow by construction: it isn't part of what's being synced, and it isn't part of the canonical text being strengthened.
- **Apply next time**: when a fix strengthens a canonical rule and mirrors it into ported siblings, do a full paragraph-level diff of each sibling against the canonical version — not just a `grep` for the sentence being changed — and treat any sentence present in a sibling but ABSENT from the canonical version as a prime suspect, re-deriving its claim against the actual spec. Reassurance sentences ("X is still safe", "this is only cosmetic", "no further handling needed") in a security rule deserve the same exploit-payload verification as the rule's positive instructions — see [[L-2026-07-18-02]]: a claim that *narrows* a guard's required handling is exactly as security-relevant as the guard itself, and is likelier to have been written from intuition than from the spec.


## L-2026-08-01-18 — Repointing a stale reference preserves its framing clause; when the same PR removed the condition entirely, the correct edit deletes the premise rather than re-aiming it at the new rule

- **Source**: eva-fix-pr run for ievo-ai/skills#528 (branch `eva-impl/461-codex-desktop-detection`, round 5)
- **Signal**: `init/SKILL.md:873` described the skill's own `Stop` hook as firing "(platform-conditional on Step 1.5's detection rule, zero setup required)". The same PR had already established the opposite: the `hooks:` frontmatter tier is a Claude Code layer Codex never loads, so the block "carries NO Codex branch" (its own frontmatter comment, `init/SKILL.md:12-19`) and its `Stop` handler takes no `if:`/`matcher` at all (`hooks-setup/SKILL.md:54`). The prose pointed a reader at a detection rule that provably cannot gate this hook.
- **Root cause**: the sentence originally read "platform-conditional on `$CODEX_CLI`", and the PR's central mechanical sweep was `$CODEX_CLI` → "Step 1.5's detection rule" across every branch point. This occurrence matched the search pattern, so it got swept — but it was the one site where the *finding of the same PR* was that there is no branch here at all. A find-and-replace treats the reference as the unit of work and silently ratifies the clause around it ("platform-conditional on X" → "platform-conditional on Y"), so the surviving premise never gets re-derived. It read as consistent in review precisely because the new referent was the correct rule *everywhere else in the diff*.
- **Apply next time**: when a PR both (a) redefines a condition and (b) sweeps references to the old condition, treat the two as separate passes, not one. For every hit of the sweep, first ask "does a condition still exist at this site?" before asking "what is the new condition's name?" — if the PR's own conclusion is that the site is unconditional (single-platform, always-on, no `if:`), the fix is to delete the conditional framing and state the flat scope, not to re-aim it. Cheap check after any such sweep: `grep -n "conditional\|depends on\|gated on\|only when"` in the touched files and re-read each hit against what the PR concluded, since the sweep's own pattern (`$CODEX_CLI` here) will not match the framing words that carry the stale claim.
